stationary scattering theory, the N N -body long-range case Thanks: Supported by DFF grant nr. 8021-00084B
E. Skibsted

Abstract
. Within the class of Dereziński-Enss pair-potentials which includes Coulomb potentials and for which asymptotic completeness is known [ De ] , we show that all entries of the N N -body quantum scattering matrix have a well-defined meaning at any given non-threshold energy. As a function of the energy parameter the scattering matrix is weakly continuous. This result generalizes a similar one obtained previously by Yafaev for systems of particles interacting by short-range potentials [ Ya1 ] . As for Yafaev’s paper we do not make any assumption on the decay of channel eigenstates. The main part of the proof consists in establishing a number of Kato-smoothness bounds needed for justifying a new formula for the scattering matrix. Similarly we construct and show strong continuity of channel wave matrices for all non-threshold energies. Away from a set of measure zero we show that the scattering and channel wave matrices constitute a well-defined ‘scattering theory’, in particular at such energies the scattering matrix is unitary, strongly continuous and characterized by asymptotics of minimum generalized eigenfunctions.



Keywords:
N
-body Schrödinger operators, stationary scattering
theory, scattering and wave matrices, minimum
generalized eigenfunctions.

Mathematics Subject Classification 2010: 81Q10, 35A01, 35P05.

1.
Introduction

Asymptotic completeness of systems of quantum particles interacting
by long-range potentials (more precisely by pair-potentials
decaying like
{\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{-\mu})
with
\mu>\sqrt{3}-1
) was proven by Dereziński
[
De
]
by
entirely time-dependent methods. While asymptotic completeness of systems of quantum particles interacting
by short-range potentials can be proven by a time-independent
method, see
[
Ya3
]
, there does seem to appear any time-independent method
for the long-range case in the literature. A notable virtue of
the mentioned papers is their generality, thus the completeness results hold
without implicit assumptions.

One deficiency of this situation is that we lack
understanding of the
scattering matrix in a general long-range setup. This is in contrast to Yafaev’s case where his method
reveals some properties of the (short-range)
scattering matrix. In particular Yafaev showed that this quantity is
weakly continuous in the spectral parameter away from the threshold
set, as deduced from a formula,
[
Ya1
]
.

We show in this paper similar results for
the long-range case. The continuity will be derived from a new
stationary formula, and similarly to Yafaev’s case our formula is justified
in terms of various Kato smoothness bounds. Only a few of them appears in
Yafaev’s papers. In particular our paper is strongly dependent of
certain time-independent bounds/observables which may be understood as
modifications of Dereziński’s key ingredients. In addition to

[
De
]
and
[
Ya3
]
our
paper relies on some resolvent bounds first proven in
[
GIS
]
and
then recently extended to a Besov space setting along with a (sharp) Sommerfeld
uniqueness result in
[
AIIS
]
.

For any channel

\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
we consider the

channel wave operators

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}J_{\alpha}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}(S_{a}^{\pm}(p_{a},t)+\lambda^{\alpha}t)},\quad J_{\alpha}f_{a}=u^{\alpha}\otimes f_{a},

where
H
denoted the full
N
-body Schrödinger
operator,
S_{a}^{\pm}(\xi_{a},t)
solves a certain Hamilton-Jacobi
equation (it is a ‘distortion’ of the function
t\xi_{a}^{2}
). For two
channels, say incoming
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
and outgoing
\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})
, the
corresponding entry
S_{\beta\alpha}=(W_{\beta}^{+})^{*}W_{\alpha}^{-}
of the scattering operator takes
the form in a diagonalizing (momentum) space,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{\beta\alpha}=\int^{\oplus}_{(\max\{\lambda^{\beta},\lambda^{\alpha}\},\infty)}S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

One can abstractly construct the fiber operators

S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
as almost everywhere defined quantities,
however such a construction does not seem to
provide properties
of scattering. On the contrary we prove (independently of any abstract construction) that there exist a (unique)
weakly continuous candidate away from the threshold
set. Another main result of the paper is that for fixed energy also the restriction
of the wave operators and their adjoints (the latter are called wave
matrices) exist. Given these results it makes sense to ask if there is a
‘scattering theory’ at a fixed energy? While this is not
settled in this paper we do show that almost all non-threshold
energies are ‘stationary scattering energies’. In particular at any such energy

S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
is strongly continuous.

The weak continuity property of
S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
all non-threshold
energies will be read off from
an explicit formula of the following form. Up to some trivial multiplicative
factors in momentum space and localization away from ‘collision planes’

\displaystyle S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\approx\Big((H-\lambda)\Phi_{\beta}^{+}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda-\lambda^{\beta})^{*}\Big)^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)\Big((H-\lambda)\Phi_{\alpha}^{-}J_{\alpha}\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha})^{*}\Big).

Here
\delta(H-\lambda)=\pi^{-1}\operatorname{Im}\big((H-(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)^{-1}\big)
is the delta-function of
H
at
\lambda
,
\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda-\lambda^{\beta})^{*}
and

\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha})^{*}
are certain auxiliary ’one-body wave
matrices’ (constructed from inter-cluster potentials), while the

stationary channel modifiers

\Phi_{\alpha}^{-}
and
\Phi_{\beta}^{+}
should be
considered as some appropriate, incoming and outgoing phase-space ‘distortions of the
identity’. The latter operators are introduced so that (looking only at the
right-hand factor)

\displaystyle(H-\lambda)\Phi_{\alpha}^{-}J_{\alpha}\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha})^{*}\approx\sum_{k}Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}\Big(B_{-}(a,k)Q^{-}(a,k)J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha})}^{*}\Big),

Here the sum is finite, the operators
B_{-}(a,k)
are bounded
and the only
\lambda
-dependence is sitting in the argument of the
incoming one-body wave
matrix. With a similar structure for the
left-hand factor the weak continuity follows from three properties to be
demonstrated in the paper:

(1)

Q^{+}(b,l)\delta(H-\lambda)Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}
is weakly continuous in
\lambda
.

(2)

Q^{-}(a,k)J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha})}^{*}
is
strongly continuous in
\lambda
.

(3)

Q^{+}(b,l)J_{\beta}{\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda-\lambda^{\beta})}^{*}
is strongly continuous in
\lambda
.

Note that uniform boundedness of the operator in

1
is strongly related to Kato boundedness of the

Q
-operators
[
Ka
]
. Apart from the limiting absorption principle bound the
corresponding boundedness assertions for
1
–
3

all arise from a well-known
positive commutator technique, for example used extensively in the cited papers

[
De
,
Ya3
,
Ya1
]
.

Although we take the existence of the wave operators

W_{\alpha}^{\pm}
for
granted in this paper, which is legitimate due to
[
De
]
, we remark that it may
be derived independently from the stationary setup
of the present paper. The proof of the existence and weak continuity of the
scattering matrix at all non-threshold energies may then also be considered as being independent of

[
De
]
. However for deducing unitarity and the strong continuity
for almost all energies of the scattering matrix, as obtained in the paper, we do indeed
use the completeness assertion of
[
De
]
. We leave it as a
conjecture that those stronger properties are fulfilled for
all

non-threshold energies.

We remark that our
method can be viewed as an extension of the one of

[
Ya1
]
for the short-range case (although involving several new
ingredients and a different representation of the scattering
matrix). In particular Yafaev’s
result on the weak continuity of the short-range
scattering matrix may be viewed as a consequence of our theory, see Remark
2.9

4
.

In Subsection
1.1
we elaborate on the consequences of our
long-range theory (for the general class of Dereziński-Enss pair-potentials)
applied to a
well-known atomic physics model.
The bulk of the paper is organized as follows. With a decay condition on the
channel bounds states
u^{\alpha}
and
u^{\beta}
one can obtain the weak
continuity of
S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
by a different method with pair-potentials
decaying only like
{\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{-\mu})
for
\mu>0
. We discuss
this in Section
2
. The reader may skip most
of this material, however the precise description of the
N
-body
model is given there and certain ingredients will be used in
later sections. Most notably this is the case for the theory of
Section
2
specialized to the one-body case. In
Section
3
we collect most of what is
needed from the one-body scattering theory. A main result of the
paper (this is the weak continuity discussed above) is stated in
Section
4
as Theorem

4.1
which holds under Condition

2.1
with
\mu>\sqrt{3}-1
. In the same section
we outline the scheme for proving the theorem, in particular we
introduce stationary modifiers. The ingredients from
[
Ya3
]
and

[
De
]
that we need (essentially vector field constructions) are given in Sections
5
and
6
, respectively. In addition the latter section
contains a Subsection
6.1

with two Mourre type
estimates. We need to do commutation with the
stationary modifiers which are given by functional calculus. We devote
Section
7
to studying a calculus
facilitating this task. The commutation is then implemented in Sections
8
and

9
in which the above assertions

1
and
2
-
3
are proven,
respectively. We finalize the proof of Theorem

4.1
in Subsection
9.2
. The exact expression for the scattering matrix, of the
type
indicated
above, is derived in Appendix
C
with inputs from

[
GIS
,
AIIS
]
and by using results from Sections
8
and

9
. We devote Appendix

A
and Appendix
B
to a few missing
technical details on assertions used previously.

Our second main result, which concerns representation
of channel states in terms of certain generalized eigenfunctions, is given
in
Section
10
as Theorem

10.1
. The involved
integrals are given in terms of
channel wave
matrices
that we construct for all non-threshold energies. Another issue of
Section
10
is the
construction of the
scattering matrix
for
all non-threshold energies (done concretely), as well as its
unitarity and strong continuity away from a null set of
energies, see Definition
10.6
and
Corollary
10.8
. Such generic energies, introduced more
precisely in Definition
10.2
, we call ‘stationary scattering
energies’. We characterize for any stationary scattering
energy and for any incoming

\alpha
-channel the associated
minimum
generalized eigenfunctions
in terms of their explicit asymptotic
properties, see Theorems

10.9
and
10.11
. The
latter result
may also be vieved as a characterization of the incoming

\alpha
-channel part of the
scattering matrix at any such energy.
Wave matrices have been studied in
many contexts in the literature, for example abstractly in

[
KK
]
and concretely for
N
-body Schrödinger operators
with short-range interactions in
[
Ya4
]
(our exposition is
rather different from Yafaev’s). The developed theory for stationary scattering
energies is similar to the standard
one for the one-body problem at any positive energy (in that case all positive energies are stationary scattering energies), see for example

[
DS
,
GY
]
.

As for the above discussion we ignore in the
bulk of the paper the possible existence of non-threshold
embedded eigenvalues. It is a minor technical exercise to include non-threshold
embedded eigenvalues for all results
of the paper, which however will not be discussed further.

1.1.
A principle example, atomic and molecular
N
-body
Hamiltonians

Consider a system of
N
charged particles of dimension
n
interacting by Coulomb forces. The
Hamiltonian reads

H=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2m_{j}}\Delta_{x_{j}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}q_{i}q_{j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{-1},\quad x_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,n\geq 3,

(1.1)

where
x_{j}
,
m_{j}
and
q_{j}
denote the position, mass and charge of
the
j
’th particle, respectively.

The Hamiltonian
H
is regarded as a self-adjoint operator
on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
, where
{\mathbf{X}}
is the
n(N-1)
dimensional real vector
space
{\mathbf{X}}:=\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}m_{j}x_{j}=0\}
.
Let
{\mathcal{A}}
denote the set of all cluster
decompositions of the
N
-particle system. The notation
a_{\max}
and

a_{\min}
refers to the
1
-cluster and
N
-cluster decompositions,
respectively.
Let for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
the notation
\#a
denote the number of
clusters in
a
.
For
i,j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
i<j
, we denote by
(ij)
the

(N-1)
-cluster decomposition given by letting
C=\{i,j\}
form a
cluster and all other particles
l\notin C
form singletons. We write
(ij)\leq a
if
i
and
j
belong to the same cluster
in
a
. More general, we write
b\leq a
if each cluster of
b

is a subset of a cluster of
a
. If
a
is a
k
-cluster decomposition,
a=(C_{1},\dots,C_{k})
,
we let

{\mathbf{X}}^{a}=\big\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}|\,\,\sum_{l\in C_{j}}m_{l}x_{l}=0,j=1,\dots,k\big\}={\mathbf{X}}^{C_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{k}},

and

{\mathbf{X}}_{a}=\big\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}|x_{i}=x_{j}\mbox{ if }i,j\in C_{m}\mbox{ for some }m\in\{1,\dots,k\}\big\}.

Note that
b\leq a\Leftrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}^{b}\subseteq{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
, and that the
subspaces
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
and
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
define an orthogonal decomposition
of
{\mathbf{X}}

equipped with
the quadratic form

q(x)=\sum_{j}2m_{j}|x_{j}|^{2},\hskip 28.45274ptx\in{{\mathbf{X}}}.

Consequently any
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
decomposes orthogonally as

x=x^{a}+x_{a}
with
x^{a}=\pi^{a}x\in{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
and
x_{a}=\pi_{a}x\in{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
.

With these notations, the many-body Schrödinger operator
(
1.1
) takes the form
H=H_{0}+V
,
where
H_{0}=p^{2}
is (minus) the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the
Euclidean space
({\mathbf{X}},q)
and

V=V(x)=\sum_{b=(ij)\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{b}(x^{b})
with
V_{b}(x^{b})=V_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{j})
for the
(N-1)
-cluster decomposition

b=(ij)
. Note for example that

\displaystyle x^{(12)}=\big(\tfrac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}(x_{1}-x_{2}),-\tfrac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}(x_{1}-x_{2}),0,\dots,0\big).

For any cluster
decomposition
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
we introduce the sub-Hamiltonian
H^{a}
as follows.
For
a=a_{\min}
we define

H^{a_{\min}}=0
on
\mathcal{H}^{a_{\min}}:=\mathbb{C}.

For
a\neq a_{\min}
we introduce the potential

V^{a}(x^{a})=\sum_{b=(ij)\leq a}V_{b}(x^{b});\quad x^{a}\in{\mathbf{X}}^{a}.

Then

\displaystyle H^{a}:=-\Delta_{x^{a}}+V^{a}(x^{a})=(p^{a})^{2}+V^{a}\ \ \text{on }\mathcal{H}^{a}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a}).

A channel
\alpha
is by
definition given as
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
, where

a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
and
u^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}^{a}
obeys

\lVert u^{\alpha}\rVert=1
and

(H^{a}-\lambda^{\alpha})u^{\alpha}=0
for a real number

\lambda^{\alpha}
. For any
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
the intercluster potential is by definition

\displaystyle I_{a}(x)=\sum_{b=(ij)\not\leq a}V_{b}(x^{b}).

Next we introduce atomic Dollard type channel wave operators. They read

\displaystyle W_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}{u_{\alpha}}\otimes\big({\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}(S_{a,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}(p_{a},t)+\lambda^{\alpha}t)}(\cdot)\big),

(1.2)

where

\displaystyle S_{a,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}(\xi_{a},\pm|t|)=\pm S_{a,{\rm atom}}(\pm\xi_{a},|t|)\text{ \,and\, }S_{a,{\rm atom}}(\xi_{a},t)=t\xi_{a}^{2}+\int_{1}^{t}\,I_{a}(2s\xi_{a})\,{\mathrm{d}}s.

The well-definedness of
W_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}

follows from the existence of
W_{\alpha}^{\pm}
, see Remarks

2.9

1
and
2
.
Let
I^{\alpha}=(\lambda^{\alpha},\infty)
and

k_{\alpha}=p_{a}^{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}
. By the intertwining property
HW_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}\supseteq W_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}k_{\alpha}
and the fact that
k_{\alpha}
is diagonalized by
the unitary map
F_{\alpha}:L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})\to L^{2}(I^{\alpha};L^{2}(C_{a}))
,
C_{a}=\mathbf{X}_{a}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{n_{a}-1}
with
n_{a}=\dim\mathbf{X}_{a}
, given by

\displaystyle(F_{\alpha}u)(\lambda,\omega)=(2\pi)^{-n_{a}/2}2^{-1/2}\lambda_{\alpha}^{(n_{a}-2)/4}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{1/2}_{\alpha}\omega\cdot x_{a}}u(x_{a})\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{a},\quad\lambda_{\alpha}=\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha},

we can for
any
two given channels
\alpha
and

\beta
write

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}:=F_{\beta}(W_{\beta,{\rm atom}}^{+})^{*}W_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}^{-}F_{\alpha}^{-1}=\int^{\oplus}_{I_{\beta\alpha}}S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,\quad I_{\beta\alpha}=I^{\beta}\cap I^{\alpha}.

The fiber operator
S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b}))
is from an abstract point of view (our point
of view is different as discussed previously)
a priori defined only for
a.e.
\lambda\in I_{\beta\alpha}
. It is the

\beta\alpha
-entry of the atomic Dollard type scattering matrix
S_{{\rm atom}}(\lambda)=\big(S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)\big)_{\beta\alpha}
.

Now a main result of this paper, specialized to the above atomic
model, reads as follows (see Remark
2.9

3
).

Theorem 1.1
.

The operator-valued function

S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\cdot)
is weakly continuous away
from the set of thresholds (i.e. eigenvalues of the proper sub-Hamiltonians) and
eigenvalues of
H
. Away from a null set
S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\cdot)
is strongly continuous.

More generally
S_{{\rm atom}}(\cdot)
is a weakly continuous
contraction away
from the set of thresholds and eigenvalues of
H
, and away from a null set
S_{{\rm atom}}(\cdot)
is a strongly continuous unitary operator.

A similar result is valid for Coulomb systems of charged
particles with static (i.e. infinite mass) nuclei. It is a minor
doable
issue to include non-threshold
embedded eigenvalues in the above result (as mentioned above). The
behaviour at thresholds is much more intriguing and completely outside
the scope of this paper. Strong continuity for all non-threshold
energies (as conjectured before) is another difficult problem.

The second main result, again for convenience specialized to the above
model, reads as follows. Recall the standard notation for weighted spaces

\displaystyle L_{s}^{2}(\mathbf{X})=\langle x\rangle^{-s}L^{2}(\mathbf{X});\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}},\,\,\langle x\rangle=\big(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\alpha
be a given channel
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
,
f:I^{\alpha}=(\lambda^{\alpha},\infty)\to{\mathbb{C}}
be
continuous and compactly supported away from the set of
thresholds and
eigenvalues, and let
s>1/2
. For any
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}f(k_{\alpha})\varphi=\int_{I^{\alpha}}\,f(\lambda)W^{\pm}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)(F_{\alpha}\varphi)(\lambda,\cdot)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\in L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),

(1.3)

where the wave matrices

W^{\pm}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\big)

with a strongly continuous dependence of
\lambda
.
In particular for
\varphi\in L_{s}^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{b})

the integrand is a continuous compactly supported

L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
-valued function. In general the integral
has the weak interpretation of a measurable

L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
-valued function.

Of particular interest is the case
f(\lambda)=f_{t}(\lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t\lambda}f_{0}(\lambda)
,

t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, in
which case the formulas (
1.3
) represent exact
Schrödinger wave packets of energy-localized states outgoing to or incoming from
the channel
\alpha
. For a state
\varphi^{-}_{\alpha}=W^{-}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}f_{0}(k_{\alpha})\varphi
the asymptotics of the
wave packet is ‘controlled’ as
t\to-\infty
. To
‘understand’ the behaviour as
t\to+\infty
(and possibly compare
with practical physics experiments) the right-hand side of
(
1.3
) in principle provides a tool. The main contribution comes from the
asymptotics of functions in the range of the outgoing resolvent

(H-(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0))^{-1}
(as confirmed by one of our formulas for the
generalized eigenfunctions in the range of
W^{-}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)
). On the other hand this quantity is in general poorly
understood for the
N
-body case. In
the paper we
derive several non-trivial estimates (more
precisely
mostly
for the delta-function of
H
only), however they are all of
‘weak type’. Strong resolvent bounds is yet another difficult
problem with room for desirable improvements. So maybe the main virtue
of Theorem
1.2
and its generalizations at
this point is conceptual: The wave matrices

W^{\pm}_{\alpha}(\lambda)
uniquely exist
admitting representations like (
1.3
).

From a practical point of view it is more convenient to substitute the
factor
W^{-}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)
in (
1.3
) by

\displaystyle W^{-}_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)=\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda}W^{+}_{\beta,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda).

(1.4)

Now each term contributes by a term which is ‘controlled’
as
t\to+\infty
, meaning that all relevant large time information
of our ‘ incoming
\alpha
-channel experiment’ is encoded in the components
of the scattering matrix. In turn,
although
the resolvent is complicated, it appears in formulas only as
the delta-function of
H
sandwiched by some Kato smooth
operators (as
discussed previously). We do not in this paper offer ‘finer analysis’ of the
derived formulas on interesting (but difficult) issues like regularity or asymptotics of associated
distributional kernels.

Finally let us remark that we indeed show the
validity of
(
1.4
) as an identity in

{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\big)
(summing in the weak sense) for
all ‘stationary scattering energies’, see
Definition
10.2
. This is for almost all energies,
and it is also an immediate consequence of our theory that at
any stationary scattering energy the scattering matrix
S_{\rm atom}(\cdot)
is a strongly continuous unitary operator
determined uniquely by asymptotics of minimum
generalized eigenfunctions.

2.
Preliminaries

We introduce the standard abstract
N
-body setup and introduce a
radial limit for the one-body case (more precisely for a specific
one-body potential). Then we develop a stationary
scattering theory for the
N
-body case with a decay assumption on
the channel bound states, Condition
2.3
, and
prove under this condition the existence and weak continuity of
entries of the
scattering matrix, see Theorem
2.12
. The
later sections of the paper do not involve Condition

2.3
. The reader may skip all results of the
section based on Condition

2.3
and go directly to Section
3
where we have collected most of what is needed for the later
sections (except primarily for Subsection
2.1
, some notation and the discussion of the eikonal and
Hamilton-Jacobi equations). Section
3
concerns a specific one-body problem for which,
essentially being viewed as a
special case, the results of the present section apply.

2.1.
N
-body Hamiltonians, limiting absorption principle and
notation

Let
{\mathbf{X}}
be
a finite dimensional real inner product space,
equipped with a
finite family
\{{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\}_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}
of subspaces closed under intersection:
For any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
there exists
c\in\mathcal{A}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\cap{\mathbf{X}}_{b}={\mathbf{X}}_{c}.

(2.1)

For the example of Subsection
1.1
the elements of
{\mathcal{A}}
are cluster
decompositions, but here
{\mathcal{A}}
is an abstract index set. We
order
{\mathcal{A}}
by writing
a\leq b
(or equivalently as
b\geq a
) if

{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\supseteq{\mathbf{X}}_{b}
.
It is assumed that there exist

a_{\min},a_{\max}\in{\mathcal{A}}
such that

{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{\min}}={\mathbf{X}},\quad{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{\max}}=\{0\}.

Let
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\subseteq{\mathbf{X}}
be the orthogonal complement of
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\subseteq{\mathbf{X}}
,
and denote the associated orthogonal decomposition of
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
by

x=x^{a}\oplus x_{a}=\pi^{a}x\oplus\pi_{a}x\in{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\oplus{\mathbf{X}}_{a}.

The component
x_{a}
may be called the
inter-cluster coordinates
,
and
x^{a}
the
internal coordinates
.
We note that the family
\{\mathbf{X}^{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
is closed under addition:
For any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
there exists
c\in\mathcal{A}
such that

{\mathbf{X}}^{a}+{\mathbf{X}}^{b}={\mathbf{X}}^{c},

cf. (
2.1
).

A real-valued measurable function
V\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
is
a
potential of many-body type

if there exist real-valued measurable functions

V_{a}\colon{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle V(x)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}V_{a}(x^{a})\ \ \text{for }x\in\mathbf{X}.

(2.2)

We take
V_{a_{\min}}=0
(without loss of
generality). We impose throughout the paper the
following condition for
a\neq a_{\min}
. By definition
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
.

Condition 2.1
.

There exists
\mu\in(0,1)
such that for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\min}\}
the
potential
V_{a}(x^{a})=V^{a}_{\rm sr}(x^{a})+V^{a}_{\rm lr}(x^{a})
, where

(1)

V^{a}_{\rm sr}(-\Delta_{a}+1)^{-1}
is compact and
\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{1+\mu}V^{a}_{\rm sr}(-\Delta_{a}+1)^{-1}
is bounded.

(2)

V^{a}_{\rm lr}\in C^{\infty}
and for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim\mathbf{X}^{a}}

\displaystyle\partial^{\gamma}V^{a}_{\rm lr}(x^{a})={\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{-\mu-|\gamma|}).

The
sub-Hamiltonian

H^{a}
associated with a cluster
decomposition
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
is defined as follows.
For
a=a_{\min}
we define

H^{a_{\min}}=0
on
\mathcal{H}^{a_{\min}}=L^{2}(\{0\})=\mathbb{C}.

For
a\neq a_{\min}

we introduce

V^{a}(x^{a})=\sum_{b\leq a}V_{b}(x^{b}),\quad x^{a}\in{\mathbf{X}}^{a},

and then

\displaystyle H^{a}=-\Delta_{x^{a}}+V^{a}\ \ \text{on }\mathcal{H}^{a}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a}).

We abbreviate

\displaystyle V^{a_{\max}}=V,\quad H^{a_{\max}}=H,\quad\mathcal{H}^{a_{\max}}=\mathcal{H}.

The
thresholds
of the
full Hamiltonian

H
are the
eigenvalues of the (proper) sub-Hamiltonians
H^{a}
;

a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
.
Equivalently stated the set of thresholds is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}(H):=\bigcup_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a}).

Note that for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\min},a_{\max}\}
the family
\{\mathbf{X}_{b}\cap\mathbf{X}^{a}\}_{b\leq a}
forms
a family of subspaces of many-body type in
\mathbf{X}^{a}
. This
self-similarity structure is useful for induction arguments involved
in the proofs of various well-known properties:
We recall (see for example
[
AIIS
]
) that under
Condition
2.1

the set

{\mathcal{T}}(H)
is closed and at most countable. Moreover the set of non-threshold eigenvalues
is discrete in
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathcal{T}}(H)
, and it
can only accumulate at points in

{\mathcal{T}}(H)
from below.
The essential spectrum of
H
is given by the formula

\sigma_{{\mathrm{ess}}}(H)=\bigl[\min{\mathcal{T}}(H),\infty\bigr)
. We refer to the
mimimum example
{\mathcal{A}}=\{a_{\min},a_{\max}\}
as the
one-body model
in which case
{\mathcal{T}}(H)=\{0\}
.

Define the
Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}
of order
s\in\mathbb{R}

associated with
H
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}=(H-E)^{-s/2}\mathcal{H};\quad E=\min\sigma(H)-1.

(2.3)

We note that the form domain of
H
is

{\mathcal{H}}^{1}=H^{1}(\mathbf{X})
and that the operator domain
\mathcal{D}(H)=\mathcal{H}^{2}=H^{2}(\mathbf{X})
(i.e. given by standard
Sobolev spaces). It is standard to consider
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{-2}

as the corresponding dual spaces. Denote
R(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
for

z\notin\sigma(H)
.

Consider and fix
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\displaystyle\chi(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{ for }t\leq 4/3,\\
1&\mbox{ for }t\geq 5/3,\end{array}\right.\quad\chi^{\prime}\geq 0,

and such that the following properties are fulfilled:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\chi},\sqrt{\chi^{\prime}},(1-\chi^{2})^{1/4},\sqrt{-\big((1-\chi^{2})^{1/2}\big)^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}.

We define correspondingly
\chi_{+}=\chi
and

\chi_{-}=(1-\chi^{2})^{1/2}
and record that

\displaystyle\chi_{+}^{2}+\chi_{-}^{2}=1\text{ \,and\, }\sqrt{\chi_{+}},\sqrt{\chi_{+}^{\prime}},\sqrt{\chi_{-}},\sqrt{-\chi_{-}^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}.

We shall use the standard notation

\langle x\rangle:=\big(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}\big)^{1/2}
for
x\in\mathbf{X}
(or more generally for
any
x
in a normed space). If
T
is an operator on a
Hilbert space
{\mathcal{G}}
and
\varphi\in{\mathcal{G}}
then

\langle T\rangle_{\varphi}:=\langle\varphi,T\varphi\rangle
. We denote the space of bounded operators
from one (general) Banach space
X
to another one
Y
by
{\mathcal{L}}(X,Y)

and abbreviate
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mathcal{L}(X,X)
. The dual space of
X

is denoted by
X^{*}
.

To define
Besov spaces
associated with the multiplication operator

|x|
on
{\mathcal{H}}

let

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle=F\bigl(\bigl\{x\in\mathbf{X}\,\big|\,\lvert x\rvert<1\bigr\}\bigr),

\displaystyle F_{m}

\displaystyle=F\bigl(\bigl\{x\in\mathbf{X}\,\big|\,2^{m-1}\leq\lvert x\rvert<2^{m}\bigr\}\bigr)\quad\text{for }m=1,2,\dots,

where
F(U)=F_{U}
is the sharp characteristic function of any given subset

U\subseteq{\mathbf{X}}
.
The Besov spaces
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{X})
,
\mathcal{B}^{*}=\mathcal{B}^{*}(\mathbf{X})
and
\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}=\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}(\mathbf{X})
are then given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{X})\,\big|\,\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}}<\infty\bigr\},\quad\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}2^{m/2}\|F_{m}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{*}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{X})\,\big|\,\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{*}}<\infty\bigr\},\quad\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{*}}=\sup_{m\geq 0}2^{-m/2}\|F_{m}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\psi\in\mathcal{B}^{*}\,\Big|\,\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{-m/2}\|F_{m}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}=0\Bigr\},

respectively.
Denote the standard
weighted
L^{2}
spaces
by

L_{s}^{2}=L_{s}^{2}(\mathbf{X})=\langle x\rangle^{-s}L^{2}(\mathbf{X})\ \ \text{for }s\in\mathbb{R},\quad L_{-\infty}^{2}=\bigcup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}L^{2}_{s},\quad L^{2}_{\infty}=\bigcap_{s\in\mathbb{R}}L_{s}^{2}.

Then for any
s>1/2

\displaystyle L^{2}_{s}\subsetneq\mathcal{B}\subsetneq L^{2}_{1/2}\subsetneq\mathcal{H}\subsetneq L^{2}_{-1/2}\subsetneq\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}\subsetneq\mathcal{B}^{*}\subsetneq L^{2}_{-s}.

(2.6)

Under a more general condition than Condition

2.1
it is demonstrated in
[
AIIS
]
that the
following limits exist locally
uniformly
in
\lambda\not\in{\mathcal{T}}(H)\cup\sigma_{{\rm pp}}(H)
:

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\,R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s}\big)\text{ for any }s>1/2.

(2.7a)

Furthermore,
in the strong weak
∗
-topology,

\displaystyle\begin{split}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)&=\swslim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\,R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\big)\\
&\text{
with a
locally
uniform
norm bound in
}\lambda\not\in\sigma_{{\rm pp}}(H)\cup{\mathcal{T}}(H).\end{split}

(2.7b)

2.2.
A one-body effective potential and one-body radial
limits

We introduce
I^{\rm sr}_{a}=\sum_{b\not\leq a}V_{\rm sr}^{b}
,
I^{\rm lr}_{a}=\sum_{b\not\leq a}V_{\rm lr}^{b}
,
I_{a}=I^{\rm sr}_{a}+I^{\rm lr}_{a}
and

\displaystyle\breve{I}_{a}=\breve{I}_{a}(x_{a})=I^{\rm lr}_{a}(x_{a})\prod_{b\not\leq a}\,\,\chi_{+}(|\pi^{b}x_{a}|\ln\langle x_{a}\rangle/\langle x_{a}\rangle);\quad a\neq a_{\max}.

This ‘regularization’
\breve{I}_{a}
appears in
[
HS
]
for the free channel,
i.e. for
a=a_{\min}
. It is a one-body potential fulfilling for any

\breve{\mu}\in(0,\mu)
the bounds

\displaystyle\partial^{\gamma}\breve{I}_{a}(x_{a})={\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-\breve{\mu}-|\gamma|}).

(2.8)

For notational
convenience we take from this point and throughout the paper
\breve{\mu}=\mu
,
i.e. more precisely we will assume (
2.8
) with

\breve{\mu}
replaced by
\mu
. We let

K_{a}(\cdot,\lambda)
,
\lambda>0
, denote the corresponding approximate solution
to the eikonal equation
\lvert\nabla_{x_{a}}K_{a}\rvert^{2}+\breve{I}_{a}=\lambda
as taken
from
[
Is
,
II
]
.
More precisely writing
K_{a}(x_{a},\lambda)=\sqrt{\lambda}\lvert x_{a}\rvert-k_{a}(x_{a},\lambda)
the following properties
are fulfilled with
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}:=(0,\infty)
and
n_{a}:=\dim\mathbf{X}_{a}
. The functions
k_{a}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{X}_{a}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
and:

(1)

For any compact
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
there exists
R>1

such that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and all
x_{a}\in\mathbf{X}_{a}

with
\lvert x_{a}\rvert>R

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\lambda}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\lvert x_{a}\rvert}k_{a}=\breve{I}_{a}(x_{a})+\lvert\nabla_{x_{a}}k\rvert^{2}.

(2)

For all multiindices
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{n_{a}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
compact
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

\displaystyle\big\lvert{\partial}_{{x_{a}}}^{\gamma}{\partial}_{\lambda}^{m}k_{a}\big\rvert\leq C\langle x_{a}\rangle^{1-\lvert\gamma\rvert-\mu}\text{
uniformly in }\lambda\in\Lambda.

We drop for the moment the subscript
a
and consider the one-body
Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\breve{h}=\breve{h}_{a}=-\Delta+\breve{I}\quad{on}\quad L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),

identifying

\mathbf{X}_{a}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
where
n=n_{a}
. Let
{\mathcal{B}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
denote
corresponding Besov spaces, cf. Subsection
2.1
, and let
C_{a}=\mathbf{X}_{a}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{n_{a}-1}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
U^{\prime}=U\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\neq\emptyset
and
U\cap\{\lvert x\rvert\geq 1\}=\{x=cx^{\prime}|\,c\in[1,\infty),x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}\}
. Let for any
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(U^{\prime})
,

\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle v^{\pm}(x)=v_{\lambda}^{\pm}[g](x)=\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\lvert x\rvert^{(1-n)/2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\mathrm{i}K_{a}(x,\lambda)}g(\hat{x});\quad\hat{x}=x/\lvert x\rvert.

Suppose
\breve{u}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\cap H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and

F_{U}(\breve{h}-\lambda)\breve{u}\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1}\int_{\lvert x\rvert<\rho}\overline{v^{\pm}(x)}\breve{u}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\pm 2^{-1}\mathrm{i}\lambda^{-1/2}\big(\langle v^{\pm},(\breve{h}-\lambda)\breve{u}\rangle-\langle(\breve{h}-\lambda)v^{\pm},\breve{u}\rangle\big).

(2.9)

Proof.

First
we compute for any
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a})
and
\lambda>0

\displaystyle(\breve{h}-\lambda)v^{\pm}_{\lambda}[g]\in L^{2}_{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})=\langle x_{a}\rangle^{-s}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\text{
for any }s\in\big(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}+\mu\big).

(2.10)

In particular

v^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
and
(\breve{h}-\lambda)v^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, whence
the right-hand side of (
2.9
) is well-defined. For any

\epsilon\in(0,1/3)
choose a decreasing function
\chi_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\displaystyle\chi_{\epsilon}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{ for }t\leq\epsilon,\\
1+\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon-t&\mbox{ for }3\epsilon\leq t\leq 1,\\
0&\mbox{ for }t\geq 1+2\epsilon,\end{array}\right.

and
\chi^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\geq-1
. Letting

\chi_{\epsilon,\rho}=\chi_{\epsilon}(\lvert x\rvert/\rho)
,
\rho>1
, we
compute the right-hand side of (
2.9
) as

\displaystyle\mp 2^{-1}\lambda^{-1/2}\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\langle v^{\pm},\mathrm{i}[\breve{h},\chi_{\epsilon,\rho}]\breve{u}\rangle=-\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1}\langle v^{\pm},\chi^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\lvert\cdot\rvert/\rho)\breve{u}\rangle.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we can write

\displaystyle-\rho^{-1}\langle v^{\pm},\chi^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\lvert\cdot\rvert/\rho)\breve{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\int_{3\epsilon<\lvert x\rvert/\rho<1}\overline{v^{\pm}(x)}\breve{u}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\int_{\lvert x\rvert/\rho<1}\overline{v^{\pm}(x)}\breve{u}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\epsilon}),

where the bounds are uniform in
\rho>1
. Whence

\displaystyle-\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1}\langle v^{\pm},\chi^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\lvert\cdot\rvert/\rho)\breve{u}\rangle=\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1}\int_{\lvert x\rvert/\rho<1}\overline{v^{\pm}(x)}\breve{u}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

∎

Remarks
.

(1)

The concept of radial limits introduced in Lemma

2.2
(and extended to the
N
-body case in
Corollary
2.5
below) is rather weak. A
stronger concept of radial limits
(conforming with the weak one)
appears in several papers, see
[
GY
,
HS
,
II
,
Is
,
IS2
]
. The
latter requires radiation condition bounds and leads to stronger
conclusions, see Section
3
.

(2)

We learned
about the assertion Lemma

2.2
from
[
As
]
, where it appears with a very
different proof. Similarly the following Subsections
2.3
–
2.5
have overlap with
[
As
]
although the goals
and
presentation are different. A main result of these subsections is
the existence and weak continuity of entries of the
scattering matrix under a suitable decay condition on
the channel bound states.

2.3.
Radial limits for channels

Consider any channel

\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
, i.e.
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
,
u^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}^{a}
and

(H^{a}-\lambda^{\alpha})u^{\alpha}=0
, obeying the following decay property.

Condition 2.3
.

With
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
, the (sub) bound state

\displaystyle u^{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\langle x^{a}\rangle^{s})\text{ for some }s>\tfrac{5}{2}-\mu.

(2.11)

Alternatively stated, Condition
2.3
reads
u^{\alpha}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X}^{a})=\langle x^{a}\rangle^{-s}L^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{a})
for
some
s>\tfrac{5}{2}-\mu
.

Let

{\mathcal{H}}_{a}=L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
and
\pi_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{a})
be given by

(\pi_{\alpha}v)(x_{a})=\langle u^{\alpha},v(\cdot,x_{a})\rangle
. It is a
consequence of
(
2.11
) that

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X}_{a}))\cap{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}),{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a}))

(2.12)

(only
u^{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}^{a})
is needed at this point).

Recalling
C_{a}=\mathbf{X}_{a}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{n_{a}-1}
,
n_{a}=\dim\mathbf{X}_{a}
, we let
C^{\prime}_{a}=C_{a}\setminus\cup_{{b\not\leq a}}\mathbf{X}_{b}
.

Suppose
u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X})
obeys
(H-\lambda)u\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})
;
\lambda>\lambda^{\alpha}
. We shall then introduce

Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(u)\in L^{2}(C_{a})
by the weak radial limit
procedure of Subsection
2.2
.
For this end we let, parallel to Lemma
2.2
and as a first step, for any
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C^{\prime}_{a})

\displaystyle{Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(u)}[g]=\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1}\int_{\lvert x_{a}\rvert<\rho}\overline{v_{\lambda,\alpha}^{\pm}[g](x_{a})}(\pi_{\alpha}u)(x_{a})\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{a},

(2.13a)

where

\displaystyle v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g](x_{a})=\chi_{+}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert)\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{(1-n_{a})/2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\mathrm{i}K_{a}(x_{a},\lambda_{\alpha})}g(\hat{x}_{a});\quad\lambda_{\alpha}=\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha}.

We claim that indeed
Lemma
2.2

applies to
\breve{u}=\pi_{\alpha}u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
, considering any open

U^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{U^{\prime}}\subseteq C_{a}^{\prime}
and
any
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(U^{\prime})
,
yielding the well-definedness of
(
2.13a
) and the formula

\displaystyle{Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(u)}[g]=\pm 2^{-1}\mathrm{i}\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1/2}\big(\langle J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g],(H-\lambda)u\rangle-\langle(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g],u\rangle\big),

(2.13b)

where the out- and incoming quasi-modes
J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]:=u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

With Condition

2.3
imposed on
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
, for any
\lambda>\lambda^{\alpha}
and
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C^{\prime}_{a})

\displaystyle(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}).

(2.14)

Furthermore, if
u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X})
obeys
(H-\lambda)u\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})
, then (
2.13b
) is fulfilled with the
left-hand side given as the limit (
2.13a
).

Proof.

Note first that with (
2.11
) at our disposal

\displaystyle(I_{a}-\breve{I}_{a})J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})\text{
for some }s>1/2,

(2.15)

which may be proved as follows using the elementary bound

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\langle\pi^{b}x_{a}+y\rangle\geq\langle\pi^{b}x_{a}\rangle\langle y\rangle^{-1};\quad y\in\mathbf{X}.

For any
b\not\leq a
and
\kappa\in[0,1]
we
estimate (for
\hat{x}_{a}\in\operatorname{supp}g
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\lvert V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x)-V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x_{a})\big\rvert^{\kappa}&=\Big\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\pi^{b}x^{a}\cdot(\nabla V_{\rm lr}^{b})\big(\pi^{b}(x_{a}+tx^{a})\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\Big\rvert^{\kappa}\\
&=\langle x^{a}\rangle^{(\mu+2)\kappa}{\mathcal{O}}\big(\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-(\mu+1)\kappa}\big),\end{split}

(2.16a)

\displaystyle\big\lvert V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x)-V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x_{a})\big\rvert^{1-\kappa}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{a}\rangle^{\mu(1-\kappa)}{\mathcal{O}}\big(\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-\mu(1-\kappa)}\big).

(2.16b)

We choose
\kappa>1-\mu
, slightly bigger, and then
use (
2.11
) to assure that
u^{\alpha}\in L_{\delta}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{a})
for some

\delta>(\mu+2)\kappa+\mu(1-\kappa)+1/2
, concluding that

\displaystyle\big(V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x)-V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x_{a})\big)J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})\text{
for some }s>1/2.

Similarly

\displaystyle\langle\pi^{b}x\rangle^{-(1+\mu)\kappa}\leq C\langle\pi^{b}x_{a}\rangle^{-(1+\mu)\kappa}\langle x^{a}\rangle^{(1+\mu)\kappa}

(2.16c)

leads to the requirement (by chosing
\kappa>(1+\mu)^{-1}
, slightly bigger)
that
u^{\alpha}\in L_{\delta}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{a})
for some
\delta>3/2
(which is consistent with (
2.11
)) for
concluding that

\displaystyle V_{\rm sr}^{b}J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})\text{
for some }s>1/2.

Next we write

\displaystyle H=H^{a}\otimes I+I\otimes{\breve{h}}_{a}+(I_{a}-\breve{I}_{a})

and conclude from (
2.10
) and (
2.15
) that

\displaystyle(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})\text{
for some }s>1/2,

(2.17)

which is stronger than (
2.14
). Consequently the second
term on the
right-hand side of (
2.13b
) is well-defined. The
well-definedness of the first term to the right follows from (
2.12
) (implying
J_{\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X}_{a}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X}))
by Fubini’s
theorem).

Finally we can justify (
2.13a
) and (
2.13b
) by
combining (
2.10
), (
2.12
), the above arguments for (
2.14
) and Lemma

2.2
. We can write the right-hand side
of (
2.13b
) exactly as the right-hand side
of (
2.9
) with
\breve{u}:=\pi_{\alpha}u\,(\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X}_{a}))
and
\lambda
there replaced by

\lambda_{\alpha}\,(>0)
. With
F_{U}
given as
in Lemma
2.2
we can check the condition
F_{U}(\breve{h}_{a}-\lambda_{\alpha})\breve{u}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
of Lemma

2.2
as follows. By an approximation argument we see that

\displaystyle F_{U}\pi_{\alpha}(H-\lambda)u-F_{U}(\breve{h}_{a}-\lambda_{\alpha})\breve{u}=F_{U}\pi_{\alpha}(I_{a}-\breve{I}_{a})u.

By the arguments for (
2.14
) the right-hand
side is in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
, and by assumption also the first term
to the left is in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
. Consequently indeed
F_{U}(\breve{h}_{a}-\lambda_{\alpha})\breve{u}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
. Note also that the right-hand
side when implemented in an expansion of the first term of
(
2.13b
) cancels with a term arising from a similar expansion of the second term of (
2.13b
).
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

For any channel
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
obeying Condition
2.3

and any
u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X})
obeying
(H-\lambda)u\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})
for some
\lambda>\lambda^{\alpha}

there exist the weak limits

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(u)=\wLlim_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1}\int_{0}^{\rho}r^{(n_{a}-1)/2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\mathrm{i}K_{a}(r\cdot,\lambda_{\alpha})}\big(\pi_{\alpha}u\big)(r\cdot)\,{\mathrm{d}}r

Proof.

By (
2.12
) and the Riesz theorem the right-hand side of (
2.13a
) belongs

L^{2}(C_{a})
as a bounded linear functional in

g\in L^{2}(C_{a}^{\prime})=L^{2}(C_{a})
. The result then follows from Fubini’s theorem.
∎

A useful example is given by
u=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f
, where
f\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})
,

\displaystyle\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}:=I^{\alpha}\setminus\big(\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H)\cup{\mathcal{T}}(H)\big)\quad\text{with}\quad I^{\alpha}:=(\lambda^{\alpha},\infty),

(2.18)

and (
2.7b
) is used.
By (
2.13b
) and Lemma

2.6

1
(stated below) it follows that

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)f)=0.

(2.19)

On the other hand the functions

Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f)
are in general nonzero,
see Lemmas
2.6

2
and

2.7
below. For later use we record that, thanks to (
2.13a
),

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big(R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)(\cdot)\big)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}),L^{2}(C_{a})\big),

(2.20)

and as functions of
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}
these
operators are locally bounded.

2.4.
Poisson operators and geometric scattering matrix

Consider a channel

\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
obeying Condition
2.3
, and
consider the
quasi-modes
J_{\alpha}v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]=u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]
,
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime})
, cf. Subsection
2.3
. The
assertion (
2.17
) allows
us
to define for
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}
the exact
generalized eigenfunctions in
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X})
,

\displaystyle P^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]

\displaystyle=J_{\alpha}v^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]-R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g],

\displaystyle\check{P}^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]

\displaystyle=J_{\alpha}v^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]-R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g].

Lemma 2.6
.

For any channel

(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
obeying Condition
2.3
,
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}
and
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime})
:

(1)

\check{P}^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]=0
,

(2)

g=Q^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big(P^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\big)
,

(3)

g=Q^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big(R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\big)
.

Proof.

A computation using (
6.1
) and (
6.2a
) (stated below)
shows that the
eigenfunctions
\check{P}^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]
are purely
incoming/outgoing in the sense of
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
, see Appendix
A
for details. Whence
1
follows from the conclusion of
this version of the Sommerfeld uniqueness result.
Clearly
2
and

3
follow from (
2.19
) and
1
, respectively.
∎

The
Poisson operators

P^{\mp}_{\lambda,\alpha}

fulfill the following properties.

Lemma 2.7
.

For any channel

(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
obeying Condition
2.3
,
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime})
and any
f\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle\pm 2^{-1}\mathrm{i}\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1/2}\langle P^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g],f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle g,Q^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}(R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f)\rangle,

(2.21a)

\displaystyle P^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\mathbf{X})\big).

(2.21b)

In particular
P^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}
depends
strongly weak
∗
-continuously of
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}
. In
the weaker topology of
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbf{X}))
,

s>1/2
,
P^{\pm}_{\lambda,\alpha}
are strongly continuous in
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

The formulas (
2.21a
) follow by applying (
2.13b
) to
u=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f
. The assertion (
2.21b
) follows
from (
2.21a
) and (
2.20
). The continuity assertions follow from checking the computation
(
2.17
) and combining with regularity of
the function
K_{a}
and continuity of the boundary values of the
resolvent, cf. (
2.7a
).
∎

For two channels
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
and
\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})
both fulfilling Condition
2.3
the

geometric scattering matrix
, or rather the component given by considering
\alpha
as incoming and

\beta
as outgoing, is given by

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g=Q^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}\big(P^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\big);\quad\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathcal{E}}^{\beta},\,g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime}).

(2.22a)

Alternatively this quantity is given by the radial limit

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g=-Q^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}\big(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\big).

(2.22b)

Lemma 2.8
.

The component

\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
of the
geometric scattering matrix extends to an operator in

{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b})\big)
with
weakly continuous dependence of
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathcal{E}}^{\beta}
.

Proof.

It follows from (
2.22a
), (
2.13a
),
(
2.12
) and (
2.21b
) that the extension
\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b})\big)
exists. We record that it is locally uniformly
bounded in
\lambda
. The weak continuity assertion then follows
from (
2.22b
), Lemma

2.7
and (
2.17
) (including
the underlying computation for (
2.17
)
exhibiting continuity). Indeed for any
g_{a}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime})
and
g_{b}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{b}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\langle g_{b},Q^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g_{a}])\rangle=2^{-1}\mathrm{i}\lambda_{\beta}^{-1/2}\langle P^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{b}],(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g_{a}]\rangle.

Due to the last assertion of Lemma

2.7
the first entry of the inner product to
the right is continuous as an
L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbf{X})
-valued
function. The second entry is continuous as an
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})
-valued
function, provided
s
is taken slightly larger than
1/2
, so indeed
the right-hand side is continuous.
∎

2.5.
Time-dependent scattering theory and stationary
representations

For any channel

\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
obeying Condition

2.3
one can
easily show the existence of

channel wave operators
by the Cook criterion using
(
2.16a
)–(
2.16c
) and one-body scattering theory
[
Hö1
]
. Consequently, more precisely, we introduce

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}J_{\alpha}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}(S_{a}^{\pm}(p_{a},t)+\lambda^{\alpha}t)},

(2.23)

where
p_{a}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla_{x_{a}}
and
S_{a}^{\pm}(\xi_{a},\pm|t|)=\pm S_{a}(\pm\xi_{a},|t|)
is
defined in term of the Legendre transform of the function
K_{a}(\cdot,\lambda)
of Subsection
2.2
. This amounts to the
following construction,
citing
[
II
, Lemma 6.1]
:

There exist an
\mathbf{X}_{a}
-valued function
x(\xi,t)
and a
positive function
\lambda(\xi,t)
both in
C^{\infty}\big((\mathbf{X}_{a}\setminus\{0\})\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\big)
and satisfying the following requirements.
For any compact set
B\subseteq\mathbf{X}_{a}\setminus\{0\}
, there exist
T,C>0
such that for
\xi\in B

and
t>T

(1)

\xi=\partial_{x_{a}}K_{a}\big(x(\xi,t),\lambda(\xi,t)\big),\quad t=\partial_{\lambda}K_{a}\big(x(\xi,t),\lambda(\xi,t)\big),

(2)

\lvert x(\xi,t)-2t\xi\rvert\leq C\langle t\rangle^{1-\mu},\quad\big\lvert\lambda(\xi,t)-\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\big\rvert\leq C\langle t\rangle^{-\mu}.

Then we define

\displaystyle S_{a}(\xi,t)=x(\xi,t)\cdot\xi+\lambda(\xi,t)t-K_{a}(x(\xi,t),\lambda(\xi,t));\quad(\xi,t)\in\big(\mathbf{X}_{a}\setminus{0}\big)\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Note that this function solves the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}S_{a}(\xi,t)=\xi^{2}+{\breve{I}}_{a}\big(\partial_{\xi}S_{a}(\xi,t)\big);\quad t>t(\xi),\,\xi\neq 0.

Remarks 2.9
.

(1)

For
\mu>1/2
in Condition
2.1
one can use
the Dollard-approximation (cf.
[
Do
]
) rather than the above exact solution
S_{a}
to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation to define channel wave operators. It reads

\displaystyle S_{a,{\mathrm{dol}}}(\xi,t)=t\xi^{2}+\int_{1}^{t}\,{\breve{I}}_{a}(2s\xi)\,{\mathrm{d}}s.

Introducing
S_{a,{\mathrm{dol}}}^{\pm}(\xi,\pm|t|)=\pm S_{a,{\mathrm{dol}}}(\pm\xi,|t|)
, the existence of the limits

\displaystyle W_{\alpha,{\mathrm{dol}}}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}J_{\alpha}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}(S_{a,{\mathrm{dol}}}^{\pm}(p_{a},t)+\lambda^{\alpha}t)}

(2.24)

then follows by the chain rule (assuming that

W_{\alpha}^{\pm}
exist) and from the bounds

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\,\lvert{\breve{I}}_{a}\big(\partial_{\xi}S_{a}(\xi,s)\big)-{\breve{I}}_{a}(2s\xi)\rvert\,{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty;\quad\xi\neq 0.

In turn the latter are consequences of the bounds

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{\xi}S_{a}(\xi,t)-2t\xi\rvert\leq C\langle t\rangle^{1-\mu};\quad C=C(\xi)<\infty.

(2.25)

Note that (
2.25
) follows from the above
property
2
and the fact that

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}S_{a}(\xi,t)-x(\xi,t)=0\text{ for }t>t(\xi).

(2)

The construction of
1
applies
to the example of Subsection
1.1
and for that case one can alternatively use the
function
S_{a,{\rm atom}}(\xi,t)
defined there. Note the property

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\,\lvert I_{a}(2s\xi)-{\breve{I}}_{a}(2s\xi)\rvert\,{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty;\quad\xi\in{\mathbf{X}_{a}}\setminus\cup_{{c\not\leq a}}\mathbf{X}_{c}.

By the chain rule the existence of
W_{\alpha,{\rm atom}}^{\pm}
then follows from the existence of

W_{\alpha,{\mathrm{dol}}}^{\pm}
.

(3)

It is also an elementary fact that the existence, along
with continuity properties of the scattering matrix, to be stated in
Theorems
2.12
and
4.1
(under
different hypotheses) imply similar assertions for the ‘Dollard’ and
‘atomic’ counterparts discussed above. For example, under the conditions
of Theorem
4.1

\displaystyle S_{\beta\alpha,{\rm atom}}(\lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta^{+}_{\beta}(\cdot,\lambda)}S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda){\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\theta^{-}_{\alpha}(\cdot,\lambda)},

where on the right-hand side the multiplication operators to the left and to
the right are both strongly continuous factors (they are given by
explicit integrals). Whence Theorem
1.1
is an
immediate consequence of Theorem
4.1
and
Corollary
10.8
.

(4)

For short-range systems, say given by
Condition
2.1
with the additional requirement

V^{a}_{\rm lr}(x^{a})={\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{-1-\epsilon})
for some

\epsilon>0
, we may use
S_{a,{\rm sr}}(\xi,t):=t\xi^{2}
rather
than
S_{a}(\xi,t)
. Arguing as above we can then use the results of this
paper (derived for the function
S_{a}
) to deduce analogue results for stationary
short-range scattering theory (based on the function
S_{a,{\rm sr}}
), overlapping with
[
Ya1
,
Ya4
]
.

Let

k_{\alpha}=p_{a}^{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}
and recall
I^{\alpha}=(\lambda^{\alpha},\infty)
. By the intertwining property
HW_{\alpha}^{\pm}\supseteq W_{\alpha}^{\pm}k_{\alpha}
and the fact that
k_{\alpha}
is diagonalized by
the unitary map
F_{\alpha}:L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})\to L^{2}(I^{\alpha};L^{2}(C_{a}))
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}(F_{\alpha}u)(\lambda,\omega)&=(2\pi)^{-n_{a}/2}2^{-1/2}\lambda_{\alpha}^{(n_{a}-2)/4}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{1/2}_{\alpha}\omega\cdot x_{a}}u(x_{a})\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{a},\\
&\quad\quad\text{ where }n_{a}=\dim\mathbf{X}_{a}\text{ \,and\, }\lambda_{\alpha}=\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha},\end{split}

(2.26)

we can write

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{\beta\alpha}:=F_{\beta}(W_{\beta}^{+})^{*}W_{\alpha}^{-}F_{\alpha}^{-1}=\int^{\oplus}_{I_{\beta\alpha}}S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\quad\text{with }I_{\beta\alpha}=I^{\beta}\cap I^{\alpha}.

The fiber operator
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b}))
is a priori defined only for
a.e.
\lambda\in I_{\beta\alpha}
. It is the

\beta\alpha
-entry of the scattering matrix
.

Similarly the restrictions of the maps
F_{\alpha}(W^{\pm}_{\alpha})^{*}

have
strong almost everywhere interpretations, meaning more precisely

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(W^{\pm}_{\alpha})^{*}f=\int^{\oplus}_{I^{\alpha}}\big(F_{\alpha}(W^{\pm}_{\alpha})^{*}f\big)(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;\quad f\in{\mathcal{H}}.

When applied to
f\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
the following assertion holds.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
denote the
reflection operator on
L^{2}(C_{a})
,
({\mathcal{R}}_{a}g)(\omega)=g(-\omega)
, and let

\displaystyle c_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi(n_{a}-3)/4}\pi^{-1/2}\lambda_{\alpha}^{1/4},\quad\lambda>\lambda^{\alpha}.

Let for
\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\alpha}^{+}(\lambda)=c_{\alpha}^{+}(\lambda)&Q^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}),L^{2}(C_{a})),\\
\Gamma_{\alpha}^{-}(\lambda)=c_{\alpha}^{-}(\lambda){\mathcal{R}}_{a}&Q^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}),L^{2}(C_{a})).\end{split}

(2.27)

The operator
\Gamma_{\alpha}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}
is the

\alpha
-entry of the future wave matrix
, while
\Gamma_{\alpha}^{-}(\lambda)^{*}
is the

\alpha
-entry of the past wave matrix
. This language is
motivated by the following result.

Theorem 2.10
.

For any channel

(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
obeying Condition

2.3
and any
f\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\big(F_{\alpha}(W^{\pm}_{\alpha})^{*}f\big)(\lambda)=\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)f\quad\text{for a.e.
}\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}.

(2.28)

In particular for any
f\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})
the restrictions
\big(F_{\alpha}(W^{\pm}_{\alpha})^{*}f\big)(\cdot)\in L^{2}(C_{a})
are
weakly continuous in
{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
2.7
it suffices to
check (
2.28
), in fact only the formulas

\displaystyle\langle f,W^{\pm}_{\alpha}F_{\alpha}^{*}g\rangle=\int\big\langle\Gamma_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)f,g(\lambda,\cdot)\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;\quad g\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\times C_{a}^{\prime}).

(2.29)

Note that the latter space is dense in
L^{2}(I^{\alpha},L^{2}(C_{a}))
.
We can mimic the proof of
[
IS3
, Lemma 3.8]
using as input

[
II
, Lemma 6.4]
, considering as in
[
IS3
]
only the plus
case and (without loss of generality) only
f\in L^{2}_{1}(\mathbf{X})
.
With reference to the proof of

[
IS3
, Lemma 3.8]
we first commute
H
and a
factor
\chi_{m}
appearing there, here taken as

\chi_{m}=\chi_{-}(\lvert x\rvert/2^{m})
, and as a result then obtain for
any
g
as in (
2.29
) the expression

\chi_{m}(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g(\lambda,\cdot)]
. Noting that
(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g(\lambda,\cdot)]\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X})
, cf. (
2.14
), we can take
the parameter
\epsilon>0
appearing in the proof of

[
IS3
, Lemma 3.8]
to
0
and
m\to\infty
in the written
order (parallel to
[
IS3
]
). By using the resulting formula
in combination with (
2.13b
) we conclude (
2.29
) (in the plus case).
∎

Under the condition of asymptotic completeness and with
Condition

2.3
fulfilled for all channels (or rather for
all open channels) we can use Theorem
2.10
and
write, arguing here formally,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\beta}^{+}(\lambda)=\sum_{\alpha}\,S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\Gamma_{\alpha}^{-}(\lambda).

Applying this formula to
f=(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]
leads with Lemma
2.6

and (
2.22b
) to the identification of

\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
and
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
.
However we will do the identification (stated precisely below) under
weaker conditions.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
g\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\times C_{a}^{\prime})
, where

\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
is any channel obeying
Condition
2.3
. Then the map

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}\ni\lambda\to f_{\lambda,g}:=(H-\lambda)J_{\alpha}v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g(\lambda,\cdot)]

is a
continuous
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})
-valued function for some
s>1/2

(depending on
\alpha
and
\mu
), and

\displaystyle\wvHlim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\int\big(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\big)f^{+}_{\lambda,g}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\int P^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g(\lambda,\cdot)]\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

(2.30)

(Here the integrand on the right-hand side is an

L^{2}_{-t}(\mathbf{X})
-valued function for any
t>1/2
.)

Proof.

I
.  Thanks to (
2.17
) and the known regularity of
the function
K_{a}
the map
\lambda\to f_{\lambda,g}
is
checked to be a
continuous
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})
-valued function for some
s>1/2
. The
assertion appears already in the proof
of Lemma
2.7
.

II
.  It remains to prove
(
2.30
).
Write
\delta_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\big(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\big)\geq 0
and note the familiar

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\langle\varphi,\delta_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\varphi\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2};\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}.

By the support properties of
g
, (
2.7a
), Step I and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it follows
that the
{\mathcal{H}}
-valued integrals to the left in
(
2.30
) are
uniformly bounded in
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.

III
.  Taking any
f\in L^{2}_{t}(\mathbf{X})
,
t>1/2
, we compute, using in the second step Lemma

2.6

1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\int\big\langle f,\big(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\big)f^{+}_{\lambda,g}\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int\big\langle f,\big(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)\big)f^{+}_{\lambda,g}\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int\big\langle f,J_{\alpha}v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g(\lambda,\cdot)]-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)f^{+}_{\lambda,g}\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int\big\langle f,P^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g(\lambda,\cdot)]\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

We conclude (
2.30
) by this computation, Step II
and the Riesz theorem.
∎

Theorem 2.12
.

Let any two channels
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
and
\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})
both fulfilling Condition
2.3
be
given (allowing the case
\alpha=\beta
). Then

\displaystyle S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\pi(n_{a}+n_{b}-2)/4}\lambda_{\alpha}^{1/4}\lambda_{\beta}^{-1/4}\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda){\mathcal{R}}_{a};\quad\lambda\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathcal{E}}^{\beta}.

(2.31)

In particular the map
{\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathcal{E}}^{\beta}\ni\lambda\to S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b})\big)
is
weakly continuous.

Proof.

The second assertion
follows from (
2.31
) and Lemma
2.8
.
To obtain (
2.31
) let
g_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{E}}^{\alpha}\times C_{a}^{\prime})
and
g_{\beta}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{E}}^{\beta}\times C_{b}^{\prime})
(taking
g_{\alpha}=g_{\beta}
if
\alpha=\beta
). We compute using Lemma

2.11
and (
2.13b
)

\displaystyle\left\langle\int P^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{\beta}(\lambda,\cdot)]\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,\int P^{-}_{\lambda^{\prime},\alpha}[g_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime},\cdot)]\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\int\Big\langle\big(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\big)f^{+}_{\lambda,g_{\beta}},\int P^{-}_{\lambda^{\prime},\alpha}[g_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime},\cdot)]\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime}\Big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\int\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\int\big\langle f^{+}_{\lambda,g_{\beta}},\tfrac{-2\mathrm{i}\epsilon}{(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda)^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}P^{-}_{\lambda^{\prime},\alpha}[g_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime},\cdot)]\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-2\pi\mathrm{i}\int\,\big\langle f^{+}_{\lambda,g_{\beta}},P^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g_{\alpha}(\lambda,\cdot)]\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=4\pi\int\,\lambda_{\alpha}^{1/2}\big\langle g_{\beta}(\lambda,\cdot),Q^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}\big(P^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g_{\alpha}(\lambda,\cdot)]\big)\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=4\pi\int\,\lambda_{\alpha}^{1/2}\big\langle g_{\beta}(\lambda,\cdot),\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g_{\alpha}(\lambda,\cdot)\big\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

In the third step we used that for any
t>1/2
the map
\lambda^{\prime}\to P^{+}_{\lambda^{\prime},\alpha}[g_{\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime},\cdot)]\in L^{2}_{-t}(\mathbf{X})

is continuous. The formula (
2.31
) follows from this
computation, Lemma

2.7
and Theorem

2.10
.
∎

3.
Wave and scattering matrices for the one-body
problem

In this section we collect various results from Section

2
specialized to the one-body case and add a few more
one-body results. Apart from this material and Subsection
2.1
the other subsections of Section

2
will not be used in the rest part of the paper.

For any
a\neq a_{\max}
we consider with the one-body potential

{\breve{I}}_{a}={\breve{I}}_{a}(x_{a})
from
(
2.8
) (there with
\breve{\mu}
replaced by
\mu
, for convenience)

\displaystyle{\breve{h}}_{a}=p^{2}_{a}+{\breve{I}}_{a},\quad{\breve{H}}_{a}=H^{a}\otimes I+I\otimes{\breve{h}}_{a}\,\text{ \,and\, }\,H_{a}=H^{a}\otimes I+I\otimes p^{2}_{a}.

(3.1)

Using (
2.23
) with
H
replaced by

{\breve{H}}_{a}
we obtain

\displaystyle\breve{W}_{\alpha}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}t{\breve{H}}_{a}}J_{\alpha}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}(S_{a}^{\pm}(p_{a},t)+\lambda^{\alpha}t)}=J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm},

(3.2)

where

\displaystyle\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}t{\breve{h}}_{a}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}S_{a}^{\pm}(p_{a},t)}.

(3.3)

Note that
\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}
is a one-body wave
operator and that the theory of Section
2
applies (in a
simplified form).
It is well-known (given the property (
2.8
)) that the scattering operator
\breve{s}_{a}=(\breve{w}_{a}^{+})^{*}\breve{w}_{a}^{-}
is a unitary operator on

L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
. Whence in that case

\displaystyle(0,\infty)\ni\lambda\to{\breve{s}}_{a}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a})\big)\text{ is
strongly continuous.}

(3.4)

More importantly for this paper we can introduce operators
{\gamma}_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)
as in (
2.27
), using obviously
modified notation
for the one-body case,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{a}^{+}(\lambda)=c_{a}^{+}(\lambda)&{\breve{q}}^{+}_{\lambda,a}{\breve{r}}_{a}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a}),L^{2}(C_{a})),\\
\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda)=c_{a}^{-}(\lambda){\mathcal{R}}_{a}&{\breve{q}}^{-}_{\lambda,a}{\breve{r}}_{a}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a}),L^{2}(C_{a})).\end{split}

(3.5)

The operators
\gamma_{a}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}
and
\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda)^{*}
, related to (
3.3
) as in Theorem

2.10
, are the

future
and past wave matrices
at
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
,
respectively. They are strongly weak*-continuous as functions of
\lambda
. In
the weaker topology of
{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbf{X}_{a})\big)
,

s>1/2
,
\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\cdot)^{*}
are strongly continuous. The above
assertions follow from the one-body version of
Lemma
2.7
.

We recall the familar formulas, see for example
[
IS2
, Lemma 3.5]

(and its proof),

\displaystyle{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)}^{*}\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\delta\big({\breve{h}}_{a}-\lambda\big)=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\big({\breve{r}}_{a}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)-{\breve{r}}_{a}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)\big);\quad\lambda>0.

(3.6)

In combination with the asserted weak*-continuity it
follows (as for
[
IS2
, Theorem 1.4]
) that
\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\cdot)
are strongly
continuous in
{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{X}_{a}),L^{2}(C_{a})\big)
. We shall only use the latter property for the free case
(i.e. with
{\breve{I}}_{a}=0
, see Appendix
C
), in which
case we will use the notation

\gamma_{a,0}(\lambda)
for
\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)
(the two operators
corresponding to different signs coincide in that case).

Introducing

\displaystyle G_{a}:={\mathcal{G}}_{a}\cdot p_{a}\text{ \,and\, }{\mathcal{G}}_{a}={\mathcal{G}}_{a}(x_{a}):=\chi_{+}(4\lvert x_{a}\rvert)\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-1/2}\big(I-\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-2}\lvert x_{a}\rangle\langle x_{a}\rvert\big),

(3.7a)

we have

\displaystyle G_{a}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)}^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})^{n_{a}}\big)\text{ with
a locally uniform bound in }\lambda>0,

(3.7b)

and in fact

\displaystyle G_{a}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\cdot)}^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})^{n_{a}}\big)\text{ are
strongly continuous}.

(3.7c)

As for (
3.7b
) it is thanks to (
3.6
) and ‘the

TT^{*}
argument’ a consequence of the resolvent bound

[
Ya1
, Theorem 3.9]
. Alternatively (
3.7b
) can
easily be derived by ‘the

T^{*}T
argument’ given in Step I of the proof of Lemma

9.1
. As for
(
3.7c
) the result follows readily from an application of (
3.7b
),
Lemma
2.7
and

[
IS1
, Theorem 1.10]
. For comparison we remark that
(
3.7c
) for the free Laplacian appears in
[
Ya1
]
and
the proof there applies
a scaling argument.

4.
Stationary channel modifiers
and a main result

We assume from this point of the paper Condition

2.1
with
\mu\in(\sqrt{3}-1,1)
. Asymptotic completeness is then a known fact,

[
De
]
. In particular the wave operators
W_{\alpha}^{\pm}
from
(
2.23
) exist. Henceforth Condition

2.3
is not used.
We will in the present section explain our overall scheme for
proving weak
continuity of any entry of the scattering matrix. This result is
stated as Theorem
4.1
. Most of the needed notation for its
proof will be fixed in this section.

We shall consider a ‘sufficiently small’ open interval
\Lambda
containing any
arbitrarily fixed

\lambda_{0}
obeying

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}:=(\min{\mathcal{T}}(H),\infty)\setminus\big(\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H)\cup{\mathcal{T}}(H)\big).

Only the scattering matrix in an open interval
I_{0}\ni\lambda_{0}
,
\bar{I}_{0}\subseteq\Lambda
, needs
consideration (by partitioning of unity).

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
taking values in
[0,1]
we write

f_{2}\prec f_{1}
if
f_{1}=1
in a neighbourhood of
\operatorname{supp}f_{2}
.
We consider
real
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda)
with
f_{2}\prec f_{1}
and such
that
f_{2}=1
in
\bar{I}_{0}
. (The smallness of
\Lambda
will depend on Mourre estimates at
\lambda_{0}
, see
Subsection
6.1
.)

For a given channel
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
let

\displaystyle{\breve{J}}^{\pm}_{\alpha}=N^{a}_{\pm}(I\otimes\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm})J_{\alpha}\text{ \,and\, }\tilde{J}_{\alpha}^{\pm}=f_{1}(H)M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}(I\otimes\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm})J_{\alpha},

where
\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}
are given by
(
3.3
), and
N^{a}_{\pm}
and
M_{a}
are symmetric operators
specified below. The latter
operator
M_{a}
is
given as in
[
Ya1
, (5.1)]
(with changed notation
M^{(a)}\to M_{a}
, and
recalled in Section
5
), in
particular
M_{a}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1},{\mathcal{H}})
, and

N^{a}_{\pm}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
will be constructed such that
(recalling
k_{\alpha}=p_{a}^{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}
)

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}^{\pm}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tk_{\alpha}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}{\breve{J}}^{\pm}_{\alpha}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tk_{\alpha}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha}),

(4.1a)

By the form of
{\breve{J}}^{\pm}_{\alpha}
this amounts to the property

\displaystyle\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}\big(I-N^{a}_{\pm}\big)J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tk_{\alpha}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})=0.

(4.1b)

Recalling the operators in (
3.1
) we introduce in addition

{\breve{h}}_{\alpha}={\breve{h}}_{a}+\lambda^{\alpha}
and then (abbreviating
I\otimes f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})=f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})
)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}=f_{1}(H)M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a}).

We call the operators
\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}

stationary channel modifiers
.
Provided the two limits

\displaystyle\Omega^{\pm}_{\alpha}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t{\breve{H}}_{a}}\text{ exist},

(4.2)

the limits

\displaystyle\begin{split}\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\tilde{J}_{\alpha}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tk_{\alpha}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t{\breve{H}}_{a}}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\text{ exist},\end{split}

(4.3a)

and denoting them by
\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\pm}
, respectively, obviously

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\pm}=\Omega^{\pm}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}f_{2}(k_{\alpha}).

(4.3b)

For
a\neq a_{\min}
the operators
N^{a}_{\pm}
are of
the form (one for the upper sign plus and one for the lower sign minus)

\displaystyle\begin{split}N^{a}_{\pm}&=A_{1}A_{2}^{a}A_{3}^{a}A_{2}^{a}A_{1};\\
A_{1}&=A_{1\pm}=\chi_{+}(\pm B/\epsilon_{0}),\\
A_{2}^{a}&=\chi_{-}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big),\\
A_{3}^{a}&=A^{a}_{3\pm}=\chi_{-}\big(\pm r^{\rho_{1}/2}B_{\delta}^{a}r^{\rho_{1}/2}\big),\end{split}

(4.4)

where
\epsilon_{0}>0
is sufficiently small
(determined by Mourre estimates at
\lambda_{0}
, see
Subsection
6.1
),

\displaystyle 1-\mu<\rho_{2}<\rho_{1}<1-\delta\text{ with }\delta\in[2/(2+\mu),\mu),

(4.5)

and

r,B,r_{\delta}^{a}
and
B_{\delta}^{a}
are operators constructed by quantities from

[
De
]
(
r
and
r_{\delta}^{a}
are multiplication operators while

B
and
B_{\delta}^{a}
are corresponding Graf vector field type constructions).
For
a=a_{\min}
we take
N^{a}_{\pm}=A_{1}^{2}=A_{1\pm}^{2}
.

More precisely

r
is a function on
\mathbf{X}
, which apart from a trivial rescaling (to assure
Mourre estimates for the Graf
vector field
\nabla r^{2}/2
) is given by the function
r
from
[
De
]
. It partly plays the role as a
‘stationary time variable’ compared to the usage of the real time
parameter in
[
De
]
. (It should not be mixed up with the function

\lvert x\rvert
.) The operator
B:=2\operatorname{Re}(p\cdot\nabla r)
. Let
r^{a}
be the same
function now constructed on
\mathbf{X}^{a}
rather than on
\mathbf{X}
. Let then
r_{\delta}^{a}=r^{\delta}r^{a}(x^{a}/r^{\delta})
and

B_{\delta}^{a}=2\operatorname{Re}\big(p^{a}\cdot(\nabla r^{a})\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)\big)
.
We give further elaboration of the above quantities in Section
6
.

We verify (
4.2
) and (
4.1b
) in Subsection

8.2
and Appendix
B
, respectively. Moreover by
(
4.1b
) and stationary
phase analysis we may rewrite (
4.3a
) and
(
4.3b
) as

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha}^{\pm}=\slim_{t\to\pm\infty}{{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t{\breve{H}}_{a}}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\big(m_{\alpha}^{\pm}\big)^{2}=W_{\alpha}^{\pm}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\big(m_{\alpha}^{\pm}\big)^{2},

(4.6a)

where
{m_{\alpha}^{\pm}}
(after conjugation by the Fourier
transform
F_{\alpha}
, see (
2.26
)) act as multiplication operators on

L^{2}\big(I^{\alpha};L^{2}(C_{a})\big)
(with multiplicative
fiber operators). More precisely

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}^{\pm}F^{-1}_{\alpha}=\int^{\oplus}_{I^{\alpha}}\pm 2\lambda_{\alpha}^{1/2}m_{a}(\hat{\xi}_{a})\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\pm 2\lambda_{\alpha}^{1/2}m_{a}(\pm\hat{\xi}_{a});\quad\hat{\xi}_{a}=\xi_{a}/\lvert\xi_{a}\rvert\in C_{a}

(4.6b)

and
m_{a}
is the function used in (
5.1
)
specifying the operator
M_{a}
. The verification of
(
4.6a
) and (
4.6b
) is given in Appendix
B
.

We conclude the following representation of any
scattering matrix entry
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
(assuming

\lambda^{\alpha},\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}
),

\displaystyle 16\lambda_{\beta}\lambda_{\alpha}f^{2}_{2}(\lambda)m_{b}(\hat{\xi}_{b})^{2}S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)m_{a}(-\hat{\xi}_{a})^{2}=\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda),

(4.7a)

where (recalling
I_{\beta\alpha}=I^{\beta}\cap I^{\alpha}=(\max\{\lambda^{\alpha},\lambda^{\beta}\},\infty)
)

\displaystyle F_{\beta}\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}F_{\alpha}^{-1}=\int^{\oplus}_{I_{\beta\alpha}}\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\quad\text{with}\quad\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}=\Big(\widetilde{W}_{\beta}^{+}\Big)^{*}\widetilde{W}^{-}_{\alpha}.

(4.7b)

We shall verify (
4.7b
) for a weakly continuous
operator-valued function
\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
. By (
4.7a
) the operator-valued function

S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
will then also be weakly
continuous on
I_{0}\ni\lambda_{0}
thanks to a freedom in
choosing the functions
m_{b}
and
m_{a}
. The reader may at this point
consult the beginning of Section
10

where these functions are taken explicitly (of course conforming
with Section
5
.)

Since
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{E}}
is arbitrary a partition of unity allow us to
conclude a main
result of the paper, stated as follows. Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta\alpha}:=I_{\beta\alpha}\setminus\big(\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H)\cup{\mathcal{T}}(H)\big)
.

Theorem 4.1
.

For any incoming channel
\alpha
and any outgoing channel
\beta
the map

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta\alpha}\ni\lambda\to S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b})\big)

is a well-defined
weakly continuous map. Within the class of such maps it is uniquely determined by the identity

\displaystyle F_{\beta}S_{\beta\alpha}F_{\alpha}^{-1}=F_{\beta}\big(W_{\beta}^{+}\big)^{*}W_{\alpha}^{-}F_{\alpha}^{-1}=\int^{\oplus}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta\alpha}}S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

Motivated by (
4.2
) and (
4.3b
) it
is convenient to introduce the notation

\displaystyle T^{\pm}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{i}\big(H\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}-\Phi_{\alpha}^{\pm}{\breve{H}}_{a}\big)=\mathrm{i}f_{1}(H)\big(HM_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}-M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}{\breve{H}}_{a}\big)f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a}).

In Section
8
we shall compute it
in detail, using various preliminary calculus considerations from Section
7
, and then show the
existence of the operators
\Omega^{\pm}_{\alpha}
of (
4.2
)
using a variety of ‘weak propagation estimates’. These estimates will
then be
applied to derive a representation formula of
\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
in Section
9
,
and from this formula we finally deduce the weak continuity of
Theorem
4.1
.

We devote Section
10
to further
results that come out as byproducts of our proof of Theorem

4.1
. In particular we show that

S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
is strongly continuous for almost
all energies in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta\alpha}
, and we introduce and show the
strong continuity of the channel wave matrices for all energies in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta\alpha}
.

4.1.
Extended lattice structure of subspaces

For the above constructions we assume that the family of subspaces
\{\mathbf{X}^{c}\}
,
c\in{\mathcal{A}}
, is stable under addition
(the standard assumption) and includes all sets of the form
\mathbf{X}_{c}

(a non-standard assumption). This can be done without loss of
generality by adding to the collection
\{\mathbf{X}^{c}\}\cup\{\mathbf{X}_{c}\}
all sums of
subspaces from the collection, and we can then consider
H
as an

N
-body operator with this new lattice structure, say
in this subsection indexed by

{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{new}}
, simply by taking
V_{d}=0
on all
added subspaces
\mathbf{X}^{d}
. The notions of eigenvalues and thresholds of

H
