are the same as with the original (i.e. ‘old’) lattice
structure, say in this subsection indexed by

{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{old}}
.

However with
{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{new}}
we can also consider the operators
{\breve{H}}_{c}

and
H_{c}
from (
3.1
) as ‘full’
N
-body operators.
More precisely for
{\breve{H}}_{c}
,
c\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{old}}
, we define

V_{c}={\breve{I}}_{c}
on
\mathbf{X}_{c}
and for all
added subspaces
\mathbf{X}^{d}\neq\mathbf{X}_{c}
we take
V_{d}=0
. If
\mathbf{X}^{d}\subseteq\mathbf{X}^{c}

is an old subspace
the potential
V_{d}
is the same as for the old subspace, and for the
remaining
case we take
V_{d}=0
.
For
H_{c}
,
c\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{old}}
, we define

V_{c}=0
on
\mathbf{X}_{c}
.
Otherwise we use
the same definition as for
{\breve{H}}_{c}
.

With these conventions it is easy to check that the set of
thresholds for
H_{c}
,
c\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{old}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
, is
exactly given by

\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{c})\cup{\mathcal{T}}(H^{c})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}(H)
. We shall use this
feature in Subsection
6.1
.

5.
Yafaev’s constructions

Let for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
(below taken sufficiently small) and for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{a}(\epsilon)=\{\lvert x_{a}\rvert>(1-\epsilon)\lvert x\rvert\}.

Note that
\mathbf{X}_{a}\setminus\{0\}\subseteq\mathbf{X}_{a}(\epsilon)
and in fact that
\mathbf{X}_{a}\setminus\{0\}=\cap_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}\,\mathbf{X}_{a}(\epsilon)
.

In
[
Ya3
]
a real function
m_{a}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{X})
,
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, is
constructed, fulfilling the following
properties for any sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
:

(1)

m_{a}(x)
is homogeneous of degree
1
for
\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
and

m_{a}(x)=0
for
\lvert x\rvert\leq 1/2
.

(2)

If
x\in\mathbf{X}_{b}(\epsilon)
and
\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
, then

m_{a}(x)=m_{a}(x_{b})
(i.e.
m_{a}(x)
does not depend on
x^{b}
).

(3)

If
{b\not\leq a}
and
x\in\mathbf{X}_{b}(\epsilon)
, then
m_{a}(x)=0
.

Let for any such function
w_{a}=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}m_{a}
and

\displaystyle M_{a}=2\operatorname{Re}(w_{a}\cdot p)=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{j\leq n}\big((w_{a})_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}+\partial_{x_{j}}(w_{a})_{j}\big);\quad n:=\dim\mathbf{X}.

(5.1)

Let for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}&={\mathbf{X}_{a}}\setminus\cup_{{c\gneq a}}\mathbf{X}_{c}={\mathbf{X}_{a}}\setminus\cup_{{c\not\leq a}}\mathbf{X}_{c},\\
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}(\delta)&=\mathbf{X}_{a}\setminus\cup_{c\gneq a}\overline{\mathbf{X}_{c}(\delta)};\quad\delta>0.\end{split}

(5.2a)

Here and below the overline means topological closure. Note that
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}(\delta)\subseteq\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}
.

We need the following additional information from
[
Ya3
, Lemma 3.5]

on the construction of the functions
m_{a}
, cf. a discussion in
Section
4
:
We can for any given point

z_{a}\in\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}

choose
m_{a}

with the additional property
m_{a}(\hat{z}_{a})\neq 0
;
\hat{z}_{a}:=z_{a}/\lvert z_{a}\rvert
.
More generally,
for any given
\delta>0
and for any sufficiently small
\epsilon>0

we can take
m_{a}
such that

m^{a}(x)=\lvert x_{a}\rvert
for
x\in\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}(\delta)
with

\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
. We shall implement the latter property explicitly
in the beginning of Section
10
,
however for our proof of weak continuity of the fiber operator

\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
of (
4.7b
) it is not
relevant. It is needed only for extracting the continuity of

S_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
in terms of (
4.7a
).

To control the Hessian of the functions

m_{a}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}m_{a}=\nabla^{2}m_{a}
, (or more precisely
p\cdot\nabla^{2}m_{a}\,p
arising in commutator calculations with
(
5.1
)) Yafaev used a family of similar
functions
m
with an additional convexity property. Before recalling
these functions
we introduce more conical subsets of
\mathbf{X}
.

Let for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)=\big(\mathbf{X}\setminus\{0\}\big)\setminus\cup_{{b\not\leq a}}\mathbf{X}_{b}(\epsilon).

(5.2b)

Note, as a motivation, that

\{\lvert x\rvert\geq 1\}\cap\operatorname{supp}m_{a}\subseteq\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)
for any small
\epsilon>0
.

Next we note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)\subseteq\cup_{d\leq a}\,\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{d}.

(5.2c)

In fact we can for any
x\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)
introduce
d(x)\leq a
by
\mathbf{X}_{d(x)}=\cap_{d\leq a,\,x\in\mathbf{X}_{d}}\,\mathbf{X}_{d}
and then check that
x\in\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{d(x)}
: If not,
x\in\mathbf{X}_{c}\cap\mathbf{X}_{d(x)}
for some
c
with
d(x)\lneq c\leq a
and
therefore
\mathbf{X}_{c}=\mathbf{X}_{c}\cap\mathbf{X}_{d(x)}=\mathbf{X}_{d(x)}
, contradicting that

c\neq d(x)
.

Introduce for
\delta>0
the open cone

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}_{d}(\delta)=\mathbf{X}_{d}(\delta)\setminus\cup_{c\gneq d}\,\overline{\mathbf{X}_{c}(3\delta^{1/n})}.

(5.2d)

Thanks to (
5.2c
) we can for any
\delta_{0}>0
write

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)\subseteq\cup_{d\leq a}\cup_{\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]}\,\mathbf{Y}_{d}(\delta).

(5.2e)

By compactness this leads, for any fixed

\epsilon\in(0,1)
, to the existence of
J\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{J}\in(0,\delta_{0}]
and
d_{1},\dots,d_{J}\leq a
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)\subseteq\cup_{j\leq J}\,\,\mathbf{Y}_{d_{j}}(\delta_{j}).

(5.2f)

By
[
Ya3
, p. 538]
there exists
\delta_{0}^{\prime}>0
such that for all

\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}^{\prime}]
there exists a real function
m=m_{a_{\max}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{X})
fulfilling the following properties:

(1)

m(x)
is homogeneous of degree
1
for
\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
and

m(x)=0
for
\lvert x\rvert\leq 1/2
.

(2)

If
x\in\mathbf{X}_{b}(\delta)
and
\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
, then

m(x)=m(x_{b})
(i.e.
m(x)
does not depend on
x^{b}
).

(3)

m(x)
is convex in
\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
.

(4)

For all
d\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
there exists
\mu_{d}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle m(x)=\mu_{d}\lvert x_{d}\rvert\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{Y}_{d}(\delta)\text{ with }\lvert x\rvert\geq 1.

Similar to (
5.1
) we introduce for any such function
w=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}m
and

\displaystyle M=M_{a_{\max}}=2\operatorname{Re}(w\cdot p)=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{j\leq n}\big(w_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}+\partial_{x_{j}}w_{j}\big).

(5.3)

To relate and further specify the introduced functions
m_{a}
,
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
and
a=a_{\max}
,
let us first fix the order of construction as follows: Fix
\epsilon>0
conforming with the
construction of the family of functions
m_{a}
fulfilling the
properties

1
–
3
(as well as the non-vanishing condition
discussed above in a separate paragraph) and fix
\delta_{0}^{\prime}>0
conforming with the
construction of the family of functions
m
fulfilling the
properties

1
–
4
. Obviously we can assume
\delta_{0}^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
. Take then
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}^{\prime}
in
(
5.2e
) and (
5.2f
). Next for
each function
m_{a}
,
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, we use (
5.2f
) and
construct functions
m_{j}
fulfilling
1
–
4

with
\delta=\delta_{j}
;
j=1\dots J
.

For each
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
we now choose
a quadratic partition
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{J}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})

subordinate to the covering (
5.2f
), and let

\xi^{+}_{j}(x)=\xi_{j}(\hat{x})\chi_{+}(4\lvert x\rvert)
;
\hat{x}:=x/\lvert x\rvert
. Then
we can write, using the support properties
1
and
3

\displaystyle m_{a}(x)=\Sigma_{j\leq J}\,\,m_{a,j}(x);\quad m_{a,j}(x)=\xi^{+}_{j}(x)^{2}m_{a}(x),

and using
2
and the properties

\mathbf{Y}_{d_{j}}(\delta_{j})\subseteq\mathbf{X}_{d_{j}}(\delta_{j})\subseteq\mathbf{X}_{d_{j}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle m_{a,j}(x)\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)=\xi^{+}_{j}(x)^{2}m_{a}(x_{d_{j}})\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert).

Similarly (using in addition the notation
G_{a}
of (
3.7a
))

\displaystyle\begin{split}&p\cdot\big(\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\nabla^{2}m_{a}(x)\big)p=\Sigma_{j\leq J}\,\,p\cdot\big(\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\xi^{+}_{j}(x)^{2}\nabla^{2}m_{a}(x_{d_{j}})\big)p,\\
&=\Sigma_{j\leq J}\,\,G^{*}_{d_{j}}\big(\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\xi^{+}_{j}(x)^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{j}\big)G_{d_{j}};\quad{\mathcal{G}}_{j}={\mathcal{G}}_{j}(x_{d_{j}})\text{
bounded}.\end{split}

(5.4a)

In turn due to
3
and
4
, the latter applied with

d=d_{j}
(and
\delta=\delta_{j}
),

\displaystyle G^{*}_{d_{j}}\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\xi^{+}_{j}(x)^{2}G_{d_{j}}\leq p\cdot\big(\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\nabla^{2}m(x)\big)p.

(5.4b)

Finally we introduce functions
\xi^{+}_{a}
and
\tilde{\xi}^{+}_{a}
as follows.
First choose any
\xi_{a}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
such that
\xi_{a}=1
on

{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\cap\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)
and
\xi_{a}=0
on

{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\setminus\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon/2)
. Choose then any
\tilde{\xi}_{a}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
using this recipe with
\epsilon

replaced by
\epsilon/2
. Finally let
\xi^{+}_{a}(x)=\xi_{a}(\hat{x})\chi_{+}(4\lvert x\rvert)
and
\tilde{\xi}^{+}_{a}(x)=\tilde{\xi}_{a}(\hat{x})\chi_{+}(8\lvert x\rvert)
, and note that

\tilde{\xi}^{+}_{a}\xi^{+}_{a}=\xi^{+}_{a}
. Then by
1
and
3

\displaystyle M_{a}=M_{a}\xi^{+}_{a}=\xi^{+}_{a}M_{a}=M_{a}\tilde{\xi}^{+}_{a}=\tilde{\xi}^{+}_{a}M_{a},

(5.5)

which in applications will provide ‘free factors’ of

\xi^{+}_{a}
and
\tilde{\xi}^{+}_{a}

where convenient.

6.
Dereziński type constructions

There exists a positive function
r\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{X})
fulfilling the
following properties
[
De
,
AIIS
]
(recall
n=\dim\mathbf{X}
):

(1)

For all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
there
exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\big\lvert\partial^{\gamma}(x\cdot\nabla)^{k}\big(r(x)-\langle x\rangle\big)\big\rvert\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-1}.

(2)

There exists
c>0
such that for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
:

\displaystyle\lvert x^{a}\rvert\leq c\Rightarrow\nabla^{a}r(x)=0

(i.e. if
\lvert x^{a}\rvert\leq c
, then
r(x)
does not depend on
x^{a}
).

(3)

r(x)
is convex.

(4)

For any given non-threshold energy of
H
there
is a Mourre
estimate for
H
with
A:=\operatorname{Re}\big(\nabla r^{2}\cdot p\big)
, or more
precisely with such
A
for a suitably rescaled version of the
function
r

obeying
1
–
3
. (Here and later we slightly abusively use the same
notation
r
for the rescaled
function
Rr(x/R)
with
R\geq 1
taken sufficiently large). See
Subsections
6.1
and

7.3.2
for details.

(5)

The family of functions
\{r^{a}|\,a\in{\mathcal{A}}\}
,
with
r^{a}
being a function on
\mathbf{X}^{a}
obeying

1
–
3
(rather than on
\mathbf{X}
, and with

r^{a_{\min}}
being a positive constant), fulfills the
following self-similar structure.

\displaystyle\forall\epsilon>0\,\exists R^{\prime}\geq 1\,\forall x\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)\cap\{\lvert x\rvert\geq R^{\prime}\}:\quad r^{2}=\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{2}+(r^{a})^{2};

(6.1)

here we use the notation of (
5.2b
),
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{a}(\epsilon)=\big(\mathbf{X}\setminus\{0\}\big)\setminus\cup_{{b\not\leq a}}\mathbf{X}_{b}(\epsilon)
.

It follows from
1
that

\displaystyle\big\lvert\partial^{\gamma}\big(r(x)-\langle x\rangle\big)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-1},

(6.2a)

\displaystyle\big\lvert\partial^{\gamma}\big(x\cdot\nabla r(x)-r(x)\big)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-1},

(6.2b)

\displaystyle\big\lvert\partial^{\gamma}\big(x\cdot(\nabla^{2}r)(x)x\big)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-1},

(6.2c)

cf.
[
De
]
.

For completeness of presentation we note that the bounds of

1
, proven only for
k\leq 2
in
[
De
]
, are equivalent to

\displaystyle\big\lvert\partial^{\gamma}(x\cdot\nabla)^{k}\big(r(x)^{2}-\langle x\rangle^{2}\big)\big\rvert\leq C,

and the latter estimates can be checked using the construction of
[
De
]
.
(They were verified explicitly in
[
Sk1
, Appendix
A]
for the seminal
construction of Graf
[
Gr
]
.) As in
[
De
]
we shall need
(
6.2a
)-(
6.2c
) rather than
1
.

We also remark that a version of (
6.1
) appears as

[
Sk2
, (5.10)]
(proven in detail in the paper using the seminal
construction of Graf). Again one can check the stated assertion
using the construction of
[
De
]
. Although it is conveniently used in
Appendix
A
it is not essential there.

Let
\omega=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}r
and

\displaystyle B=2\operatorname{Re}(\omega\cdot p)=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{j\leq n}\big(\omega_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}+\partial_{x_{j}}\omega_{j}\big).

(6.3a)

For the
self-adjointness of
B
we
refer to Subsection
7.3
. This operator appears in
the definition of the factor
A_{1}
of the operators
N^{a}_{\pm}
of Section
4
.

With
\delta
as in (
4.5
)
we define for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max},a_{\min}\}
, and with

r^{a}
given by
5
and
\omega^{a}=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}r^{a}
,

\displaystyle r_{\delta}^{a}

\displaystyle=r_{\delta}^{a}(x)=r^{\delta}r^{a}(x^{a}/r^{\delta}),

(6.3b)

\displaystyle\quad\omega^{a}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\omega^{a}_{\delta}(x)=\omega^{a}\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big),

(6.3c)

\displaystyle B_{\delta}^{a}

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Re}\big(p^{a}\cdot\omega^{a}_{\delta}\big).

(6.3d)

The quantities of (
6.3a
)–(
6.3d
) are
building blocks of
the factors
A_{2}^{a}
and
A_{3}^{a}
of the operators
N^{a}_{\pm}
and fix them for any choice of
parameters
\rho_{2},\rho_{1}
and
\delta
in (
4.5
). For the
self-adjointness of
B_{\delta}^{a}
and
B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}=r^{\rho_{1}/2}B_{\delta}^{a}r^{\rho_{1}/2}
we
refer to Subsection
7.5
. Recall that
N^{a_{\min}}_{\pm}:=A_{1}^{2}
.

6.1.
Mourre estimates and limiting absorption principles

Parallel to (
2.7a
) we
note that (with the limits taken uniformly in
\lambda
in the norm-topology)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\forall s>1/2\,\forall\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}:\quad f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a}){\breve{R}}_{a}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a}){\breve{R}}_{a}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\,\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s}\big).\end{split}

(6.4)

Considered as operator-valued functions of

\lambda
, these quantities are continuous. These facts follow
from similar (well-known) assertions for the one-body operator

{\breve{h}}_{a}
localized to positive energies.

We claim that for some
\epsilon_{1}>0
(being independent of the
scaling parameter used below)

\displaystyle f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\geq\epsilon_{1}f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})^{2}f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha}).

(6.5a)

Here the commutator
\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]
is given
by its formal expression, see
Subsection
7.3.2
or
[
AIIS
, (2.15)]
, which is a bounded form on
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
.

We can verify (
6.5a
) as follows. Consider

\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda)
with
f_{1}\prec\tilde{f}_{2}\prec\tilde{f}_{1}
. Upon shrinking the support of
\tilde{f}_{2}
(for fixed
\tilde{f}_{1}
)

\displaystyle\lVert\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\big(\tilde{f_{1}}({\breve{H}}_{a})-\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})\big)\rVert\text{
is (arbitrarily) small},

and it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})\geq\epsilon_{2}\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})^{2}.

(6.5b)

At this point we stress that the commutator in
(
6.5a
) is given by its formal expression and note that it can be estimated uniformly in
R\geq 1
. (Recall
that we are going to use the rescaled version
r(x)\to Rr(x/R)
,
R\geq 1
sufficiently big.) The ‘size’ of the support of

\tilde{f}_{2}
may depend on the scaling parameter
R
through an

R
-dependence of
\tilde{f}_{1}
(this dependence consequently also
holds for the ’smaller’ functions
f_{2}
and
f_{1}
, however being an
irrelevant property). The stated smallness follows from the
fact that
\lambda_{0}-\lambda^{\alpha}
is not an eigenvalue of

{\breve{h}}_{a}
(there are no positive eigenvalues) and a compactness argument.

In turn (possibly by taking the scaling parameter large enough, as seen
by the concrete expression of Subsection
7.3.2
for the commutator) it suffices to show

\displaystyle\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})\mathrm{i}[H_{a},r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})\geq\epsilon_{3}\tilde{f_{1}}(H_{a})^{2}.

(6.5c)

Now the Mourre estimate (
6.5c
) essentially follows from

[
AIIS
]
(see the
paragraph below for a comment),
again (if needed) in terms of the rescaled version
r(x)\to Rr(x/R)
,
R\geq 1
sufficiently big.
(As mentioned before we suppress such modification and will use the
same notation
r
for the rescaled version of the function.) Note that
\mathbf{X}_{a}
belongs to the lattice of
subspaces
\{\mathbf{X}^{c}\}
, cf. Subsection
4.1
,
and that we use the fact that

\lambda_{0}
is not a threshold nor an eigenvalue of the
N
-body
Hamiltonian
H_{a}
.

Strictly speaking the conjugate operator appearing
in (
6.5c
) is defined in terms of the vector field

\tilde{\omega}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}r^{2}
associated to the
construction of
[
De
]
while the Mourre
estimate of
[
AIIS
]
(originating from
[
Sk1
]
) is stated in
terms of the vector field from the
seminal paper
[
Gr
]
. However the difference of the two vector
fields is minor, caused only from the fact that different
regularization procedures are used. In particular one can indeed, based
on the construction of
[
De
]
, easily check that the same proof as the one
referred to in
[
AIIS
]
works for
the Mourre estimate (
6.5c
) (with the stated version of the
Graf vector). A similar
remark applies for (
6.7
) stated below.

Next, since (
6.5a
) is established, it is convenient
(and harmless) to change
notation by replacing
f_{1}
in (
6.5a
) by some
\tilde{f}_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda)
with
{f_{1}}\prec\tilde{f_{2}}
,
amounting to

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\geq\epsilon_{1}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})^{2}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha}).

(6.6)

Note also the Mourre estimate used in
[
AIIS
]
, corresponding to
replacing
H_{a}
in (
6.5c
) by
H
,

\displaystyle\tilde{f_{2}}(H)\mathrm{i}[H,r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]\tilde{f_{2}}(H)\geq\epsilon_{4}\tilde{f_{2}}(H)^{2};

(6.7)

again possibly for the rescaled version of
r
only, and
with
\tilde{f}_{2}
given as in (
6.6
). See
Subsection
7.3.2
for a convenient form of
(
6.7
) needed in Section
8
.

We recall
from
[
AIIS
]
that (
6.7
) implies
the following version of (
6.4
) in a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{N}}

of
\operatorname{supp}f_{1}
,
cf. (
2.7a
) and (
2.7b
):

\displaystyle\forall\,s>1/2:\quad R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s}\big)\text{ for }\lambda\in{\mathcal{N}}.

(6.8a)

In any such norm-topology the limits are taken
uniformly on
{\mathcal{N}}
, and for the (stronger) Besov space topology the
limiting operators obey

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\big)\text{ with a uniform bound in }\lambda\in{\mathcal{N}}.

(6.8b)

With (
6.6
)
and (
6.7
) in place we can henceforth consider the scaling
parameter
R\geq 1
as fixed, see Subsection

7.3
for a more general discussion.
In particular the above parameters
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{4}
can be chosen as a
function of the distance to the biggest threshold of
H
below

\lambda
, see Lemma
7.7
.
We may at this point fix the parameter
\epsilon_{0}>0
in the factor
A_{1}
of the operators
N^{a}_{\pm}
in Section
4
by the single requirement
4\epsilon^{2}_{0}<\min\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{4}\}
, however its size will not play any role. Henceforth we
just consider
\epsilon_{0}
as a small positive parameter. (The
freedom of possibly choosing
\epsilon_{0}
smaller will come in
conveniently, although not crucially, in the proof of Lemma

9.1
.) In combination
with the previous constructions (
6.3a
)–(
6.3d
) we have
by now fixed
N^{a}_{\pm}
(for any given
parameters
\rho_{2},\rho_{1}
and
\delta
).

7.
Calculus considerations

To facilitate our treatment of

T^{\pm}_{\alpha}
in Section
8
we recall from

[
AIIS
, Section 2]
a calculus in which the Mourre estimate
(
6.7
) can be implemented. This calculus fits well for
computing
T^{\pm}_{\alpha}
by the usual product rule for commutation.

In Subsection
7.1

we introduce notation frequently used in the later arguments.
Subsection
7.2

concerns the Helffer–Sjöstrand formula, and two applications on
computing commutators are given.
In Section
7.3
we provide the self-adjoint realization of
the operator
B
of (
6.3a
).
We investigate the first commutator
\mathrm{i}[H,B]
in Subsection
7.3.2
,
and the second commutator
\mathrm{i}[\mathrm{i}[H,B],B]
in
Subsection
7.3.3
. This will apply for commutation
with the factor
A_{1}
. Commutation
with the factor
A_{3}^{a}
is similar. This will be treated in Subsection
7.5
. Commutation
with the factors
M_{a}
and
A_{2}^{a}
are easier, to be treated in Subsections
7.4
and
7.6
, respectively.

7.1.
Notation

Let
T
be a linear operator on
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{X})
such that

T,T^{*}:L^{2}_{\infty}\to L^{2}_{\infty}
, and let
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then we
say that
T
is an
operator of order
t
, if
for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
the restriction
T_{|L^{2}_{\infty}}
extends to
an operator
T_{s}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{s-t})
. Alternatively stated,

\displaystyle\|r^{s-t}Tr^{-s}\psi\|\leq C_{s}\|\psi\|\text{ for all }\psi\in L^{2}_{\infty}.

(7.1)

We can (and will) use this with
r
replaced by

r_{R}=r_{R}(x):=Rr(x/R)
for a fixed sufficiently large
R\geq 1
,
cf.
Subsection
6.1
. Slightly abusively we dont change the
notation and use
r
rather than
r_{R}
throughout the section, although in the context of Subsection

7.3.2
the quantity
r_{R}
is needed in our
presentation (and meant). Note (for consistency) that
T_{s}
extends the restriction
T_{|{\mathcal{D}}(T)\cap L^{2}_{s}}
.
If
T
is of order
t
, we write

\displaystyle T={\mathcal{O}}(r^{t}).

(7.2)

Note also that, if
T={\mathcal{O}}(r^{t})
and
S={\mathcal{O}}(r^{s})
, then
T^{*}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{t})

and
TS={\mathcal{O}}(r^{t+s})
.
If

T={\mathcal{O}}(r^{t})
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then we write
T={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-\infty})
.
If
T={\mathcal{O}}(r^{s})
for some
s<t
, we write

T={\mathcal{O}}(r^{t_{-}})
. (This will in Section
8
be
a desirable property with
t=-1
.)

7.2.
Functional calculus

Here we present the Helffer–Sjöstrand formula to represent
functions of self-adjoint operators,
and its application to commutators.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
we denote by
\mathcal{F}^{t}
the set of real
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
obeying

\displaystyle|f^{(k)}(x)|\leq C_{k}\langle x\rangle^{t-k}\text{ for any }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}.

It is known that for any
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
there always exists a
good almost analytic extension

\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})
, meaning more precisely that such function

\tilde{f}
obeys

\tilde{f}_{|\mathbb{R}}=f,\quad|\tilde{f}(z)|\leq C\langle z\rangle^{t},\quad\bigl|(\bar{\partial}\tilde{f})(z)\bigr|\leq C_{k}|\operatorname{Im}z|^{k}\left\langle z\right\rangle^{t-k-1}\ \ \text{for any }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Here one can choose
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{C})

if
f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on
{\mathcal{H}}
, and let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Take an almost analytic extension
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})
of
f

as above.
Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k>t

the operator
f^{(k)}(T)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
is expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{(k)}(T)&=(-1)^{k}k!\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(T-z)^{-k-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)\,\text{ with}\\
&\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)=\pi^{-1}(\bar{\partial}\tilde{f})(z)\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v;\quad z=u+\mathrm{i}v.\end{split}

(7.3)

The expression (
7.3
) for
k=0
is the well-known Helffer–Sjöstrand
formula used extensively in the literature to compute and bound commutators.
In general there are several variations of the definition of a commutator,
and in this paper we do not fix a particular one.
It will be clear from the context
in what sense we will be considering a commutator.
Typically, for symmetric operators
T
and
S
, we first define
\mathrm{i}[T,S]
as the quadratic form

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{i}[T,S]\rangle_{\psi}=2\langle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(ST)\rangle_{\psi}=\mathrm{i}\langle T\psi,S\psi\rangle-\mathrm{i}\langle S\psi,T\psi\rangle\ \ \text{for }\psi\in\mathcal{D}(T)\cap\mathcal{D}(S),

and then extend it to a larger space.

Let us provide an example of a commutator formula derived this way using (
7.3
).

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on
{\mathcal{H}}
,

S
be a symmetric relatively
T
-bounded operator,
and assume that there exists a bounded extension

(|T|+1)^{-\epsilon/2}\bigl(\mathrm{i}[T,S]\bigr)(|T|+1)^{-\epsilon/2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\ \ \text{for some }\epsilon\in[0,2].

Let a
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with
t<1-\epsilon
be given, and let
\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}

be given as in (
7.3
).
Then, as a quadratic form on
\mathcal{D}(f(T))\cap\mathcal{D}(S)
,

\mathrm{i}[f(T),S]=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(T-z)^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{i}[T,S]\bigr)(T-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z),

and it extends to a bounded self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
.

Another example is the commutator
[f(H),r^{s}]
treated below, which is
an example of commutators of functions of entries from the triple of operators
(H,r,B)
.
Other such examples will be discussed in
Lemmas
7.5
and
7.9
.
In Section
8
we will repeatedly use
Lemmas
7.3
,
7.5
and

7.9
–
7.11
.

Lemma 7.3
.

(1)

For any
f\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
the operator
f(H)
is of order
0
.

(2)

Let any
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with
t<1/2
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
be given.
Then
\mathrm{i}[f(H),r^{s}]

has an expression,
as a quadratic form on
\mathcal{D}(f(H))\cap L^{2}_{\max\{0,s\}}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),r^{s}]=-2s\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(r^{s-1}\omega\cdot p)\,(H-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z).

(7.4)

In particular
\mathrm{i}[f(H),r^{s}]
is of order
s-1
.

7.3.
The operator
B
, the Mourre estimate and commutation with
A_{1}

We show that the operator
B
is self-adjoint and examine commutators
with functions of
B
, including the prime example
\mathrm{i}[f(H),A_{1}]
,

f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
real and
A_{1}
given by (
4.4
).

7.3.1.
Self-adjoint realization

We recall the self-adjoint realization of the operator
B
from

[
AIIS
]
and accompanying properties related to
the spaces (
2.3
).

Lemma 7.4
.

The operator
B
defined as (
6.3a
)
is essentially self-adjoint on
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbf{X})
,
and the self-adjoint extension, denoted by
B
again,
satisfies that for some
C>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(B)\supseteq\mathcal{H}^{1},\quad\|B\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\ \ \text{for }\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(7.5a)

In addition,
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB}
for each
t\in\mathbb{R}

naturally restricts/extends
as bounded operators
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\pm k}\to\mathcal{H}^{\pm k}
,
k=1,2
, and
they satisfy

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{\pm k})}<\infty,

(7.5b)

respectively. Moreover, the restriction
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{k})
,
k=1,2
, is
strongly continuous in
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

(1)

For any
F\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
the operator
F(B)
is of order
0
.

(2)

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with
t<1
and

s\in\mathbb{R}
be given.
Then
\mathrm{i}[F(B),r^{s}]

is represented,
as a quadratic form on
\mathcal{D}(F(B))\cap L^{2}_{\max\{0,s\}}
,
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[F(B),r^{s}]=-2s\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(B-z)^{-1}\bigl(\omega^{2}r^{s-1}\bigr)(B-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{F}(z).

In particular
\mathrm{i}[F(B),r^{s}]
is of order
s-1
.

7.3.2.
First commutator with
B
and the Mourre estimate

Here we are going to compute the commutator
\mathrm{i}[H,B]
,
and bound it from below.
We define

\mathrm{i}[H,B]
first as a (bounded) quadratic form on
\mathcal{H}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{i}[H,B]\rangle_{\psi}=2\langle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(BH)\rangle_{\psi}=\mathrm{i}\langle H\psi,B\psi\rangle-\mathrm{i}\langle B\psi,H\psi\rangle\ \ \text{for }\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{2}.

(7.6)

We recall that
\omega:=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}r
and let

\tilde{\omega}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}r^{2},\quad\tilde{h}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{2},\quad h=\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r.

Then
formal
computations suggest that

\displaystyle B=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[H,r],

\displaystyle A:=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}[H,r^{2}]=r^{1/2}Br^{1/2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}A:=\mathrm{i}[H,A]=

\displaystyle 4p\cdot\tilde{h}p-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\Delta^{2}r^{2}\big)-2\tilde{\omega}\cdot(\nabla V),

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}B:=\mathrm{i}[H,B]=

\displaystyle r^{-1/2}\bigr(\mathrm{i}[H,A]-B^{2}\bigr)r^{-1/2}+r^{-2}\omega\cdot h\omega.

Thus we could expect that
\mathrm{i}[H,B]
extends continuously to larger spaces,
and this is justified in the following lemma, which in turn partly
justifies the above computations.

Lemma 7.6
.

Denoting the extension of the quadratic form
{\bf D}B=\mathrm{i}[H,B]
of (
7.6
) by the same notation,
it is expressed as

\displaystyle{\bf D}B

\displaystyle=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\big(H{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}H\big)\ \ \text{in }{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{2},\mathcal{H}^{-1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{1},\mathcal{H}^{-2})}

(7.7a)

and, more explicitly, as

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\bf D}B=r^{-1/2}\big(L-B^{2}\big)r^{-1/2},\end{split}

(7.7b)

where
L\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{2},\mathcal{H}^{-1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{1},\mathcal{H}^{-2})

is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}L&=4p\cdot\tilde{h}p-\tfrac{1}{2}(\Delta^{2}r^{2})+r^{-1}\omega\cdot h\omega\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl(-\tilde{\omega}^{a}\cdot\bigl(\nabla^{a}V^{a}_{\rm lr}\bigr)+\bigl(V^{a}_{\rm sr}\tilde{\omega}^{a}\bigr)\cdot\nabla^{a}-\nabla^{a}\cdot\bigl(V^{a}_{\rm sr}\tilde{\omega}^{a}\bigr)+V^{a}_{\rm sr}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\tilde{\omega}^{a}\Bigr).\end{split}

(7.7c)

Here
\tilde{\omega}^{a}
and
\nabla^{a}
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
denote the projection
onto the internal components of
\tilde{\omega}
and
\nabla
,
respectively.

Note that formally

\displaystyle L=\mathbf{D}A+r^{-1}\omega\cdot h\omega=\mathbf{D}A+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1}).

Whence we may use the following version of the Mourre estimate, cf.
[
AIIS
]
. For

\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathcal{T}}(H)
,
\lambda>\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}:=\min\sigma_{{\mathrm{c}}}(H)=\min\sigma_{{\mathrm{ess}}}(H)
, the notation
d(\lambda)
is used for
the distance to the biggest threshold of
H
below
\lambda
.

Lemma 7.7
.

For any
\lambda\in(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}},\infty)\setminus{\mathcal{T}}(H)
and
\epsilon>0

there exist a
neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of
\lambda
and a compact operator
K
on

{\mathcal{H}}
, such that for all real
f\in C^{\infty}_{{\mathrm{c}}}({\mathcal{U}})

f(H)Lf(H)\geq f(H)\bigl(4d(\lambda)-\epsilon-K\bigr)f(H).

Here, strictly speaking,
r
is meant as the rescaled
version
r_{R}=r_{R}(x):=Rr(x/R)
with
R=R(\lambda,\epsilon)\geq 1
taken
sufficiently large, and also
{\mathcal{U}}
and
K
may depend on
R
.

We will in Section
8
implement Lemma
7.7

in combination with Lemma
7.6
in the following form,
see also (
7.13
).

Corollary 7.8
.

For any
\lambda\in(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}},\infty)\setminus{\mathcal{T}}(H)

there exist
\sigma>0
and a
neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of
\lambda
: For any real

f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathcal{U})
there exists
C>0

such that (as quadratic forms on
\mathcal{H}
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(H)(\mathbf{D}B)f(H)\geq f(H)r^{-1/2}\bigl(\sigma^{2}-B^{2}\bigr)r^{-1/2}f(H)-Cr^{-2}.\end{split}

(7.8)

Here the strict meaning is the bound with
r
replaced with the rescaled
version
r_{R}
with
R\geq 1
taken
sufficiently large (as in Lemma
7.7
, and
{\mathcal{U}}
and
C
may depend on
R
).

Finally we compute and bound commutators of functions of
H
and
B
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}^{t^{\prime}}
with

t<-1/2
and
t^{\prime}<1
, respectively.
Then the commutators
\mathrm{i}[f(H),B]
and
\mathrm{i}[f(H),F(B)]

extend from
\mathcal{D}(B)
and
\mathcal{D}(F(B))
to
bounded quadratic forms
on
\mathcal{H}
,
represented as

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),B]

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}\bigl(\mathbf{D}B\bigr)(H-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)\quad{\text{ \,and\, }}

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),F(B)]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{C}}(B-z)^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{i}[f(H),B]\bigr)(B-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{F}(z),

respectively.
Moreover, with the notation (
7.2
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),B]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})\quad\text{ \,and\, }\quad\mathrm{i}[f(H),F(B)]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1}).

(7.9)

7.3.3.
Second commutator
{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}(\cdot)

Here we provide a realization of the
second commutator
\mathrm{i}[\mathbf{D}B,B]
,
and bound it in some operator space.
Although
one can explicitly compute this second commutator,
this will not be needed.

Note that by Lemmas
7.4
and
7.6
we may consider

{(\mathbf{D}B){\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}(\mathbf{D}B)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{2},\mathcal{H}^{-2}).}

Lemma 7.10
.

There exists the strong limit

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}(H)=\mathrm{i}[\mathbf{D}B,B]

\displaystyle:=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\bigl((\mathbf{D}B)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}(\mathbf{D}B)\bigr)\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{2},\mathcal{H}^{-2}).

Moreover

\displaystyle(H-\mathrm{i})^{-1}{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}(H)(H+\mathrm{i})^{-1}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\mu}).

(7.10)

Finally, as a continuation of Lemmas
7.9
and

7.10
, we consider a second commutator of
B
and a function of
H
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with
t<-1

the second commutator

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}\big(f(H)\big)=\mathrm{i}\bigl[\mathrm{i}[f(H),B],B\bigr]

extends from
\mathcal{D}(B)
to a bounded quadratic form on
\mathcal{H}
,
represented as

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}\big(f(H)\big)

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}(H)(H-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}({\mathbf{D}}B)(H-z)^{-1}({\mathbf{D}}B)(H-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z).

In particular

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B}\big(f(H)\big)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\mu}).

(7.11)

7.3.4.
Commutation with
A_{1}

By combining Lemmas
7.9
–
7.11
we obtain the
following result.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}^{t^{\prime}}
with

t<-1
and
t^{\prime}<1
, respectively. Suppose also that
F^{\prime}\geq 0
with

\sqrt{F^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),F(B)]=\sqrt{F^{\prime}}(B)\big(\mathrm{i}[f(H),B]\big)\sqrt{F^{\prime}}(B)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}}).

In particular for any real functions
f,g
and
{F}
with
F^{\prime}\geq 0
,

f,g,\sqrt{F^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
and given such that
g(\lambda)f(\lambda)=\lambda f(\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f(H)\mathrm{i}[g(H),F(B)]f(H)\\
&\quad=\sqrt{F^{\prime}}(B)f(H)(\mathbf{D}B)f(H)\sqrt{F^{\prime}}(B)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}}).\end{split}

(7.12)

We shall in Section

8
use (
7.12
) in combination with
Corollary
7.8
. Note that with (
7.8
) and with
(
7.12
) for
{F^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
supported close to

0
we can infer the bound

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists\epsilon>0:\quad&f(H)\mathrm{i}[g(H),F(B)]f(H)\\
&\quad\geq\epsilon f(H)\sqrt{F^{\prime}}(B)r^{-1}\sqrt{F^{\prime}}(B)f(H)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}}).\end{split}

(7.13)

7.4.
Commutation with
M_{a}

The operators
B
,
M=M_{a_{\max}}
and
M_{a}
,
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, are all of first order given in
terms of smooth bounded globally Lipschitz vector
fields. Consequently the conclusions of Lemma
7.4
also
hold
for
M_{a}
,
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
. However we shall actually not need the
self-adjointness of the latter (and use their propagators for
example); symmetry will suffice. However we will need to pass
functions of
H
through
M_{a}
. Recalling

w_{a}:=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}m_{a}
we let
w^{b}_{a}=(w_{a})^{b}
for any
a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with

t<-1/2
and
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
.
Then the product
M_{a}f(H)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-0})
, and the commutator
\mathrm{i}[f(H),M_{a}]
extends from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
to
a bounded quadratic form
on
\mathcal{H}
,
represented as

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),M_{a}]

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}\mathrm{i}[H,M_{a}](H-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z);

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[H,M_{a}]

\displaystyle=4p\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}m_{a}\big)p-(\Delta^{2}m_{a})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl(-w_{a}^{b}\cdot\bigl(\nabla^{b}V^{b}_{\rm lr}\bigr)+\bigl(V^{b}_{\rm sr}w_{a}^{b}\bigr)\cdot\nabla^{b}-\nabla^{b}\cdot\bigl(V^{b}_{\rm sr}w_{a}^{b}\bigr)+V^{b}_{\rm sr}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}w_{a}^{b}\Bigr).

In particular
(with the notation (
7.2
))

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),M_{a}]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1}),

(7.14a)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{i}[f(H),M_{a}]-4f^{\prime}(H)p\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}m_{a}\big)p\\
=\mathrm{i}[f(H),M_{a}]&+4\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}p\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}m_{a}\big)p(H-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\mu})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\mu}).\end{split}

(7.14b)

In applications the second term to the left in (
7.14b
)
for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}

will be controlled by (
5.4a
) and (
5.4b
), i.e. by
such term for
a=a_{\max}
.

7.5.
Commutation with
A_{3}^{a}
,
a\neq a_{\min}

Here we examine the operator
A_{3}^{a}
appearing as a factor in
N^{a}_{\pm}
. For convenience we only look at the ‘plus’ case, i.e. we study the
factor
A_{3}^{a}
appearing as a factor in
N^{a}_{+}
. The ‘minus’ case may be treated similarly.

Clearly the operators

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{1}&:=B_{\delta}^{a},\\
B_{2}=B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}&:=r^{\rho_{1}/2}B_{\delta}^{a}r^{\rho_{1}/2}=2\operatorname{Re}\big(p^{a}\cdot r^{\rho_{1}}\omega^{a}_{\delta}\big)\end{split}

(7.15)

are both of first order given in
terms of smooth globally Lipschitz vector
fields. As for
B_{1}
the vector field is bounded as well, and
consequently all of the conclusions of Lemma
7.4
hold
upon replacing
B
by
B_{1}
. The operators
B_{1}
and
B_{2}

are essentially self-adjoint on
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbf{X})
and
the analogue of (
7.5b
) is fulfilled for both of them.

Now

A_{3}^{a}=\chi_{-}(B_{2})
is well-defined and we can record that

A_{3}^{a}\in{\mathcal{O}}(r^{0})
, cf. Lemma
7.5
. From a formula similar
to the one of Lemma
7.5

2
we see that

[A_{3}^{a},r^{s}]
is of order
s-1+\rho_{1}<s
, exemplifying good
commutation properties when commuting with functions of
r
.

As for commutation with
functions of
H
we revisit Subsections

7.3.2
–
7.3.4
except for the part concerning the Mourre
estimate. This is technically the most demanding part of Section
7
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Introducing the quadratic form
{\bf D}_{a}B_{2}=\mathrm{i}[H_{a},B_{2}]
as

\displaystyle{\bf D}_{a}B_{2}

\displaystyle=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\big(H_{a}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB_{2}}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB_{2}}H_{a}\big)\ \ \text{in }{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{2},\mathcal{H}^{-1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{1},\mathcal{H}^{-2})},

(7.16a)

it is given by

\displaystyle{{\bf D}_{a}B_{2}}=r^{\rho_{1}/2}\big(T_{1}+\cdots+T_{5}\big)r^{\rho_{1}/2};

(7.16b)

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=4p^{a}\cdot r^{-\delta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)p^{a},

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\delta\operatorname{Re}\big(p^{a}\cdot r^{-\delta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)x^{a}r^{-1}B\big),

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}\operatorname{Re}\big(Br^{-1}B_{\delta}^{a}\big),

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{b\leq a}\Bigl(-(\omega^{a}_{\delta})^{b}\cdot\bigl(\nabla^{b}V^{b}_{\rm lr}\bigr)+\bigl(V^{b}_{\rm sr}(\omega^{a}_{\delta})^{b}\bigr)\cdot\nabla^{b}-\nabla^{b}\cdot\bigl(V^{b}_{\rm sr}(\omega^{a}_{\delta})^{b}\bigr)+V^{b}_{\rm sr}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(\omega^{a}_{\delta})^{b}\Bigr),

\displaystyle T_{5}

\displaystyle=T_{5}(x)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-3\delta})\text{ explicitly given by (summing over
repeated indices):}

\displaystyle-\partial^{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\partial^{a}_{j}\,\{r^{-\delta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)_{ij}\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)\}-\delta(\Delta r)\mathop{\mathrm{div}^{a}}\{r^{-1}F^{a}\}-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}^{a}\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)\partial^{a}_{j}\{\lvert\omega\rvert^{2}r^{-2}\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\partial_{i}\partial^{a}_{j}\,M_{ij}-\delta r^{-1}\{\partial^{a}_{j}(\Delta r)\}F_{j}^{a};

\displaystyle F^{a}=F^{a}(x)=\{\big({y^{a}\cdot\nabla_{y^{a}}\omega^{a}}\big)(y^{a})\}_{|y^{a}=x^{a}/r^{\delta}},\quad M_{ij}=M_{ij}(x)=\delta r^{-1}\omega_{i}F^{a}_{j}.

Remarks
.

Although the stated exact expression for
T_{5}
will
not be relevant for us, we remark that
the fifth term of the expression is on the form

{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-2})
(and no better), however it cancels with a term
from expanding the fourth term using the Leibniz rule for
differentiation. The first term has the stated order

{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-3\delta})
, but the sum of all the others has a better
order. The stated order
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-3\delta})
will by far be sufficient for our treatment of
T_{5}
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}^{t^{\prime}}
with

t<-1/2
and
t^{\prime}<1
, respectively.
Then the commutators
\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),B_{2}]
and
\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]
extend from
\mathcal{D}(B_{2})
and
\mathcal{D}(F(B_{2}))
to
bounded quadratic forms
on
\mathcal{H}
,
represented as

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),B_{2}]

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}\bigl(\mathbf{D}_{a}B_{2}\bigr)(H_{a}-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)\quad{\text{ \,and\, }}

(7.17a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{C}}(B_{2}-z)^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),B_{2}]\bigr)(B_{2}-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{F}(z),

(7.17b)

respectively.
Moreover

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),B_{2}]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta})\quad\text{ \,and\, }\quad\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta}).

(7.18)

Ideally we would like the right-hand sides of
(
7.18
) to be on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
, which is not
doable. However there is the following partial result of this
type: Let us split

\displaystyle{{\bf D}_{a}B_{2}}

\displaystyle=D_{1}+D_{2};

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=r^{\rho_{1}/2}\big(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}\big)r^{\rho_{1}/2}

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=r^{\rho_{1}/2}\big(T_{4}+T_{5}\big)r^{\rho_{1}/2}.

From the the property
2
of Section
6
(with
r
replaced by
r^{a}
) and the relations

(1+\mu)\delta-\rho_{1}>1

and
3\delta-\rho_{1}>1
it follows that

\displaystyle-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{2}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}}),

and therefore we conclude by plugging into
(
7.17a
) and (
7.17b
) that the contribution from
D_{2}
to

\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]
is on the desired form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
.
As for the contribution from
D_{1}
to

\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]
we write

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]-{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}\big(T^{(1)}F^{\prime}(B_{2})\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}}(B_{2}-z)^{-2}\mathrm{{\operatorname{ad}}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(1)})(B_{2}-z)^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{F}(z);

\displaystyle T^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta}).

The second term to the right is
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
, which follows from the
following more complicated version of Lemma
7.11

involving

\displaystyle T^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(D_{1})(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{2(\rho_{1}-\delta)}).

For convenience we write in the remaining part of
the section

T\simeq T^{\prime}
for any
given operators

T,T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
, if
T-T^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
T^{(1)}
and
T^{(2)}
be given as above for
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
with

t<-1/2
. Then there exists the strong limits

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}[T^{(j)},B_{2}]:=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\bigl(T^{(j)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tB_{2}}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB_{2}}T^{(j)}\bigr)\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H});\,\quad j=1,2.

Moreover

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}\big(T^{(1)}\big)

\displaystyle=T^{(2)}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}}),

and for the above second term we can substitute

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(1)})

\displaystyle={\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(2)})+\mathrm{i}\big({\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(1)})-T^{(2)}\big)B_{2}-\mathrm{i}B_{2}\big({\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(1)})-T^{(2)}\big)

\displaystyle={\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(2)})+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})B_{2}+B_{2}{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})B_{2}+B_{2}{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}}).

Proof.

We compute (for the very last assertion)

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(T^{(2)})=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(D_{1})(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+3\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(D_{1})(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)

\displaystyle+3\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{\mathrm{i}B_{2}}(D_{1})(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)

\displaystyle-6\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}D_{1}(H_{a}-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{f}(z)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(r^{3(\rho_{1}-\delta)})\simeq 0.

∎

By commutation – of the same sort, continuing the above
calculations – we conclude the
following result.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{t}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}^{t^{\prime}}
with

t<-1/2
and
t^{\prime}<1
, respectively. Suppose also that
F^{\prime}\leq 0
with

\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]\simeq-\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}(B_{2})\big(\mathrm{i}[f(H_{a}),B_{2}]\big)\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}(B_{2}).

In particular for any real functions
f,g
and
{F}
with
F^{\prime}\leq 0
,

f,g,\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
and given such that
g(\lambda)f(\lambda)=\lambda f(\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f(H_{a})\mathrm{i}[g(H_{a}),F(B_{2})]f(H_{a})\\
&\quad\simeq-\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}(B_{2})f(H_{a})\big(\mathrm{i}[g(H_{a}),B_{2}]\big)f(H_{a})\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}(B_{2})\\
&\quad\simeq-\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}(B_{2})f(H_{a})D_{1}f(H_{a})\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}(B_{2}).\end{split}

(7.19a)

Effectively the right-hand side of (
7.19a
) will
contribute by a negative term.
To see this we first write

\displaystyle T_{1}+T_{2}

\displaystyle=4\big(p^{a}-\tfrac{\delta}{4}B\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}\big)\cdot r^{-\delta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)\big(p^{a}-\tfrac{\delta}{4}\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}B\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\tfrac{\delta^{2}}{4}B\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}\cdot r^{-\delta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}B.

Using (
6.2c
) this leads to

\displaystyle f(H_{a})r^{\rho_{1}/2}\big(T_{1}+T_{2}\big)r^{\rho_{1}/2}f(H_{a})

(7.19b)

\displaystyle=f(H_{a})r^{\rho_{1}/2}\big(4\big(p^{a}-\tfrac{\delta}{4}B\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}\big)\cdot r^{-\delta}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)\big(p^{a}-\tfrac{\delta}{4}\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}B\big)\big)r^{\rho_{1}/2}f(H_{a})

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}+\delta-2}).

Now the first term to the right is positive, and the last term

{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}+\delta-2})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
.

We are only interested in
f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
taking values in
[0,1]
. For convenience let us
henceforth impose this condition.
The contribution from
T_{3}
is of order
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})
(here we use
that
F^{\prime}
is compactly supported), but with
a localization to
B>0
and provided
\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})

we can again find a better lower bound. More
precisely we have the following result for which it remains to be
noted that the
right-hand side of (
7.19c
) is positive.

Lemma 7.18
.

Suppose
\zeta:=\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})
. Then
for any
\epsilon>0
and with
\eta(b):=\sqrt{b}\chi_{+}(b/\epsilon)
and
\zeta_{1}(b):=\sqrt{b}\zeta(b)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\zeta(B_{2})f(H_{a})r^{\rho_{1}/2}T_{3}r^{\rho_{1}/2}f(H_{a})\zeta(B_{2})\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\\
&\simeq\rho_{1}f(H_{a})\eta(B)\zeta_{1}(B_{2})r^{-1}\zeta_{1}(B_{2})\eta(B)f(H_{a}).\end{split}

(7.19c)

Proof.

Pick
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
such that

f\prec\tilde{f}
, and let
\widetilde{B}_{a}=\tilde{f}(H_{a})B\tilde{f}(H_{a})
. Then by commutation

\displaystyle T:=

\displaystyle\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\zeta(B_{2})f(H_{a})r^{\rho_{1}/2}\operatorname{Re}\big(Br^{-1}B_{\delta}^{a}\big)r^{\rho_{1}/2}f(H_{a})\zeta(B_{2})\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)

\displaystyle\simeq f(H_{a})\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\operatorname{Re}\big(\zeta(B_{2})\widetilde{B}_{a}r^{-1}B_{2}\zeta(B_{2})\big)\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)f(H_{a}).

It follows from the first bound of
(
7.18
) and the bound
\tilde{f}{(H_{a})}[B_{2},{B}]\tilde{f}{(H_{a})}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta})
that

\displaystyle[\zeta(B_{2}),\widetilde{B}_{a}]=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(B_{2}-z)^{-1}\mathrm{[}B_{2},\widetilde{B}_{a}](B_{2}-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\zeta}(z)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta}).

(7.20a)

Whence

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\big(\zeta(B_{2})\widetilde{B}_{a}r^{-1}B_{2}\zeta(B_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}\big(\widetilde{B}_{a}\zeta(B_{2})r^{-1}B_{2}\zeta(B_{2})\big)+\operatorname{Re}\big([\zeta(B_{2}),\widetilde{B}_{a}]r^{-1}B_{2}\zeta(B_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}\big(\widetilde{B}_{a}r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\big)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta-1}),

and we infer that

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\simeq f(H_{a})\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\operatorname{Re}\big(\widetilde{B}_{a}r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\big)\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)f(H_{a})

\displaystyle\simeq f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\big(\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon){B}r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\big)f(H_{a}).

Next we write

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\big(\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon){B}r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}\big(\eta^{2}(B)r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\big)+\operatorname{Re}\big(\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon){B}[r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2}),\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)]\big),

and (for the second term)

\displaystyle[r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2}),

\displaystyle\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)]

\displaystyle=[r^{-1},\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)]\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})+r^{-1}[\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2}),\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)]

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-2})\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})+r^{-1}[\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2}),\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)].

Here the first term contributes by a term on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
, so
we are left with

\displaystyle T\simeq f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\big(\eta^{2}(B)

\displaystyle r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\big)f(H_{a})

\displaystyle-f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\Big(\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon){B}r^{-1}[\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon),\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})]\Big)f(H_{a}).

The first term

\displaystyle f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\big(\eta^{2}(B)

\displaystyle r^{-1}\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})\big)f(H_{a})

\displaystyle\simeq f(H_{a})

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\big(\eta(B)\zeta_{1}(B_{2})r^{-1}\zeta_{1}(B_{2})\eta(B)\big)f(H_{a})

\displaystyle+f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\big(\eta(B)r^{-1}[\eta(B),\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})]\big)f(H_{a}),

and here in turn the first term is what we need for
(
7.19c
).

It remains to show that

\displaystyle-f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\Big(\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)

\displaystyle{B}r^{-1}[\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon),\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})]\Big)f(H_{a})

\displaystyle+f(H_{a})\operatorname{Re}\big(\eta(B)r^{-1}[\eta(B),\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})]\big)f(H_{a})\simeq 0.

To deal with these commutators we let

\widetilde{Z}_{a}=\tilde{f}(H_{a})Z\tilde{f}(H_{a})
,
Z=\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})
. By
(
7.9
) it suffices to show that

\displaystyle[\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon),\widetilde{Z}_{a}],\,[\eta(B),\widetilde{Z}_{a}]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{0_{-}}).

(7.20b)

Let us only do the second bound. We use
(
7.9
) and
(
7.18
) computing in the last step exactly as in (
7.20a
)

\displaystyle[\eta(B),\widetilde{Z}_{a}]

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(B-z)^{-1}\mathrm{[}B,\widetilde{Z}_{a}](B-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\eta}(z)

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(B-z)^{-1}\mathrm{[}\widetilde{B}_{a},\zeta^{2}_{1}(B_{2})](B-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\eta}(z)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{0_{-}}).

∎

Remark 7.19
.

The procedure in the
proof of Lemma
7.18
of regularizing by
replacing an operator
T
by

\widetilde{T}_{a}=\tilde{f}(H_{a})T\tilde{f}(H_{a})
before computing a
commutator with
T
by the Helffer–Sjöstrand
formula will be used
frequently in the remaining part of the paper. We are dealing with various
examples of first order operators, most typically
B
,
B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}
and
\xi^{+}_{j}(x)G_{d_{j}}
, that along with functions of
B
and

B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}
at
various points need to be commuted. The technical problem one should
avoid (and possibly would encounter upon ‘blindly’ applying the Helffer–Sjöstrand
formula) is the appearance of certain (presumably unbounded)
commutators without a proper weight. Thus for
example the formal commutator
[B,B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}]
is of first
order but does not seem relatively bounded neither to
B
nor
to
B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}
. In the
proof of Lemma
7.18
the
\widetilde{T}_{a}
-construction cured
this problem providing
H_{a}
-weights. We used ‘free’ factors of

\tilde{f}(H_{a})
at our disposal and the feature that commutation with those was
relatively harmless. We will proceed similarly later (mostly with cases
where
H_{a}
is replaced by
H
), although typically
without giving
the details.

We have computed the commutator (
7.19a
) as a sum of two negative operators plus an
operator on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
. This is with
a localization to
B>0
and for

\sqrt{-F^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})
, which will be conditions met in
our treatment of
A^{a}_{3}
in the ‘plus’ case.

We shall in applications need
commutation with
A^{a}_{3}
, however with
H_{a}
replaced by
H
. The presence of
factors of
M_{a}
enables us to reduce to the case studied
above. Note that thanks to (
5.5
) for any real
{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\big(f(H_{a})-f(H)\big)\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-\mu}),

(7.21)

and note that

{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-\mu}){\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
(the second
factor appears in (
7.18
)).
Alternatively, one can essentially just mimic the above arguments with

H_{a}
replaced by
H
; the additional complication is minor thanks to (
5.5
).

7.6.
Commutation with
A_{2}^{a}
,
a\neq a_{\min}

Recalling

\displaystyle A_{2}^{a}=\chi_{-}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)\text{ with }r_{\delta}^{a}=r^{\delta}r^{a}(x^{a}/r^{\delta}),

we compute for real
{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[f(H),A_{2}^{a}]

\displaystyle=-2\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\big(\chi_{-}^{\prime}(\cdot)\nabla(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a})\cdot p\big)(H-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z)

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(H-z)^{-1}\big(S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}\big)(H-z)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{f}(z);

\displaystyle\quad S_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\big(\chi_{-}^{\prime}(\cdot)r^{(\rho_{2}-1)}B^{a}_{\delta}\big),

\displaystyle\quad S_{2}=(\rho_{2}-1)\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\big(\chi_{-}^{\prime}(\cdot)r^{\rho_{2}-2}r_{\delta}^{a}B\big),

\displaystyle\quad S_{3}=\delta\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\big(\chi_{-}^{\prime}(\cdot)r^{\rho_{2}+\delta-2}\big(r^{-\delta}r_{\delta}^{a}-\omega_{\delta}^{a}\cdot\tfrac{x^{a}}{r^{\delta}}\big)B\big).

Due to (
6.2b
) we can write

\displaystyle S_{3}=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\big({\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{2}+\delta-2})B\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\big({\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})B\big),

and consequently the contribution from
S_{3}
to the
commutator is of order
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
. On the other hand the
contributions from
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are of order
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{2}-1})

and
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})
, respectively, and no better.

Applied to real
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
with

g(\lambda)f(\lambda)=\lambda f(\lambda)
(as we used for
(
7.12
) and (
7.19a
)) we estimate with
\eta(b)=\sqrt{b}\chi_{+}(b/\epsilon)
and for any
\epsilon>0
,
\zeta(b)=-b\chi_{+}(-b)
,

\chi_{1}(s)=-(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}(s)=2\chi_{+}(s)\chi_{+}^{\prime}(s)
,
\chi_{2}(s)=-s(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}(s)=2s\chi_{+}(s)\chi_{+}^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle 2\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\operatorname{Re}\Big(f(H)\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{2}^{a}]f(H)\chi_{-}(B_{2})A_{2}^{a}\Big)\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)

\displaystyle\simeq 2\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\operatorname{Re}\Big(f(H)\big(S_{1}+S_{2}\big)f(H)\chi_{-}(B_{2})A_{2}^{a}\Big)\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)

(7.22)

\displaystyle\simeq-\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)f(H)r^{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}-1)/2}\sqrt{\chi_{1}}(\cdot)B_{2}\chi_{-}(B_{2})\sqrt{\chi_{1}}(\cdot)r^{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}-1)/2}f(H)\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)

\displaystyle\quad+(1-\rho_{2})\eta(B)f(H)\chi^{1/2}_{-}(B_{2})\big(\chi_{1}(\cdot)r^{\rho_{2}-2}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)\chi^{1/2}_{-}(B_{2})f(H)\eta(B)

\displaystyle\simeq\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)f(H)r^{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}-1)/2}\sqrt{\zeta}(B_{2})\chi_{1}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)\sqrt{\zeta}(B_{2})r^{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}-1)/2}f(H)\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)

\displaystyle\quad+(1-\rho_{2})\eta(B)f(H)\chi^{1/2}_{-}(B_{2}){\chi_{2}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)}r^{-1}\chi^{1/2}_{-}(B_{2})f(H)\eta(B).

We commuted repeatedly functions of
B_{2}
and
B
with multiplication
operators (for example
A_{2}^{a}
). Using the fact that
\rho_{1}<1/3
this
is rather harmless (we do not give the details). Note also that the above treatment of
S_{2}
used commutation of
functions of
B
and
\chi_{-}(B_{2})
. This is implemented as explained in Remark

7.19
(with
H_{a}
replaced by

H
).

To the
right of the computation (
7.6
) both terms are positive.

8.
Computation of
T^{\pm}_{\alpha}
and existence of

\Omega^{\pm}_{\alpha}

We pick
\tilde{f_{1}},\tilde{f_{2}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Lambda)
such that
f_{1}\prec\tilde{f}_{2}\prec\tilde{f_{1}}
(cf. Subsection
6.1
) and let
g(\lambda)=\lambda\tilde{f}_{1}(\lambda)
.
This allows us to write

\displaystyle T^{\pm}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{i}f_{1}(H)\Big(g(H)M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}-M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}g({\breve{H}}_{a})\Big)f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a}),

and with this representation we can show that
T^{\pm}_{\alpha}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta})
in the sense of Subsection

7.1
. Furthermore we can show that all
terms in an expansion that are not on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-2\epsilon})

for some
\epsilon>0
tend to have a sign allowing us to prove
non-trivial smoothness estimates for concrete expressions that fail
to be on the favourite form

{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1/2-\epsilon})
. Note that for any
\epsilon>0
the
operators

r^{-1/2-\epsilon}f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})
and
r^{-1/2-\epsilon}f_{1}(H)

are
{\breve{H}}_{a}
- and
H
-smooth in the sense of Kato (see
[
Ka
]
), cf.
(
6.4
) and (
6.8a
).

8.1.
Propagation estimates

For any
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
and any self-adjoint operator
T
on
{\mathcal{H}}
we
let
\psi(t)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}\psi
and denote

\langle T\rangle_{t}=\langle\psi(t),T\psi(t)\rangle
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In the proof of the following lemma we calculate modulo terms
on the form
f_{1}(H){\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})f_{1}(H)
. We will in this subsection
write
T_{1}\simeq_{1}T_{2}
if
T_{1}-T_{2}
has this form (which is slightly
different from the relation used in Subsections
7.5
–
7.6
).
Recall the smoothness bound

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\lVert Q_{0}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C_{\epsilon}\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};\quad Q_{0}=r^{-1/2-\epsilon},\,\epsilon>0.

(8.1)

We will below extensively use the notation

B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}=r^{\rho_{1}/2}B_{\delta}^{a}r^{\rho_{1}/2}
for

a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\setminus\{a_{\min}\}
, cf. (
7.15
). Recall that
the factors
A^{a}_{3}=A^{a}_{3\pm}
of
N^{a}_{\pm}
are given in terms of

B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}
with parameters given as in
(
4.5
). In the list below
1
–
8

there appear respective operators
Q_{1}\dots Q_{8}
(equipped with
additional indices). For
a={a_{\min}}
only
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
enter.
They all have the feature of being
H
-bounded
and (if wanted) we can make them bounded by looking instead at

Q_{1}\tilde{f}_{1}(H)\dots Q_{8}\tilde{f}_{1}(H)
, respectively. None of
these operators appears to be on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1/2)_{-}})
, so the
corresponding bounds do not conform with (
8.1
). On the
other hand some are on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1/2})
, just missing the
applicability of (
8.1
), and the ‘worse’ appearing order in
the list is
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(\rho_{1}-\delta)/2})
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
. The following bounds hold for a constant
C>0

being independent of
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

(1)

For
j\leq J=J(a)
(as in (
5.4a
))

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\big\lVert Q_{1}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};\quad Q_{1}=Q_{1}(a,j)=\xi^{+}_{j}(x)G_{d_{j}}.

(2)

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\big\lVert Q_{2}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};\quad Q_{2}^{\pm}=r^{-1/2}\sqrt{(\chi^{2}_{+})^{\prime}}\Big(\pm B/\epsilon_{0}\Big).

(3)

Let
T_{1}^{a}=\sqrt{-\chi^{\prime}_{-}}\big(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big){\chi_{+}}(\pm B/\epsilon_{0})
and

{\mathcal{H}}^{a}={\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}r^{a}\big)\big(x^{a}/r^{\delta}\big)}
.
Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,

\displaystyle\big\lVert Q_{3}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};

\displaystyle\quad Q_{3}^{\pm}=Q_{3}^{\pm}(a)=2\big({\mathcal{H}}^{a}\big)^{1/2}\big(p^{a}\mp\tfrac{\delta}{4}\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}B\big)\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})r^{(\rho_{1}-\delta)/2}T_{1}^{a}M_{a}.

(4)

Let
\eta(b)=\sqrt{b}\chi_{+}(b/\epsilon_{0})
,
\zeta_{1}(b)=\sqrt{-b\chi^{\prime}_{-}(b)}
and
T_{2}^{a}={\zeta_{1}}\big(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big){\eta}(\pm B)
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,

\displaystyle\big\lVert Q_{4}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};

\displaystyle\quad Q_{4}^{\pm}=Q_{4}^{\pm}(a)=\rho_{1}^{1/2}r^{-1/2}T^{a}_{2}M_{a}.

(5)

Let
T_{1+}^{a}=\sqrt{-\chi^{\prime}_{-}}\big(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big)\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big){\chi_{+}}(\pm B/\epsilon_{0})
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{a}
be given as in
3
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,

\displaystyle\big\lVert Q_{5}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};

\displaystyle\quad Q_{5}^{\pm}=Q_{5}^{\pm}(a)=2\big({\mathcal{H}}^{a}\big)^{1/2}\big(p^{a}\mp\tfrac{\delta}{4}\tfrac{x^{a}}{r}B\big)\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})r^{(\rho_{1}-\delta)/2}T_{1+}^{a}M_{a}.

(6)

Let
\eta(b)=\sqrt{b}\chi_{+}(b/\epsilon_{0})
,
\zeta_{1}(b)=\sqrt{-b\chi^{\prime}_{-}(b)}
(as in
4
) and

T_{2+}^{a}={\zeta_{1}}\big(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big)\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big){\eta}(\pm B)
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,

\displaystyle\big\lVert Q_{6}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};

\displaystyle\quad Q_{6}^{\pm}=Q_{6}^{\pm}(a)=\rho_{1}^{1/2}r^{-1/2}T_{2+}^{a}M_{a}.

(7)

Let
\zeta_{2}(b)=\sqrt{-b\chi_{+}(-b)}
,

T_{3}^{a}=\zeta_{2}\big(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big)\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})\chi_{+}(\pm B/\epsilon_{0})
and

\chi_{1+}(s)=2\chi_{+}(s)\chi_{+}^{\prime}(s)
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,

\displaystyle\big\lVert Q_{7}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};

\displaystyle\quad Q_{7}^{\pm}=Q_{7}^{\pm}(a)={r^{(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}-1)/2}\sqrt{\chi_{1+}}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)}T_{3}^{a}M_{a}.

(8)

Let
\eta(b)=\sqrt{b}\chi_{+}(b/\epsilon_{0})
(as in
4
),

T_{4}^{a}=\chi^{1/2}_{-}\big(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big)\eta(\pm B)
and
\chi_{2+}(s)=2s\chi_{+}(s)\chi_{+}^{\prime}(s)
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,

\displaystyle\big\lVert Q_{8}^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2};

\displaystyle\quad Q_{8}^{\pm}=Q_{8}^{\pm}(a)=(1-\rho_{2})^{1/2}{r^{-1/2}\sqrt{\chi_{2+}}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)}T_{4}^{a}M_{a}.

Proof.

We only consider the upper sign case. The lower sign case assertions can be
demonstrated similarly.

1
:

We choose
m=m_{j}
conforming with (
5.4b
) and let
M=M_{j}
be the
corresponding operator defined in (
5.3
). Applying
(
7.14b
) and (
5.4b
) we can then estimate

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}f_{1}(H)[g(H),M]f_{1}(H)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}4{f_{1}(H)p\cdot\big(\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}m\big)pf_{1}(H)}

\displaystyle\geq 4f_{1}(H)G^{*}_{d_{j}}\chi_{+}(\lvert x\rvert)\xi^{+}_{j}(x)^{2}G_{d_{j}}f_{1}(H).

Let

\displaystyle\Psi_{1,j}=f_{1}(H)M_{j}f_{1}(H)

Since
\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{1,j}
is bounded,
1
follows from
integration of
\tfrac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\langle\Psi_{1}\rangle_{t}=\langle\mathrm{i}[g(H),\Psi_{1}]\rangle_{t}
using the above
estimation and
(
8.1
).

2
:
Let

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}=f_{1}(H){\chi^{2}_{+}}(B/\epsilon_{0})f_{1}(H).

We obtain
2
by integrating
\tfrac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\langle\Psi_{2}\rangle_{t}=\langle\mathrm{i}[g(H),\Psi_{2}]\rangle_{t}
using (
7.13
), (
8.1
)
and the boundedness of
\Psi_{2}
. Here
(
7.13
) is used with
f=\tilde{f}_{2}
. Upon multiplying both
sides of that estimate from the left and from the right by a factor

f_{1}(H)
the second term to the right gets the good form
\simeq_{1}0
.

3
,
4
:

Let
A_{1}=\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon_{0})
,
A_{3}^{a}=\chi_{-}\big(B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}\big)
(as in
(
4.4
) for the ‘plus case’) and

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}=f_{1}(H)M_{a}A_{1}A_{3}^{a}A_{1}M_{a}f_{1}(H).

We obtain
3
and
4
by integrating
\tfrac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\langle\Psi_{3}\rangle_{t}
using
1
,
2
,
(
8.1
),
the boundedness of
\Psi_{3}
and by using (
7.19a
)–(
7.21
)
(as well as the arguments for the latter assertions and the
accompanying comments).
By the product rule for commutation there are several
terms. Let us first treat the contribution
from
\mathrm{i}[g(H),M_{a}]
: We write using
(
7.14b
), (
5.4a
) and Remark
7.19

\displaystyle 2f_{1}(H)\operatorname{Re}\big(\mathrm{i}[g(H),M_{a}]A_{1}A_{3}^{a}A_{1}M_{a}\big)f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}8f_{1}(H)\operatorname{Re}\big(p\cdot\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}m_{a}\big)pA_{1}A_{3}^{a}A_{1}M_{a}\big)f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}8\Sigma_{j\leq J}f_{1}(H)\operatorname{Re}\Big(\,G^{*}_{d_{j}}\xi^{+}_{j}{\mathcal{G}}_{j}\big(\tilde{f}_{1}(H)\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}\tilde{f}_{1}(H)\big)A_{1}A_{3}^{a}A_{1}M_{a}\Big)f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}\Sigma_{j\leq J}\,T_{j};\quad T_{j}={f_{1}(H)\,G^{*}_{d_{j}}\xi^{+}_{j}{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}_{j}}\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}f_{1}(H)}\text{ with }{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}_{j}}\text{ bounded}.

Indeed the last expression arises by commuting the
factor
\tilde{f}_{1}(H)\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}\tilde{f}_{1}(H)
to the right producing an error on the
form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}-\delta-1})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
along the pattern of Remark
7.19
.
Now we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
1
to each term
T_{j}
on the
right-hand side.

As for the contribution
from
\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{1}]
we use (
7.12
), writing

\displaystyle 2f_{1}(H)\operatorname{Re}\big(\mathrm{i}\tilde{f}_{2}(H)M_{a}[g(H),A_{1}]A_{3}^{a}A_{1}M_{a}\tilde{f}_{2}(H)\big)f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}2f_{1}(H)\operatorname{Re}\big(\mathrm{i}M_{a}\tilde{f}_{2}(H)[g(H),A_{1}]\tilde{f}_{2}(H)A_{3}^{a}A_{1}M_{a}\big)f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}f_{1}(H)\sqrt{(\chi^{2}_{+})^{\prime}}\Big(\pm B/\epsilon_{0}\Big){\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})\sqrt{(\chi^{2}_{+})^{\prime}}\Big(\pm B/\epsilon_{0}\Big)f_{1}(H).

The right-hand side is treated by
2
.

Next let us elaborate on how
(
7.19a
)–(
7.21
) are used for treating the contribution
from
\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{3}^{a}]
. We need to compute

\displaystyle f_{1}(H)M_{a}A_{1}\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{3}^{a}]A_{1}M_{a}f_{1}(H).

Modulo a term in
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})
this
quantity is given by

\displaystyle f_{1}(H)\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}M_{a}A_{1}\mathrm{i}[\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}g(H)\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+},A_{3}^{a}]A_{1}M_{a}\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle\simeq_{1}f_{1}(H)M_{a}A_{1}\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})\mathrm{i}[g(H_{a}),A_{3}^{a}]\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})A_{1}M_{a}f_{1}(H).

Note that any derivative of
\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}
will
contribute by an error on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-\infty})
due to
(
5.5
). Then we apply the computations of Subsection
7.5
, and by the same argument as above
(i.e. using that
f_{1}\prec\tilde{f}_{2}
) we
can then remove appearing factors of
\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})
.

5
–
8
:
With
A_{1}
and
A_{3}^{a}
as above we
consider

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}=f_{1}(H)M_{a}A_{1}\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)A_{3}^{a}\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)A_{1}M_{a}f_{1}(H).

We compute and integrate
\tfrac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\langle\Psi_{4}\rangle_{t}
, using again

1
,
2
and (
8.1
) as well as the
boundedness of
\Psi_{4}
. We got from the above computation of
\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{3}^{a}]
effectively negative
main terms. There is an additional contribution, more
precisely from the two appearances of

\mathrm{i}[g(H),\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)]
and this is also
effectively negative (contributing by two additional negative terms), cf. the treatment of

\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{2}^{a}]
in Subsection

7.6
. Since the four non-trivial contributing terms
come out with the same sign we conclude

5
–
8
.
∎

Remarks 8.2
.

(1)

Due to
(
7.21
) the appearing factor of

\tilde{f}_{2}(H_{a})
in
3
,
5
and
7
can be
replaced by
\tilde{f}_{2}(H)
(or by
\tilde{f}_{2}(\breve{H}_{a})
, cf.
2
below). (This was actually implicitly used in
the above proof.) The factor serves as a regularization indeed
making
Q^{\pm}_{3}(a)
,
Q^{\pm}_{5}(a)
and
Q^{\pm}_{7}(a)
bounded relatively to
H

and can (presumably) not be removed. By the same argument one
could make all of the ‘
Q
-operators’ bounded, if preferred. Note
for example that in all cases
Q\tilde{f}_{2}(H)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(\rho_{1}-\delta)/2})

in the precise sense of (
7.2
).

(2)

There are very similar
bounds for
{\breve{H}}_{a}
. So denote
\varphi(t)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t{\breve{H}}_{a}}\varphi
for

\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Using (
6.4
) and
(
6.6
) we may replace
f_{1}(H)\psi(t)
in

1
–
8
by

f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\varphi(t)
and the expression

\lVert\psi\rVert^{2}
to right of the estimates by
\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2}
.
The proof of these
modifications of

1
–
8
are essentially the same. Note that the analogue of (
8.1
) reads

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\lVert Q_{0}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\varphi(t)\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C_{\epsilon}\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2};\quad Q_{0}=r^{-1/2-\epsilon},\,\epsilon>0.

(8.2)

When modified by replacing the factors of
f_{1}(H)
by factors of

f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})
the propagation observables

\Psi_{1,j},j\leq J,\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3}
and
\Psi_{4}
may be denoted
by

\Phi_{1,j},

j\leq J,\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
, respectively (to
be useful in the proof of Lemma

9.1
).

(3)

The structure of the bounds

1
–
8
and the analogous ones mentioned in
2
is given as

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\big\lVert Q^{\pm}f_{1}(H)\psi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert^{2},

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\big\lVert Q^{\pm}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\varphi(t)\big\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2},

respectively, where
Q^{\pm}
may (or may not) depend on

a
and
j\leq J=J(a)
. In addition we also have such bounds with

Q={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1/2)_{-}})
,
cf. (
8.1
) and (
8.2
). We give the
mentioned

Q
’s (including
Q={\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1/2)_{-}})
) some uniform index to distinguish them, say

Q^{\pm}=Q^{\pm}(a,k)
(not specifying
k
). By the Kato smoothness
theory
[
Ka
]
a consequence of the above bounds are the following resolvent
bounds, valid for any combination of the indices,

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{\operatorname{Im}z>0}\lVert(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{-1}Q^{+}(b,l)f_{1}(H)\big(R(z)-R(\bar{z})\big)f_{1}(H)Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}\rVert<\infty.

(8.3a)

In particular, recalling (
2.7a
),
(
6.8a
) and the surrounding discussion, there exist weak limits as
z\to\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
from above and these limits define a weakly continuous
function in

\lambda
. Whence we can record that the limiting operators

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad Q^{+}(b,l))f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)f_{1}(H)Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})\\
&\quad\quad\text{ exist with a weakly continuous
dependence of }\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

(8.3b)

(4)

We do not show the existence of

\displaystyle\wlim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\,Q^{+}(b,l))f_{1}(H)R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)f_{1}(H)Q^{-}(a,k)^{*},

although it is likely doable by mimicking

[
Ya1
, Section 3]
. Up to Subsection
10.3
it is more
convenient
to deal with the delta function only. However in
Subsection
10.3
we need the resolvent bounds

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0}\lVert Q^{\pm}(b,l)f_{1}(H){R(z)}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{H}})}<\infty,

(8.3c)

which follow by combining our commutator
bounds, the Besov space bound (cf. (
2.7b
))
and the proof
of
[
Ya1
, Lemma 3.3]
.

8.2.
Integrability of
T^{\pm}_{\alpha}

We shall show the assertion (
4.2
) in a standard fashion verifying ‘integrability of the
time-derivative’.

Lemma 8.3
.

The operators
\Omega^{\pm}_{\alpha}
are well-defined.

Proof.

The existence of the time-limits will be verified by means of Section

7
, (
8.1
), Lemma
8.1
and
Remarks
8.2

2
, computing and estimating the quantity

\displaystyle T^{\pm}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}f_{1}(H)g(H)M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})-\mathrm{i}f_{1}(H)M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}g({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle={f_{1}(H)\mathrm{i}[g(H),M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}]f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathrm{i}f_{1}(H)M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}\big(g(H)-g({\breve{H}}_{a})\big)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})=:T_{1}+T_{2}.

For convenience we treat only the ‘plus case’.
The calculation is modulo terms on the form

T=f_{1}(H){\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})
. Let us abbreviate this
property as
T\simeq_{2}0
(which is a different relation than the one
used in Subsection
8.1
). Any such ‘error’
can be treated by
the smoothness bounds
(
8.1
) and (
8.2
).

We aim at verifying integrability of
T^{+}_{\alpha}
in the precise
sense stated below as
(
8.6
) (
T^{-}_{\alpha}
can be
treated similarly).
Most of the work is
essentially already done in the proof of Lemma

8.1
. However the
computation and estimation of the term
T_{2}
to the right is different. We claim
that this term
\simeq_{2}0
. To see this we
note that on the support of the factors
A_{2}^{a}
in the definition of

N^{a}_{+}

\displaystyle\langle x^{a}\rangle\leq C{r}^{1-\rho_{2}}.

(8.4)

Next we
use a Taylor formula like the one used in (
2.16a
), however used
differently.
We write and estimate for any
b\not\leq a
, on the support of

\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}
, with (
8.4
) and for
r
large

\displaystyle\big\lvert V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x)-V_{\rm lr}^{b}(x_{a})\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\Big\lvert\int_{0}^{1}\pi^{b}x^{a}\cdot(\nabla V_{\rm lr}^{b})\big(\pi^{b}(x-tx^{a})\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\langle x^{a}\rangle\big(\lvert x^{b}\rvert-C_{2}\lvert x^{a}\rvert\big)^{-(1+\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}{r}^{1-\rho_{2}}\big(\lvert x\rvert-C_{4}{r}^{1-\rho_{2}}\big)^{-(1+\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}{r}^{-\rho_{2}-\mu}.

Similarly, under the same
conditions as above,

\displaystyle\lvert\pi^{b}x_{a}\rvert\geq\lvert\pi^{b}x\rvert-\lvert\pi^{b}x^{a}\rvert\geq c_{1}r\geq c_{2}\lvert x_{a}\rvert;\quad c_{1}>c_{2}>0.

For the case where
I^{\rm sr}_{a}=0
we can use the above bounds and
the fact that
\rho_{2}+\mu>1
to
argue, abbreviating
\chi_{-}\big(\langle x^{a}\rangle{r}^{\rho_{2}-1}/C\big)=\chi_{-}(\cdot)
,

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\simeq_{2}f_{1}(H){\mathcal{O}}(r^{0})\chi_{-}(\cdot)\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}\big(g(H)-g({\breve{H}}_{a})\big)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\simeq_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}f_{1}(H){\mathcal{O}}(r^{0})(H-z)^{-1}\chi_{-}(\cdot)\tilde{\xi}_{a}^{+}\big(I_{a}-\breve{I}_{a}\big)({\breve{H}}_{a}-z)^{-1}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{g}(z)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(H){\mathcal{O}}(r^{-\rho_{2}-\mu})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\,\simeq_{2}0.

If
I^{\rm sr}_{a}\neq 0
there is an extra term on the form
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\mu})
in
the above integral, which obviously yields the same conclusion.

As for the term
T_{1}
we use as in Section
8.1
the functions
f_{1}\prec\tilde{f}_{2}\prec\tilde{f_{1}}
. We may write
T_{1}
as

\displaystyle T_{1}\simeq_{2}f_{1}(H)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)\mathrm{i}[g(H),M_{a}N^{a}_{\pm}M_{a}]\tilde{f}_{2}(H)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha}).

(8.5)

Applying the product rule for commutation several
terms arise,
essentially all treated in Section

7
(see also the proof of Lemma
8.1
). Let us show how to treat the contribution from the factors
of
\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{1}]
. (The contribution from the factors
of
\mathrm{i}[g(H),M_{a}]
may be treated similarly, cf. the proof of Lemma

8.1
.)

By (
7.12
) and Remark
7.19
we can write it as

\displaystyle 2f_{1}(H)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)M_{a}\operatorname{Re}\Big(\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{1}]A_{2}^{a}A_{3}^{a}A_{2}^{a}A_{1}\Big)M_{a}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\simeq_{2}f_{1}(H)(Q^{+}_{2})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-0})Q^{+}_{2}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha});\quad Q^{+}_{2}=r^{-1/2}\sqrt{(\chi^{2}_{+})^{\prime}}\Big(\pm B/\epsilon_{0}\Big).

Next we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality estimating

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\big\lvert\langle f_{1}(H)\psi(t),(Q_{2}^{+})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-0})Q_{2}^{+}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\varphi(t)\rangle\big\rvert\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\lVert Q_{2}^{+}{f_{1}(H)\psi(t)}\rVert\,\lVert Q_{2}^{+}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\varphi(t)\rVert\,{\mathrm{d}}t
