\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\lVert Q_{2}^{+}{f_{1}(H)\psi(t)}\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\Big)^{1/2}\,\Big(\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\lVert Q_{2}^{+}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\varphi(t)\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\Big)^{1/2}.

The first factor is finite due to Lemma
8.1

2
, in turn with a bound proportional to

\lVert\psi\rVert
. Similarly, thanks to Remark
8.2

2
, the
second factor is finite too (although not needed with a bound proportional with

\lVert\varphi\rVert
).

We may argue similarly for the other commutators arising from expanding
the commutator in (
8.5
). More precisely we first compute

\displaystyle f_{1}(H)M_{a}A_{1}\mathrm{i}[g(H),A_{2}^{a}A_{3}^{a}A_{2}^{a}]A_{1}M_{a}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad\simeq_{2}f_{1}(H)M_{a}A_{1}\mathrm{i}\Big[g(H),\Big(A_{3}^{a}-\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)A_{3}^{a}\chi_{+}\big(r^{\rho_{2}-1}r_{\delta}^{a}\big)\Big)\Big]A_{1}M_{a}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad\simeq_{2}f_{1}(H)\Big(-(Q_{3}^{+})^{2}-Q_{4}^{+})^{2}+(Q_{5}^{+})^{2}+Q_{6}^{+})^{2}+(Q_{7}^{+})^{2}+Q_{8}^{+})^{2}\Big)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha}).

We integrate, estimate by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and invoke Remarks
8.2

2
and
3
.

These estimates lead
to the conclusion that

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\big\lvert\langle\psi(t),T^{+}_{\alpha}\varphi(t)\rangle\big\rvert\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C_{\varphi}\lVert\psi\rVert,

and by the same arguments, that

\displaystyle\forall\epsilon>0\,\,\exists t_{\epsilon}=t_{\epsilon}(\varphi)>0:\quad\int^{\infty}_{t_{\epsilon}}\big\lvert\langle\psi(t),T^{+}_{\alpha}\varphi(t)\rangle\big\rvert\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq\epsilon\lVert\psi\rVert.

(8.6)

Clearly the existence of

\Omega^{+}_{\alpha}
follows from (
8.6
). The existence of

\Omega^{-}_{\alpha}
can be shown similarly.
∎

9.
Formula for
\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)

Recall from Section
4
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{\pm}_{\alpha}=\Omega^{\pm}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{\pm}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\text{ \,and\, }\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}=\big(\Omega^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\breve{w}_{b}^{+}f_{2}(k_{\beta}\big)^{*}\Omega^{-}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}_{a}^{-}f_{2}(k_{\alpha}).

(9.1)

We show in Appendix
C
the following representation
of
\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
, formally given as

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}f^{2}_{2}(\lambda){\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}\big(T^{+}_{\beta}\big)^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*};\quad\lambda_{\alpha}:=\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha}.

(9.2)

Parallel to Section
3
we call the appearing

\lambda
-depending operators

J_{\beta}{\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}^{*}
and
J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}

future
and past auxiliary
channel wave matrices
, respectively. In Subsections
9.1
and
9.2
we examine the well-definedness of
the right-hand side of (
9.2
) upon substituting
expressions for
T^{+}_{\beta}
and
T^{-}_{\alpha}

derived in the proof of Lemma

8.3
. With the resulting final
representation we then derive the weak
continuity of
\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\cdot)
from
which Theorem
4.1
follows.

9.1.
Phase space estimates of auxiliary channel wave matrices

We aim at bounding the operators (recall the index convention of
Remark
8.2

3
)

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}=f^{2}_{1}(\lambda)Q^{\pm}(a,k)J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),{\mathcal{H}}\big)

as well as showing a continuity property. This will
along with (
8.3b
) be
crucial for our usage of (
9.2
).

Lemma 9.1
.

For all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
and indices
k

\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\,\,\lVert Q^{\pm}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\rVert<\infty,

(9.3)

and the operator-valued functions

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),{\mathcal{H}}\big)

are strongly continuous in
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the cases
Q^{\pm}(a,k)\neq Q_{0}
since
for

Q_{0}
(as given by (
8.1
)) the assertions reduce to properties of

{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}
stated in Section

3
. Whence from this point we assume

Q^{\pm}(a,k)\neq Q_{0}
.
The uniform
bounds (
9.3
) will be proved in Step I below. The stated
strong continuity assertions will then be shown in Steps
II–V. Note that weak continuity is an immediate consequence of
(
9.3
) and the strong continuity for
Q^{\pm}(a,k)=Q_{0}
(to
be used in Steps IV and V).

I.
By ‘the
T^{*}T
argument’ is suffices for (
9.3
) to show that

\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\,\,\lVert{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}J_{\alpha}^{*}f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})Q^{\pm}(a,k)^{*}Q^{\pm}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\rVert<\infty.

We pick, using quantities from Remark

8.2

2
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Phi\in\mathrm{span}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\{\Phi_{1,j},\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3},\Phi_{4}\,|\,j\leq J\}:\\
&\quad\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\Phi]\geq f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})Q^{\pm}(a,k)^{*}Q^{\pm}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\\
&\quad\quad+f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a}){\mathcal{O}}(r^{(-1)_{-}})f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha}).\end{split}

(9.4)

For
\rho>1
we let
\chi_{\rho}=\chi_{-}(r/\rho)
and compute
using the notation
\langle T\rangle_{\phi}=\langle\phi,T\phi\rangle
with

\phi:=J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g
,

g\in L^{2}(C_{a})
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a}-\lambda,\chi_{\rho}\Phi\chi_{\rho}]\rangle_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\langle\chi_{\rho}\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\Phi]\chi_{\rho}\rangle_{\phi}+2\operatorname{Re}{\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\chi_{\rho}]\Phi\chi_{\rho}\rangle_{\phi}}.

For the last term to the right we compute and insert the expression

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\chi_{\rho}]=-\rho^{-1}{\sqrt{-\chi^{\prime}_{-}}(r/\rho)B\sqrt{-\chi^{\prime}_{-}}(r/\rho)},

commute and invoke the dual versions of
(
3.5
) allowing us to conclude that this term is
bounded by
C\lVert g\rVert^{2}
with a constant
C>0
being independent of

\rho>1
. For the first term we use (
9.4
),
(
3.5
) and a commutation, yielding the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda,\rho>1}\,\,\lVert\chi_{\rho}Q^{\pm}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\rVert<\infty.

(9.5)

Clearly
(
9.3
) follows from (
9.5
) and
Lebesgue’s monotone convergence theorem (by taking
\rho\to\infty
).

II.
For

Q_{1}(a,j)=\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}
(as in Lemma

8.1

1
) the strong continuity assertion follows from
(
3.7c
) if
d_{j}=a
due to the representation

\displaystyle\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}=f^{2}_{1}(\lambda)\xi^{+}_{j}J_{\alpha}G_{d_{j}}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}.

For
d_{j}\neq a
we have
d_{j}\lneq a
(since
d_{j}\leq a
). The fact
that the factor

\xi^{+}_{j}
is supported in
Y_{d_{j}}(\delta_{j})
then yields the estimate

\lvert x^{a}\rvert\geq\epsilon\lvert x\rvert
on
\operatorname{supp}\xi^{+}_{j}
for some

\epsilon>0
.
We decompose for
(large)
\rho>1

\displaystyle\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{1}\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}+\chi_{2}\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}},

\displaystyle\chi_{1}=\chi^{2}_{+}\big(\lvert x^{a}\rvert/\rho\big),\quad\chi_{2}=\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\lvert x^{a}\rvert/\rho\big),

and write correspondingly

\displaystyle\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g=\chi_{1}\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g+\chi_{2}\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g;\quad g\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a})\big).

For the first term we estimate using (
3.5
), cf. the proof of

[
Ya1
, Lemma 4.5]
,

\displaystyle\lVert\chi_{1}\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g\rVert^{2}\leq C_{1}\int_{\lvert x^{a}\rvert\geq\rho}{\mathrm{d}}x^{a}\big(\lvert u^{\alpha}\rvert^{2}+\lvert p^{a}u^{\alpha}\rvert^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{-1}\int_{\lvert x_{a}\rvert\leq\epsilon^{-1}\lvert x^{a}\rvert}\big(\lvert\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})^{*}g\rvert^{2}+\lvert p_{a}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g\rvert^{2}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{a}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int_{\lvert x^{a}\rvert\geq\rho}\,\big(\lvert u^{\alpha}\rvert^{2}+\lvert p^{a}u^{\alpha}\rvert^{2}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}x^{a};

here the constant
C_{2}
is independent of
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Consequently the right-hand side can be taken arbitrary
small uniformly in
\lambda
by taken
\rho>1
big enough (now fixed).

For the the second term note that
\lvert x\rvert\leq 2\epsilon^{-1}\rho
on the support of
\chi_{2}
. Hence this term is continuous in

\lambda\in\Lambda

thanks to the established continuity for
Q^{\pm}(a,k)=Q_{0}
.
Whence we have treated the case

Q_{1}(a,j)=\xi^{+}_{j}G_{d_{j}}
for any
j\leq J
.

III.
For
Q_{2}^{+}
(as in Lemma

8.1

2
, leaving out a similar discussion
for
Q_{2}^{-}
) it suffices by (
9.3
) to show continuity in

\lambda
of

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{+}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{+}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g;\quad g\in C^{\infty}(C_{a}).

For
\lambda
close to a fixed
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
we have
correspondingly that
\lambda_{\alpha}=\lambda-\lambda^{\alpha}
is close to

\lambda^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\alpha}
, and we can to some extent
use that for
s>1/2

\displaystyle\lVert\langle x_{a}\rangle^{-s}\phi(\lambda)\rVert\to 0\text{ for }\lambda\to\lambda^{\prime};\quad\phi(\lambda):={\gamma_{a}^{+}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g-{\gamma_{a}^{+}(\lambda^{\prime}_{\alpha})}^{*}g.

By arguments from Step II, in particular it suffices to show that for some

\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\lVert Q_{2}^{+}\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\lvert x^{a}\rvert/(\epsilon r)\big)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}\phi(\lambda)\rVert\to 0\text{ for }\lambda\to\lambda^{\prime}.

In turn, possibly seen by using one-body versions of Lemmas
2.6
and

2.7
and
[
AIIS
, Theorem 1.8]

(more precisely by using resolvent bounds), it suffices to
show that
\lVert\tilde{\varphi}(\lambda)\rVert=o(\lvert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rvert^{0})
, where for

\epsilon,\epsilon_{0}>0
taken small

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{\varphi}_{+}(\lambda)+\tilde{\varphi}_{-}(\lambda),

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{f}_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})\sqrt{(\chi^{2}_{+})^{\prime}}(B/\epsilon_{0})\tilde{f}_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})r^{-1/2}T^{\pm}_{\epsilon}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}\phi(\lambda),

\displaystyle\quad T^{\pm}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle:=\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\lvert x^{a}\rvert/(\epsilon r)\big)\chi_{+}\big(\pm{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big).

(Recall from the discussion at the end of Subsecton

6.1
that we allow ourselves
the freedom of considering only sufficiently small values of
\epsilon_{0}
.)
We can represent

\displaystyle\begin{split}B&=2\operatorname{Re}{\big((x/r)\cdot p+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})\cdot p\big)}\\
&=2\operatorname{Re}\big({(x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}}+{(x^{a}/r)\cdot p^{a}}+{{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})\cdot p}\big)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1}),\end{split}

(9.6)

and by using this
and commutation (cf. Remark

7.19
), we estimate (with
\langle T\rangle_{\varphi}=\langle\varphi,T\varphi\rangle
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tfrac{1}{2}\lVert\tilde{\varphi}_{\pm}(\lambda)\rVert^{2}\\
&\leq\langle I\mp{B}/(4\epsilon_{0})\rangle_{\tilde{\varphi}_{\pm}(\lambda)}+o(\lvert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rvert^{0})\\
&\leq\langle(1+C\epsilon)I\mp\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)/(2\epsilon_{0})}\rangle_{\tilde{\varphi}_{\pm}(\lambda)}+o(\lvert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rvert^{0})\\
&\leq\langle(C\epsilon-1)I\rangle_{\tilde{\varphi}_{\pm}(\lambda)}+o(\lvert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rvert^{0})\\
&\leq o(\lvert\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rvert^{0}).\end{split}

(9.7)

IV.
As for
Q_{3}^{\pm}(a)
and
Q_{4}^{\pm}(a)
we introduce

Q^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\lvert Q_{3}^{\pm}(a)\rvert^{2}+\lvert Q_{4}^{\pm}(a)\rvert^{2}}
. It suffices (thanks to
(
9.3
) and the
weak continuity) to
show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lVert Q^{\pm}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g\rVert^{2}\\
&\quad\to\lVert Q^{\pm}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda^{\prime}_{\alpha})}^{*}g\rVert^{2}\text{
for
}\lambda\to\lambda^{\prime};\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad g\in C^{\infty}(C_{a}).\end{split}

(9.8)

Letting
\chi_{\rho}=\chi_{-}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert/\rho)
for
\rho>1
and
\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)=J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}g
,
we compute as
in Step I
(now with
\Phi^{\pm}_{3}=\Phi^{\pm}_{3}(a)
)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\chi_{\rho}\Phi^{\pm}_{3}\chi_{\rho}]\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\Phi^{\pm}_{3}]\rangle_{\chi_{\rho}\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}+2\operatorname{Re}{\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\chi_{\rho}]\Phi^{\pm}_{3}\chi_{\rho}\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}}.

We take the limit
\rho\to\infty
. For the first term we
get in the limit
\mp\lVert f^{2}_{1}(\lambda)Q^{\pm}\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)\rVert^{2}
plus a term that we
know is continuous in
\lambda
by Steps II and III. Here we used
commutation to replace
\lVert f^{2}_{1}(\lambda)Q^{\pm}\chi_{\rho}\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)\rVert^{2}
by

\displaystyle\langle Q_{3}^{\pm}(a)^{*}\chi^{2}_{\rho}Q_{3}^{\pm}(a)\rangle_{f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}+\langle Q_{4}^{\pm}(a)^{*}\chi^{2}_{\rho}Q_{4}^{\pm}(a)\rangle_{f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}

before taking the limit.

As for the second term we compute as follows, using again
conveniently
one-body versions of Lemma

2.7
and
[
AIIS
, Theorem 1.8]
, and
using in
the second step also
1

and
2
from Section
5
. (The
computation overlaps partially one in Appendix
A
.) A
further elaboration is needed, it is given after the computation. We compute for any

\epsilon,\epsilon_{0}>0
taken small

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\to\infty}2\operatorname{Re}\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\chi_{\rho}]\Phi^{\pm}_{3}\chi_{\rho}\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}

(9.9)

\displaystyle=\pm 2\sqrt{\lambda_{\alpha}}f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\big\langle(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)M_{a}^{2}\big\rangle_{\chi_{+}\big(\pm{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\pm\Big(2\sqrt{\lambda_{\alpha}}\Big)^{3}f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\big\langle(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\big\rangle_{m_{a}(\hat{x}_{a})\chi_{+}\big(\pm{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\mp\tfrac{2\lambda_{\alpha}}{\pi}f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lVert{m_{a}}g\rVert_{L^{2}(C_{a})}^{2}.

Note that in the first step of (
9.9
) we used that
effectively the factor
A_{3}^{a}=A_{3\pm}^{a}
of
\Phi^{\pm}_{3}
can be removed. This is
essentially due to the fact that

B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}=0
in
\{\lvert x^{a}\rvert<cr^{\delta}\}
,
cf.
2
of Section
6
, since for the complement we have

\displaystyle 2\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\big\langle p_{a}\cdot\hat{x}_{a}(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)\chi^{2}_{+}\big(\tfrac{2\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{cr^{\delta}}\big)\Phi^{\pm}_{3}\big\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}=0.

In fact (using commutation and Remark

7.19
)

\displaystyle 2\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\big\langle p_{a}\cdot\hat{x}_{a}(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\tfrac{2\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{cr^{\delta}}\big)\Phi^{\pm}_{3}\big\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=2f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\big\langle p_{a}\cdot\hat{x}_{a}(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\tfrac{2\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{cr^{\delta}}\big)A_{3}^{a}M_{a}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})^{2}A_{1}^{2}M_{a}\big\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)},

and

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\tfrac{2\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{cr^{\delta}}\big)\big(A_{3}^{a}-I\big)=\pm\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\tfrac{2\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{cr^{\delta}}\big)B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}(\pm B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}-z)^{-1}\,z^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\chi_{-}}(z)=0.

(9.10)

Once
A_{3}^{a}
is removed we can also remove the remaining factor

\chi^{2}_{-}\big(\tfrac{2\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{cr^{\delta}}\big)
. Similarly in the first step we also replaced the factors of

A_{1}=A_{1\pm}
by factors of
\chi_{+}\big(\pm{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)
. Using the one-body version of
[
AIIS
, Theorem
1.8]
as in Step III this is
justified by the assertions

\displaystyle 2f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\big\langle p_{a}\cdot\hat{x}_{a}(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)M_{a}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\big(A_{1}^{2}-I\big)\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})M_{a}\big\rangle_{\phi_{1}^{\pm}}=0,

\displaystyle 2f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\big\langle p_{a}\cdot\hat{x}_{a}(\chi^{2}_{-})^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x_{a}\rvert}{\rho}\big)\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)M_{a}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})A_{1}^{2}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})M_{a}\big\rangle_{\phi_{2}^{\pm}}=0;

\displaystyle\quad\quad\phi_{1}^{\pm}=\chi_{+}\big(\pm{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)\phi^{\pm}(\lambda),

\displaystyle\quad\quad\phi_{2}^{\pm}=\chi_{+}\big(\mp{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)\phi^{\pm}(\lambda).

In turn these assertions may be verified essentially as in
Step III (more precisely by commutation along the lines of (
9.7
)) by showing

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)\chi_{+}\big(\pm{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)^{2}\big(A_{1\pm}^{2}-I\big)\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1/2})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{0_{-}}),\end{split}

(9.11a)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\chi_{-}\big(\tfrac{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}{\epsilon r}\big)\chi_{+}\big(\mp{\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)^{2}A^{2}_{1\pm}\tilde{f}_{2}({\breve{H}}_{a})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1/2})={\mathcal{O}}(r^{0_{-}}).\end{split}

(9.11b)

Obviously the right-hand side of (
9.9
), i.e.
\mp\tfrac{2\lambda_{\alpha}}{\pi}f^{4}_{1}(\lambda)\lVert{m_{a}}g\rVert_{L^{2}(C_{a})}^{2}
, is continuous in

\lambda
. We conclude that the function

\displaystyle\lambda\to\lVert Q^{\pm}f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha})\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)\rVert^{2}

is continuous, as wanted.

V.
We mimic Step IV using now

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\lvert Q_{5}^{\pm}(a)\rvert^{2}+\lvert Q_{6}^{\pm}(a)\rvert^{2}+\lvert Q_{7}^{\pm}(a)\rvert^{2}+\lvert Q_{8}^{\pm}(a)\rvert^{2}},

\Phi^{\pm}_{4}=\Phi^{\pm}_{4}(a)
(replacing
\Phi^{\pm}_{3}(a)
)
and using
again Steps II and III,
yielding similarly the strong continuity for
Q_{5}^{\pm}(a),Q_{6}^{\pm}(a),Q_{7}^{\pm}(a)
and
Q_{8}^{\pm}(a)
. In fact this case is simpler
in that one readily shows the following analogue of (
9.9
),

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\to\infty}2\operatorname{Re}\langle\mathrm{i}[{\breve{H}}_{a},\chi_{\rho}]\Phi^{\pm}_{4}\chi_{\rho}\rangle_{\phi^{\pm}(\lambda)}=0,

which obviously is continuous in
\lambda
. ∎

9.2.
Conclusion of argument, the weak
continuity

The expression to the right in (
9.2
) can (and should) be written, cf. the proof of
Lemma
8.3
, as a sum of terms on
the form

\displaystyle\Big(f^{2}_{2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle{\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}Q^{+}(b,l)^{*}B^{*}_{+}\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(Q^{+}(b,l)f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)f_{1}(H)Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}\Big)\Big(B_{-}Q^{-}(a,k)J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\Big),

where
B_{+}=B_{+}(b,l)
and
B_{-}=B_{-}(a,k)
are bounded
operators. The middle factor is weakly continuous by
Remark
8.2

3
. The factor to the right is
strongly continuous by Lemma
9.1
. The
adjoint of the factor to the left is
strongly continuous by the same result. Consequently the
entire product is weakly continuous, and Theorem

4.1
follows (cf. the
discussion given before the statement). ∎

10.
Channel wave matrices and scattering at fixed
energy

In this concuding section we derive various consequences of our proof
of Theorem
4.1
. Whence we concretely
construct the open channel wave
matrices and the scattering matrix for all energies in the small
neighbourhood
I_{0}\ni\lambda_{0}
fixed in Section

4
. Away
from a null set of
energies, more precisely at any stationary scattering
energy in
I_{0}
, the scattering matrix is unitary and strongly
continuous and it is characterized by asymptotic properties of minimum
generalized eigenfunctions. The discussion
relies heavily on Appendix
C
.

For all
b\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
we pick an arbitrary increasing sequence

(C_{b,k})_{k}
of open reflection symmetric subsets of
C_{b}^{\prime}
with closure

\overline{C}_{b,k}\subset C_{b}^{\prime}
and
\cup_{k}C_{b,k}=C_{b}^{\prime}
. We pick for each such set a
function
m_{b}=m_{b,k}
as in Section
5
such
that
m_{b}(\hat{\xi})=1
for all
\hat{\xi}\in C_{b,k}
. Let for any channel
\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})

\displaystyle\chi_{\beta,k}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\beta,k}(p_{b})=F_{\beta}^{-1}1_{C_{b,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})F_{\beta},

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{\beta,k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{\beta,k}(p_{b})=F_{\beta}^{-1}(4\lambda_{\beta})^{-1}1_{C_{b,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})F_{\beta}.

By combining (
4.6a
), (
4.6b
) and
(
C.4a
) we then obtain (by an elementary approximation argument)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&W^{+}_{\beta}(f_{2}g)(k_{\beta})\chi_{\beta,k}\varphi=\widetilde{W}^{+}_{\beta}g(k_{\beta})\tilde{\chi}_{\beta,k}\varphi\\
&=\int\,\tfrac{\pi}{2\lambda_{\beta}}(f_{2}g)(\lambda)\big(f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)T^{+}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\big)1_{C_{b,k}}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;\\
&\quad\quad\quad\text{ for any }\varphi\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{b})\text{ with }s>1/2.\end{split}

(10.1a)

Note that indeed
T^{+}_{\beta}=T^{+}_{\beta,k}
depends on
k
.

We are lead to introduce the
approximate channel wave matrices

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)^{*}=\tfrac{\pi}{2\lambda_{\beta}}f_{1}(H){\delta(H-\lambda)T^{+}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}}1_{C_{b,k}};\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}},\,\lambda\in I_{0}\,(\ni\lambda_{0}).

(10.1b)

10.1.
Channel wave matrices

By (
10.1a
) the right-hand side of (
10.1b
)
is independent of details of the
construction of
T^{+}_{\beta,k}
. Considered as an
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}))
-valued function of
\lambda\in I_{0}

for
any
s>1/2
, it is strongly continuous. Clearly

\displaystyle 1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k+1}(\lambda)=\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}(C_{b})).

We can then for any
\lambda\in I_{0}
and
\psi\in L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
with
s>1/2
introduce a measurable function

\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi
on
C_{b}
by

\displaystyle 1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi=\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi;\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(10.2)

We argue that
\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\in L^{2}(C_{b})
as follows.
With (
10.1a
) it follows from Stone’s formula that

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta}\,\lVert\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\leq\int_{\Delta}\,\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\quad\text{for
any interval }\Delta\subseteq I_{0},

leading
to the
conclusion that

\displaystyle\lVert\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}\text{ for a.e. }\lambda\in I_{0}.

(10.3)

Since
\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\cdot)\psi\in L^{2}(C_{b})
is
weakly continuous and the functional
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{2}
is weakly lower semi-continuous we
conclude that

\displaystyle\lVert\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0}.

In particular also
\lVert\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\lVert\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}
, showing that indeed
\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\in L^{2}(C_{b})
.

We can now record, using Lemma
9.1
and
(
2.7a
), that for any
s>1/2

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}(C_{b}))\text{
with a weakly continuous dependence of }\lambda\in I_{0},

and in fact that the
outgoing channel wave matrix

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}))\text{
depends strongly continuously of }\lambda\in I_{0}.

By taking
k\to\infty
in (
10.1a
) we conclude that for any
\psi\in L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
,
s>1/2
,

\displaystyle\big(F_{\beta}(W^{+}_{\beta})^{*}\psi\big)(\lambda)=\Gamma_{\beta}^{+}(\lambda)\psi\quad\text{for a.e.
}\lambda\in I_{0}.

We can argue similarly for
W^{-}_{\beta}
, cf. (
C.10a
), and
introduce the
incoming channel wave
matrix

\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}
, concluding by the same
reasoning that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}(C_{b}))\text{
depends weakly continuously of }\lambda\in I_{0},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}))\text{
depends strongly continuously of }\lambda\in I_{0},

\displaystyle\forall\,\psi\in L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),\,s>1/2:\quad\big(F_{\beta}(W^{-}_{\beta})^{*}\psi\big)(\lambda)=\Gamma_{\beta}^{-}(\lambda)\psi\quad\text{for a.e.
}\lambda\in I_{0}.

We summarize as follows.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\beta
be a given channel
\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})
,
f:I^{\beta}=(\lambda^{\beta},\infty)\to{\mathbb{C}}
be
bounded and
continuous, and let and
s>1/2
. For any
\varphi\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{b})

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{\beta}(f1_{I_{0}})(k_{\beta})\varphi=\int_{I_{0}}\,f(\lambda)\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\in L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),

(10.4a)

More generally for any
\varphi\in 1_{I_{0}}(k_{\beta})L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{b})

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{\beta}f(k_{\beta})\varphi=\int_{I_{0}}\,f(\lambda)\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}(F_{\beta}\varphi)(\lambda,\cdot)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\in L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),

(10.4b)

In (
10.4a
) the integrand is a bounded and continuous

L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
-valued function. For (
10.4b
) the integral
has the weak interpretation of a measurable

L_{-s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
-valued function.

For
f(\lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t\lambda}
,

t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the formulas (
10.4a
) and (
10.4b
) represent
Schrödinger wave packets of energy-localized states outgoing to or incoming from
the channel
\beta
, cf. a discussion in Subsection
1.1
.

10.2.
Parseval identities and construction of the scattering matrix

Let
Q=Q_{0}
be given as in (
8.1
) (for an arbitrary
\epsilon>0
).
By the orthogonality and completeness of channel wave operators we deduce the Parseval
formulas

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Delta}\,\,\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q_{0}^{*}\varphi\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\int_{\Delta}\,\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{Q_{0}^{*}\varphi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{for
all intervals }\Delta\subseteq I_{0}\text{ \,and\, }\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}.\end{split}

(10.5)

Moreover, arguing more or less as before, we can deduce
from (
10.5
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q_{0}^{*}\varphi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{Q_{0}^{*}\varphi}\quad\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0},

(10.6a)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q_{0}^{*}\varphi\rVert^{2}=\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{Q_{0}^{*}\varphi}\quad\text{ a.e. in }I_{0},\text{ say for }\lambda\in I_{0}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}_{0}.

(10.6b)

By first taking first
\varphi
from any dense countable
subset
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
we then
obtain (
10.6a
) and (
10.6b
)
for all vectors from
{\mathcal{C}}

with the null set
{\mathcal{N}}_{0}
in (
10.6b
) being independent of

\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Secondly we extend by continuity and conclude that (
10.6b
) holds with the same null
set
{\mathcal{N}}_{0}
for all

\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}
. In particular we obtain
that for some null subset
{\mathcal{N}}_{0}\subseteq I_{0}

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in L^{2}_{\infty}:\,\,\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0},

(10.7a)

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in L^{2}_{\infty}:\,\,\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}=\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}_{0}.

(10.7b)

We are lead to introduce the following concept.

Definition 10.2
.

A stationary scattering energy is a point
\lambda\in I_{0}
for which

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in L^{2}_{\infty}:\,\,\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}=\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{\psi}.

(10.8)

We have shown that almost all energies in
I_{0}
are stationary scattering
energies. The property missing for possibly having this conclusion for all
energies in
I_{0}
is clearly the continuity in
\lambda
of the sum to the left in (
10.8
).

Recall that the above operator
Q_{0}
is only one out of many
‘
Q
-operators’ from our procedure. More
precisely for each

k
the expansions of
T^{\pm}_{\beta}
used in Sections
8
and
9
involved
several (but
finitely many) ‘
Q
-operators’ either on the above form

Q_{0}
or being one of those from Lemma
8.1
. So when varying
k
we
obtain in total an infinite family of ‘
Q
-operators’,
say denoted by
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

For any
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\varphi\in L^{2}_{\infty}
we record that

\tilde{f}_{2}(H)Q^{*}\varphi\in L^{2}_{\infty}
, cf. Remark

8.2

1
, and

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q^{*}\varphi=\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)Q^{*}\varphi=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)Q^{*}\varphi\in L^{2}(C_{b});\,\lambda\in I_{0}.

However (more generally) for any finite sum
\psi=\sum_{j}Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}
, where now

\varphi_{j}
is arbitrary from
{\mathcal{H}}
, the quantity
\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi=\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)\psi
is well-defined. Thanks to
(
10.1a
) it is unambiguously given as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi=\sum_{j}1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}\in L^{2}(C_{b}),

and as used before

1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)=1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k+1}(\lambda)
,
allowing us to compute

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi:

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)\psi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\lim_{k\to\infty}1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\lim_{k\to\infty}1_{C_{b,k}}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}\in L^{2}(C_{b}).

We used the following analogue of (
10.3
),

\displaystyle\lVert\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)Q_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}}\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0}.

(10.9)

This reasoning yields that

\displaystyle\forall Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}:\,\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)Q^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}},L^{2}(C_{b})),

in fact with a weakly continuous dependence of

\lambda\in I_{0}
.

We are lead to introduce the subspace
{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}^{-2}
of vectors

\psi
as above, i.e. vectors being given as a finite
sum of terms on the form
Q^{*}\varphi
, where
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
and

\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Mimicking the proof of (
10.6a
) we
conclude the following extension of (
10.9
),

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in{\mathcal{V}}:\,\,\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi}\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0}.

(10.10)

We are lead to define

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda):{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{G}}:=\Sigma^{\oplus}_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}L^{2}(C_{b});\quad\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)=\big(\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\big)_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda}.

By (
10.10
) and the above computation,

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)=\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)
are well-defined linear maps. Using the natural
Hilbert space structure on
{\mathcal{G}}
,
(
10.10
) may be phrased as

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in{\mathcal{V}}:\,\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\leq\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi}\text{ for all }\lambda\in I_{0}.

(10.11a)

The
expression
\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi}
appearing to the right in
(
10.11a
) is continuous in
\lambda\in I_{0}
,
cf. Remark
8.2

3
. The
quantities on the left-hand side might not have this property,
however we can record that
\Gamma^{\pm}(\cdot)\psi

are weakly continuous and note
the following version af (
10.5
),

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in{\mathcal{V}},\forall\text{ intervals }\Delta\subseteq I_{0}:\,\int_{\Delta}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\int_{\Delta}\,\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

(10.11b)

Since almost all energies in
I_{0}
are stationary scattering
energies the Parseval formulas (
10.11b
) may be seen as a
consequence of the following extension of (
10.8
).

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
be
any stationary scattering energy. Then

\displaystyle\forall\psi\in{\mathcal{V}}:\,\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}=\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\,\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}=\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi}.

(10.12)

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
\psi=\Sigma_{j}Q^{*}_{j}\varphi_{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
, let

\chi_{m}=\chi_{-}(r/m)
for
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and then

\psi_{m}=\Sigma_{j}Q^{*}_{j}\chi_{m}\varphi_{j}
. Since

\chi_{m}\varphi_{j}\in L^{2}_{\infty}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}(H)Q^{*}_{j}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{0})

(cf. Remark
8.2

1
), also
\tilde{f}_{2}(H)\psi_{m}\in L^{2}_{\infty}
, and for such vector we know the identities (
10.8
). By (
10.11a
) the sequences

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)\psi_{m}=\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi_{m}\to\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi
in

{\mathcal{G}}
. Whence also
\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\tilde{f}_{2}(H)\psi_{m}\rVert^{2}\to\lVert\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\rVert^{2}
. Similarly
\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi_{m}}\to\langle\delta(H-\lambda)\rangle_{f_{1}(H)\psi}
. Since the identities in (
10.8
)
hold for all elements of two sequences we conclude the limiting identities,
agreeing with (
10.12
).
∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
. Then the subspaces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}^{\pm}_{\infty}=\{{\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi}=\big(\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi\big)_{{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}}\in{\mathcal{G}}\,|\,\psi\in L^{2}_{\infty}\}\text{ are dense in }{\mathcal{G}}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove density of the subspaces
{\mathcal{G}}^{\pm}_{\mathcal{V}}=\{{\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi}\in{\mathcal{G}}\,|\,\psi\in{\mathcal{V}}\}
, cf. the proof of Lemma
10.3
.
Let us only
consider the ‘minus case’. For any ‘open channel’
\alpha
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
g_{a}\in L^{2}(C_{a})
we consider

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}=\psi_{\alpha,k,g_{a}}=T^{-}_{\alpha,k}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}1_{C_{a,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})g_{a}.

(10.13a)

We can write
\psi_{\alpha}
as a finite sum of vectors on
the form
Q^{*}\varphi
where
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Whence

\psi_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{V}}
and

\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi_{\alpha}
is a
well-defined vector in
L^{2}(C_{b})
. We compute for any
l\in{\mathbb{N}}

the vector

1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi_{\alpha}

using Remark
C.1
, concluding
that
1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi_{\alpha}=0
if
\beta\neq\alpha
, while for
\beta=\alpha

\displaystyle 1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})\Gamma^{-}_{\beta}(\lambda)\psi_{\alpha}=1_{C_{a,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha,l}(\lambda)\psi_{\alpha}=-\tfrac{2\lambda_{\alpha}}{\pi}1_{C_{a,l}\cap{C_{a,k}}}g_{a}.

By taking
l\to\infty
it follows that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{\mathcal{V}}\ni\Gamma^{-}(\lambda)\psi_{\alpha}=(\delta_{\beta\alpha}\tfrac{-2\lambda_{\alpha}}{\pi}1_{{C_{a,k}}}g_{a})_{{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}}.

(10.13b)

By varying over
\alpha
(with
\lambda^{\alpha}<\lambda_{0}
),
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
g_{a}\in L^{2}(C_{a})

the
corresponding vectors to the right in (
10.13b
) span a
dense subset of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
∎

Definition 10.5
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
be
any stationary scattering energy. Then we let
U(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{G}})
be
the uniquely defined unitary
operator
U(\lambda)
on
{\mathcal{G}}
such that

\displaystyle U(\lambda){\Gamma^{-}(\lambda)\psi}={\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)\psi};\quad\psi\in{\mathcal{V}}.

(Note that Lemma
10.4
is used.)

Definition 10.6
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
. The scattering matrix
S(\lambda)
is the unique bounded operator on
{\mathcal{G}}
with each
\beta\alpha
-entry
given by
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
as specified in Theorem

4.1
. (Note that
\lVert S(\lambda)\rVert\leq 1

by the orthogonality of channels and the weak continuity.)

Theorem 10.7
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
be any stationary scattering energy. Then
S(\lambda)
is unitary.
Moreover
the
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{G}})
-valued function
S(\cdot)
is strongly
continuous at
\lambda
, for
any
\psi\in{\mathcal{V}}
the
{\mathcal{G}}
-valued functions

\Gamma^{\pm}(\cdot)\psi=(\Gamma^{\pm}_{\alpha}(\cdot)\psi)_{{\lambda^{\alpha}<\lambda_{0}}}
are norm-continuous
at
\lambda
, and

\displaystyle S(\lambda)\Gamma^{-}(\lambda)\psi=\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)\psi.

(10.14)

Proof.

It suffices to check that

S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)=U_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
. By
(
10.13a
), (
10.13b
),
(
10.1b
), (
9.2
) and (
4.7a
)

\displaystyle 1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})U_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)1_{{C_{a,k}}}g_{a}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\pi}{2\lambda_{\alpha}}\Gamma^{+}_{\beta,l}(\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,k}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}1_{C_{a,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})g_{a}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\pi}{2\lambda_{\alpha}}\tfrac{\pi}{2\lambda_{\beta}}1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b}){\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}\big(T^{+}_{\beta,l}\big)^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,k}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}1_{C_{a,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})g_{a}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}{16\lambda_{\beta}\lambda_{\alpha}}1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b}){\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}\big(T^{+}_{\beta,l}\big)^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,k}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}1_{C_{a,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})g_{a}

\displaystyle=\big(16\lambda_{\beta}\lambda_{\alpha}\big)^{-1}1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)1_{C_{a,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})g_{a}

\displaystyle=1_{C_{b,l}}(\hat{\xi}_{b})S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)1_{C_{a,k}}(\hat{\xi}_{a})g_{a}.

We take
k,l\to\infty
and conclude that indeed

U_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g_{a}=S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g_{a}
for any
g_{a}\in L^{2}(C_{a})
.
∎

The free energy
K:={\mathrm{diag}}(k_{\alpha})
is diagonalised on the neighbourhood

I_{0}\ni\lambda_{0}
by

\displaystyle F_{I_{0}}:\sum_{\lambda^{\alpha}<\lambda_{0}}

\displaystyle\oplus 1_{I_{0}}(k_{\alpha})L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})\to\sum_{\lambda^{\alpha}<\lambda_{0}}\oplus L^{2}(I_{0},L^{2}(C_{a}))=\int_{I_{0}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{G}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;

\displaystyle F_{I_{0}}={\mathrm{diag}}(F_{\alpha}).

Recall from Theorem
4.1
the
notation
S_{\beta\alpha}=\big(W_{\beta}^{+}\big)^{*}W_{\alpha}^{-}
.

Corollary 10.8
.

The scattering matrix considered
as a map

\displaystyle I_{0}\ni\lambda\to S(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{G}})\text{ is
weakly continuous.}

Within the class of such maps it is uniquely determined by the identity

\displaystyle F_{I_{0}}SF^{-1}_{I_{0}}

\displaystyle=F_{I_{0}}(S_{\beta\alpha})_{\lambda^{\beta},\,\lambda^{\alpha}<\lambda_{0}}\,F^{-1}_{I_{0}}=\int^{\oplus}_{I_{0}}S(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

(10.15)

For almost all energies in
I_{0}
the scattering matrix
is unitary and strongly continuous.

Remark
.

It is stated in
[
Ya1
, Theorem 6.7]
that for short-range
systems the scattering
matrix is strongly continuous away from thresholds and eigenvalues. The proof
appears incorrect to the author (however the assertion of weak continuity
is correctly derived).

10.3.
Besov space setting and minimum generalized eigenfunctions

In Lemma
10.3
we considered
\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)=\big(\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)\big)_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda}
as mappings

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda):{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
;

\lambda\in I_{0}
. The extended space
{\mathcal{V}}\supseteq L^{2}_{\infty}
was convenient for our constructions, however there is
a different extension which appears ’more natural’, namely the Besov
space
{\mathcal{B}}
in which

L^{2}_{\infty}
is densely embedded. Using
(
2.7b
), (
10.7a
) and extension by continuity it follows that

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{G}})
with a uniform bound in

\lambda\in I_{0}
.
Clearly
\Gamma^{\pm}(\cdot)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{G}})
are
strongly continuous at any stationary scattering energy
\lambda\in I_{0}
in
which case (
10.14
) also holds for
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Due to
Theorem
10.9
(stated below) the vector

\Gamma^{-}(\lambda)\psi
with

\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}
(appearing
to
the left in (
10.14
)) in this case is the generic form of vectors in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Introducing the space of minimum generalized eigenfunctions,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}=\{\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}|\,(H-\lambda)\phi=0\},

we note that
\delta(H-\lambda)\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
for

\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}
(or possibly for
\psi\in f_{1}(H){\mathcal{V}}
). However if
\lambda
is a
stationary scattering energy, in fact any

\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
has this form. This is a
consequence
of the following more general
result. We remark that there are analogous assertions for various one-body type Hamiltonians in the literature, see for
example
[
IS2
]
.

Theorem 10.9
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
be any stationary scattering energy.
Then the operators
\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda):{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
,

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}:{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
and
\delta(H-\lambda):{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
all map
onto. Moreover the wave matrices

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}:{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}(\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}^{*})
are bi-continuous linear isomorphisms.

Proof.

The ranges of
\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)

are dense due to Lemma

10.4
, and clearly
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
is closed in

{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
.
Thanks to the open
mapping and closed range theorems (see for example see
[
Yo
]
)
it then suffices
to show that
\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}
map onto
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
. (Here
and below we use the identities

\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\Gamma^{\pm}(\lambda)=\delta(H-\lambda)
as
quadratic forms on
{\mathcal{B}}
.)

For convenience we only show that
\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)^{*}
maps
onto

{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
. Let
B
is given by (
6.3a
) (possibly rescaled),
and let
h
be the function
h(e)=e\tilde{f}_{1}(e)
(
h(e)
is
denoted by
g(\lambda)
in
Section
8
) and note that
h(H)-\lambda
annihilates the spaces
\ran\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
. Due to
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\chi_{-}(\pm B/\epsilon)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}\text{ for any }\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}.

(10.16)

(The fact that
\chi_{-}(\pm B/\epsilon)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{0})
implies that

\chi_{-}(\pm B/\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}})
, cf.
[
Hö2
, Theorem 14.1.4]
.)
For any given
\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
we introduce for
\epsilon>0

taken small
enough

\displaystyle\phi_{+}=\chi_{+}(B/\epsilon)\phi,\quad\phi_{-}=\phi-\phi_{+},\quad g_{\rho}=\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)\chi_{\rho}\big(h(H)-\lambda\big)\phi_{+};

here

\chi_{\rho}=\chi_{-}(r/\rho)
,
\rho>1
. By commuting the factor
h(H)-\lambda
through the factor
\chi_{\rho}

we deduce that
\sup_{\rho>1}\,\lVert g_{\rho}\rVert<\infty
(note that only the commutator contributes).
Let
g_{\infty}=\wlim_{n\to\infty}g_{\rho_{n}}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, the limit
taken along a suitable
sequence
\rho_{n}\to\infty
, and let
\breve{\phi}=\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)^{*}g_{\infty}
. We can now mimic the proof
of
[
IS2
, Lemma 3.12]
, using the identity

\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)^{*}\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)=\delta(H-\lambda)
, the
decomposition
\phi=\phi_{+}+\phi_{-}
and
the bounds (
10.16
) in combination with (
7.9
), and conclude that
\phi=\breve{\phi}
. In particular
\phi\in\ran\Gamma^{+}(\lambda)^{*}
as wanted.
∎

The following functions
w^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[g]
are identical
with the functions
v^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[g]
of Subsection

2.3
up to trivial factors.

Lemma 10.10
.

Let
\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})
be any
channel with
\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}
. For
\epsilon>0
taken
small enough the following bounds hold for any
\lambda\in I_{0}
and any
g\in L^{2}(C_{b})
.

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{-}(\mp B/\epsilon)\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}g-J_{\beta}w^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[g]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*},

where

\displaystyle w^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[g](x_{b})

\displaystyle=d_{\beta}^{\pm}\lambda_{\beta}^{-1/4}\chi_{+}(\lvert x_{b}\rvert)\lvert x_{b}\rvert^{(1-n_{b})/2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\mathrm{i}K_{b}(x_{b},\lambda_{\beta})}g(\hat{x}_{b});

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad d_{\beta}^{\pm}=\mp\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\mathrm{i}(n_{b}-3)/4}\pi^{-1/2}.

Proof.

By (
10.1b
) and its ‘minus-analogue’

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\pm}_{\beta,k}(\lambda)^{*}=\pm\tfrac{\pi}{2\lambda_{\beta}}f_{1}(H){\delta(H-\lambda)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}}1_{C_{b,k}}.

(10.17)

The identity (
C.4b
) may formally be seen as
a replacement
of the factor

\pm 2\pi\delta(H-\lambda)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}
from (
C.4a
) by
{\Phi_{\beta,k}^{\pm}+\mathrm{i}R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}}
, and from
the derivation of
(
C.4a
) we see that this is legitimate considering the image
space of the operators as being

L^{2}_{-1}(\bf X)
.
In particular there exist in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),L^{2}_{-1}(\bf X))

\displaystyle f_{1}(H)R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}1_{C_{b,k}}:=\wlim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}f_{1}(H)R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}1_{C_{b,k}}.

By combining (
8.3c
) and Lemma

9.1
we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{\epsilon>0}\,\lVert R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}1_{C_{b,k}}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*})}<\infty.

Whence in fact

\displaystyle S^{\mp}:=f_{1}(H)R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}1_{C_{b,k}}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*}).

Next we expand for each sign
T^{\pm}_{\beta,k}
in terms of the
Q
-operators and
consider the approximation of
S^{\mp}
given by replacing
R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)f_{1}(H)Q^{*}_{j}
by

R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)f_{1}(H)Q^{*}_{j}\chi_{\rho}
, say denoted by

S^{\mp}_{\rho}
(as in the proof of Theorem

10.9
, here

\chi_{\rho}=\chi_{-}(r/\rho)
with
\rho
considered large). Now
S^{\mp}_{\rho}\to S^{\mp}
strongly in

{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*})
, and by (
10.16
)

\chi^{2}_{-}(\mp B/\epsilon)S^{\mp}_{\rho}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*})
. Whence also
\chi^{2}_{-}(\mp B/\epsilon)S^{\mp}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*})
.

By density and continuity we are left with showing

\displaystyle{\tfrac{1}{4\lambda_{\beta}}\chi^{2}_{-}(\mp B/\epsilon)f_{1}(H)\Phi_{\beta,k}^{\pm}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}1_{C_{b,k}}g_{k}-J_{\beta}w^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{k}]}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}

for any
g_{k}=1_{C_{b,k}}g\in C^{\infty}(C_{b})
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. In turn, thanks to the form of
\Phi_{\beta,k}^{\pm}
(and commutation), it remains to show that

\displaystyle{\tfrac{1}{4\lambda_{\beta}}f_{1}(H)\Phi_{\beta,k}^{\pm}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}1_{C_{b,k}}g_{k}-J_{\beta}w^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{k}]}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}

for any such
g_{k}
.

By using the analysis of Steps IV and V in
the proof of Lemma
9.1
(not to be repeated
here) the first term
simplifies
exactly as the subtracted second term modulo a term in
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
.
∎

We can now give a ‘geometric interpretation’ of the operator

\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}
needed for a wave packet description of
an incoming
\alpha
-channel experiment, energy-localized in

I_{0}
, cf. a discussion in Subsection
1.1
. More precisely we characterize for any stationary scattering
energy the space of
generalized eigenfunctions in the range of

\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}
in terms of asymptotics expressed by
the quasi-modes
J_{\beta}w^{\pm}_{\lambda,\beta}[\cdot]
of Lemma
10.10
. The result
may also be vieved as a characterization of the incoming

\alpha
-channel part of the
scattering matrix.
We equip the space
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
with the quotient-norm, thus
making it a Banach space.

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
\lambda\in I_{0}
be any stationary scattering energy and
\alpha=(a,\lambda^{\alpha},u^{\alpha})
be any
channel with
\lambda^{\alpha}<\lambda_{0}
. Then the following
existence and uniqueness result holds for any
g\in L^{2}(C_{a})
.

(1)

Let
u=\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}g
, and let

(g_{\beta})_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be given by

g_{\beta}=S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g
. Then, as an
identity in
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
,

\displaystyle u=J_{\alpha}w^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]+\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}J_{\beta}w^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{\beta}].

(10.18)

(2)

Conversely, if (
10.18
)
is fulfilled for some
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}

and some
(g_{\beta})_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{G}}
,
then
u=\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}g
and

g_{\beta}=S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g
for all

\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}
.

Proof.

I.
We note that with
F_{\rho}:=F(\{x\in X|\,\lvert x\rvert<\rho\})
,
\rho>1
, the expression

\displaystyle\lVert u\rVert_{\rm quo}=\limsup_{\rho\to\infty}\,\rho^{-1/2}\lVert F_{\rho}u\rVert

is a norm on
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
equivalent with the
quotient-norm. Using this norm the Cauchy criterion assures that for
any
(g_{\beta})_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathcal{G}}
the right-hand
side of (
10.18
) is well-defined. The cross-terms do not
contribute for the following reason: For given open different channels

\beta=(b,\lambda^{\beta},u^{\beta})
and
\beta^{\prime}=(b^{\prime},\lambda^{\beta^{\prime}},u^{\beta^{\prime}})
with
b=b^{\prime}
, Fubini’s theorem and the fact that

\langle u^{\beta},u^{\beta^{\prime}}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{b}}=0
clearly implies

\displaystyle\langle F_{\rho}J_{\beta}w^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{\beta}],F_{\rho}J_{\beta^{\prime}}w^{+}_{\lambda,\beta^{\prime}}[g_{\beta^{\prime}}]\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\int_{{\mathbf{X}}}\,\overline{F_{\rho}u^{\beta}w^{+}_{\lambda,\beta}[g_{\beta}]}\,F_{\rho}u^{\beta^{\prime}}w^{+}_{\lambda,\beta^{\prime}}[g_{\beta^{\prime}}]\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0.

(10.19)

If
b\neq b^{\prime}
we let
c\in{\mathcal{A}}
be given by
{\mathbf{X}}^{c}={\mathbf{X}}^{b}+{\mathbf{X}}^{b^{\prime}}
and
n_{c}=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{c}
, and note that in this
case

n_{c}\leq(n_{b}+n_{b^{\prime}}-1)/2
. By approximation, and by using Fubini’s
theorem and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can assume that the functions

g_{\beta},g_{\beta^{\prime}},u^{\beta}
and
u^{\beta^{\prime}}
are all bounded with
compact support. With this assumption we claim that the integral in (
10.19
) is
{\mathcal{O}}(\rho^{1/2})
(and hence in particular

o(\rho)
as wanted). On the support of the integrand,

\lvert x^{c}\rvert\leq C_{1}(\lvert x^{b}\rvert+\lvert x^{b^{\prime}}\rvert)\leq C_{2}
and

\displaystyle\lvert x_{b}\rvert^{(1-n_{b})/2}\lvert x_{b^{\prime}}\rvert^{(1-n_{b^{\prime}})/2}\leq C_{3}\lvert x\rvert^{1-(n_{b}+n_{b^{\prime}})/2}\leq C_{4}\lvert x\rvert^{(1-n_{c})}\lvert x\rvert^{-1/2}.

If
n_{c}=0
it follows that
\lvert x\rvert\leq C_{2}
, and
consequently the integral is
{\mathcal{O}}(\rho^{0})={\mathcal{O}}(\rho^{1/2})
. If
n_{c}\geq 1
we estimate
\lvert x\rvert^{(1-n_{c})}\lvert x\rvert^{-1/2}\leq\lvert x_{c}\rvert^{(1-n_{c})}\lvert x_{c}\rvert^{-1/2}
and use
spherical coordinates in
{\mathbf{X}}_{c}
and the fact that
\lvert x_{c}\rvert\leq\lvert x\rvert<\rho
, indeed yielding the desired bound
{\mathcal{O}}(\rho^{1/2})
.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\big\lVert u-J_{\alpha}w^{-}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\big\rVert_{\rm quo}^{2}=\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda_{0}}\big(4\pi\lambda_{\beta}^{1/2}\big)^{-1}\,\lVert g_{\beta}\rVert^{2}.

(10.20)

II.
For
1
we decompose
u=\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}g
as

\displaystyle u=\chi^{2}_{-}(B/\epsilon)u+\chi^{2}_{+}(B/\epsilon)u.

The first term corresponds to the first
term to the right in (
10.18
) thanks to Lemma

10.10
. For the second term we substitute, cf. (
1.4
) and (
10.14
),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}g=\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda}\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}g_{\beta};\quad g_{\beta}=S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g.

The series converges in
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
. By continuity we can
take the factor

\chi^{2}_{+}(B/\epsilon)
inside the summation. Then we obtain

1
by applying Lemma

10.10
to the terms of the (convergent) series
\sum_{\lambda^{\beta}<\lambda}\chi^{2}_{+}(B/\epsilon)\Gamma^{+}_{\beta}(\lambda)^{*}g_{\beta}
.

III.
For
2
we note that

u^{\prime}:=u-\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}g
is in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
and
obeys

\displaystyle\chi_{-}(B/\epsilon)u^{\prime}=0\text{ in }{\mathcal{B}}^{*}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}.

Here we first use (
10.18
) to
\Gamma^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}g
to obtain a similar representation of

u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
, then we multiply by
\chi_{-}(B/\epsilon)
using
the fact
that
this operator is continuous in
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}/{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
and finally we use Appendix

A
to the terms of the resulting series. (Note that
(
A.1
) for any
g\in L^{2}(C_{a})
follows by approximation
since we know the assertion for
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime})
.) This means that
\chi_{-}(B/\epsilon)u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
and then in turn, thanks to
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
, that
u^{\prime}=0
. Finally
(
10.20
) applied to the mentioned representation of
u^{\prime}

implies that also
g_{\beta}-S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)g=0
, as wanted.
∎

Appendix A
Completion of the proof of Lemma

2.6

We complete the proof of Lemma
2.6

1
. For convenience we only show that
\check{P}^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]=0
. By
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
it suffices to show that for some (small)

\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\chi_{-}(B/\epsilon)u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*},

(A.1)

cf. (
10.16
). Our proof of (
A.1
)
does not rely on (
2.11
).
We recall that

\displaystyle v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g](x_{a})=\chi_{+}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert)\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{(1-n_{a})/2}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}K_{a}(x_{a},\lambda_{\alpha})}g(\hat{x}_{a});\quad g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(C_{a}^{\prime}).

Depending on the support of
g
we can pick
\xi^{+}_{a}
as in
(
5.5
) such that

\displaystyle\big(1-\xi^{+}_{a}\big)u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}[g]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}.

Let
{\breve{H}}_{a}
be given by (
3.1
) and
E<\min\sigma({\breve{H}}_{a})
. By commutation, cf. Lemma
7.5
, it suffices
to find
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\rho^{-1}\lVert\varphi_{\rho}\rVert^{2}\to 0\text{ for }\rho\to\infty;\\
&\varphi_{\rho}:=\chi_{-}(B/\epsilon)T_{\rho}u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha},\quad T_{\rho}=\chi_{-}(r/\rho)\xi^{+}_{a}({\breve{H}}_{a}-E)^{-1}.\end{split}

(A.2)

Note at this point that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&({\breve{H}}_{a}-E)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big)-(\lambda-E)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*},\\
&{u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}}-(\lambda-E)({\breve{H}}_{a}-E)^{-1}\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*},\end{split}

(A.3)

cf. (
2.10
). As we will below the introduced factor

({\breve{H}}_{a}-E)^{-1}
in
T_{\rho}
facilitates commutation in the following estimation:

We estimate (with
\langle T\rangle_{\varphi}:=\langle\varphi,T\varphi\rangle
),
commute repeatedly (using Lemma
7.9
), apply
(
6.1
) and (in the last step) (
6.2a
),

\displaystyle-2\rho^{-1}\lVert\varphi_{\rho}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\rho^{-1}\langle B/\epsilon\rangle_{\varphi_{\rho}}

\displaystyle=-(\rho\epsilon)^{-1}\langle T^{*}_{\rho}BT_{\rho}\rangle_{\chi_{-}(B)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big)}+o(\rho^{0})

\displaystyle=-(\rho\epsilon)^{-1}\operatorname{Re}\langle T^{*}_{\rho}r^{-1}\big(2{x_{a}}\cdot p_{a}+{\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}(r^{a})^{2}}\cdot p^{a}\big)T_{\rho}\rangle_{\chi_{-}(B)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big)}+o(\rho^{0})

\displaystyle=-(\rho\epsilon)^{-1}\langle T^{*}_{\rho}r^{-1}\big(2\sqrt{\lambda_{\alpha}}\lvert x_{a}\rvert\big)T_{\rho}\rangle_{\chi_{-}(B)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes v^{+}_{\lambda,\alpha}\big)}+o(\rho^{0})

\displaystyle=-(\rho\epsilon)^{-1}{2\sqrt{\lambda_{\alpha}}\big\langle\langle x\rangle/r\big\rangle}_{\varphi_{\rho}}+o(\rho^{0})

\displaystyle=-(\rho\epsilon)^{-1}{2\sqrt{\lambda}_{\alpha}}\lVert\varphi_{\rho}\rVert^{2}+o(\rho^{0}).

Note for the fourth step that we need to commute
r^{-1}{x_{a}}\cdot p_{a}
through a factor
\chi_{-}(B)
(before we can replace
r^{-1}{x_{a}}\cdot p_{a}\approx\sqrt{\lambda}_{\alpha}r^{-1}\lvert x_{a}\rvert
and commute back). After a
little commutation this may in turn be accomplished by showing

\displaystyle\big[{\breve{H}}_{a}-E)^{-1}r^{-1}{x_{a}}\cdot p_{a}({\breve{H}}_{a}-E)^{-1},\chi_{-}(B)\big]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})

in the sense of (
7.2
). However with the
two factors of resolvents in place the result follows from the Helffer–Sjöstrand
formula and Lemma
7.9
, cf. Remark

7.19
.

Clearly the above estimation yields (
A.2
) for any positive

\epsilon<{\sqrt{\lambda}_{\alpha}}
(by a subtraction). ∎

Appendix B
Proof of (
4.1b
)
(
4.6a
) and (
4.6b
)

For convenience we consider only the assertions for
t\to+\infty
. Our procedure relies on standard stationary phase analysis
on which we omit details, see for
example
[
Hö1
,
II
]
for elaborated accounts.

(
4.1b
):
We need to show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\forall\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a}):\quad\lim_{t\to+\infty}\lVert\big(I-A_{1}A_{2}^{a}A_{3}^{a}A_{2}^{a}A_{1}\big)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes\varphi(t)\big)\rVert=0;\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\varphi(t)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}S_{a}(p_{a},t)}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\varphi.\end{split}

(B.1)

We can assume that
\varphi
is smooth in momentum
space. Then also
f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\varphi
is smooth in momentum space,
and
there it is
compactly supported
away from origo. Recalling that
S_{a}(p_{a},t)=t\xi_{a}^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{1-\mu})
the stationary phase
method then yields the effective localization
\lvert x_{a}\rvert\geq\epsilon^{\prime}t
for some (computable)
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
. On the other hand
x^{a}
is a localized
variable due to the presence of the factor
u^{\alpha}
. This means more
precisely that we may freely insert to the left of the tensor product
in (
B.1
) the factor
\chi_{-}\big({r^{-\delta^{\prime}}}{\lvert x^{a}\rvert}\big)
for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
. We do this for a
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
.

Noting that we can also freely insert the factor
\chi_{+}\big({\operatorname{Re}{\big((x_{a}/r)\cdot p_{a}\big)}}/(4\epsilon_{0})\big)
to the left of the tensor product in
(
B.1
) (thanks to stationary phase analysis), it follows essentially
from (
9.11a
) that
A_{1}^{2}=A_{1+}^{2}

can be replaced by
I
. Similarly the factors
A_{2}^{a}
and

A_{3}^{a}=A_{3+}^{a}
can one by one be replaced by

I
. This is
due to the fact that
in
\{\lvert x^{a}\rvert<cr^{\delta}\}
,
with
c>0
given in the property
2
of Section
6
(this is for
r
replaced by
r^{a}
),

r^{a}_{\delta}=r^{\delta}r^{a}(0)
and
B_{\delta,\rho_{1}}^{a}=r^{\rho_{1}/2}B_{\delta}^{a}r^{\rho_{1}/2}=0
. Clearly we
can then remove
A_{2}^{a}
, and the
justification of removing
A_{3}^{a}
is provided by (
9.10
). This completes the proof of (
4.1b
).

(
4.6a
) and (
4.6b
):
Due to
(
B.1
) it suffices to show (recalling the notation
(
5.2a
)) that for all
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{a})

with
\hat{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{a}):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{t\to+\infty}\lVert{M_{a}\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes\varphi(t)\big)-u^{\alpha}\otimes\big({m_{\alpha}^{+}}\varphi(t)\big)}\rVert=0,\\
&\lim_{t\to+\infty}\lVert\big(f_{1}(H)-f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})\big)\big(u^{\alpha}\otimes\big(\big(m_{\alpha}^{+}\big)^{2}\varphi(t)\big)\big)\rVert=0;\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\varphi(t)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}S_{a}(p_{a},t)}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\varphi.\end{split}

(B.2)

The first assertion of (
B.2
) follows by combining
the properties
1

and
2
from Section
5
with arguments from our
proof of (
B.1
) (including in particular stationary
phase analysis). The second part follows easily from stationary
phase analysis. ∎

Appendix C
Proof of (
9.2
) and formulas for channel wave matrices

The
derivation of the formula (
9.2
) for
\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
, interpretated correctly in
Subsection
9.2
, will be given using
smoothness bounds from Section
8
, arguments from Subsections
9.1
and
9.2
and finally bounds from
[
AIIS
]
. We follow essentially the scheme of
[
DS
, Appendix
A]
(for a similar issue, see for example the proof of
[
Ya2
, Proposition 7.2]
).

Formally (
9.1
) leads with
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
to the formula

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)&=-2\pi f^{2}_{2}(\lambda){\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}\big)^{*}\big(T^{-}_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}(T^{+}_{\beta})^{*}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)T^{-}_{\alpha}\big)J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}\\
&=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}f^{2}_{2}(\lambda){\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}\big(T^{+}_{\beta}\big)^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}.\end{split}

(C.1)

Note that we have not given an independent justification
for
the above middle term (with the factor
R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)
), including its well-definedness. However
this will not be
needed, and the stated expression to the far right does indeed have a clean
interpretation, cf. Remark
8.2

3
and Subsections
9.1
and
9.2
.

Let for
\epsilon>0
and
\lambda\in\Lambda

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda)=(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{-1}\big((k_{\beta}-\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{-1}-(k_{\beta}-\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{-1}\big).

The outset for our analysis is the following
two formulas which may be derived exactly as in
[
DS
, Appendix
A]
. In the first formula
g
is any complex continuous function
on
{\mathbb{R}}
vanishing at infinity.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{W}^{+}_{\beta}(g1_{\Lambda})(k_{\beta})\varphi\\
&\quad=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}+\mathrm{i}R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)T^{+}_{\beta}\big)J_{\beta}\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda)\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,\end{split}

(C.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\varphi_{b},\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}\varphi_{a}\rangle\\
&\quad=-2\pi\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\langle\varphi_{b},\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda^{\prime})\big(\widetilde{W}^{+}_{\beta}\big)^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}^{-}_{a}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\delta_{\epsilon,\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime})\varphi_{a}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime}.\end{split}

(C.3)

Note that (
C.3
) is based on the fact that

\displaystyle\slim_{t\to+\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\tilde{J}_{\alpha}^{-}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tk_{\alpha}}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})=0,

which in turn is a consequence of (
9.11b
) and stationary phase analysis, cf. Appendix

B
.

Although (
C.2
) is valid for any
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{X}_{b})
we take below

\varphi=\varphi_{b}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{b})
for
s>1/2
, and compute by taking

\epsilon\to 0
using
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
, here done formally only,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{W}^{+}_{\beta}g(k_{\beta})\varphi&=2\pi\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{0,\beta}(\lambda)\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;\\
\delta_{0,\beta}(\lambda)&=(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{-1}\big((k_{\beta}-\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)^{-1}-(k_{\beta}-\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)^{-1}\big)\\
&=\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta}).\end{split}

See Section
3
for
notation, and note that this formula has the following precise
meaning. We substitute, referring again to Section
3
,

\displaystyle\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{0,\beta}(\lambda)=f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta}).

Then by combining the proof of
Lemma
8.3
,
Remark
8.2

3
and Lemma

9.1
we conclude that the integral to
the right is well-defined,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{W}^{+}_{\beta}g(k_{\beta})\varphi\\
&\quad=2\pi\int\,(gf_{2})(\lambda)\big(f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\big)\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,\end{split}

(C.4a)

where the correct interpretation of the product in
parentheses involves the ‘
Q
-operators’ as in
Subsection
9.2
(more precisely obtained by expanding

T^{+}_{\beta}
into terms on the
form (
C.7
) given below). We call (
C.4a
) a ‘channel
wave matrix’ representation (examined closer in Section
10
). Although the below proof of
(
C.4a
) reveals a different representation, more
closely related to (
C.2
), the formula
(
C.4a
) is more useful for most of our purposes
(with exceptions only in Subsection
10.3
). The
alternative formula
reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{W}^{+}_{\beta}g(k_{\beta})\varphi\\
&\quad=\int\,(gf_{2})(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}+\mathrm{i}R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0)T^{+}_{\beta}\big)J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.\end{split}

(C.4b)

Now there are two assertions
that need to be checked to justify (
C.4a
) by
taking the ‘
\epsilon\to 0
’–limit in
(
C.2
), in the precise meaning of taking limit
in the weak topology of
L^{2}_{-1}(\mathbf{X})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\big)T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda)\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)-R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\big)T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

(C.5a)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}+\mathrm{i}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)T^{+}_{\beta}\big)J_{\beta}\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda)\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=0.

(C.5b)

For (
C.5a
) we write with
\delta_{\epsilon,\lambda}(\lambda^{\prime}):=\pi^{-1}\epsilon/\big((\lambda-\lambda^{\prime})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\big)

for any fixed
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda)\varphi&=\breve{w}^{+}_{b}\int^{\infty}_{\lambda^{\beta}}f_{2}(\lambda^{\prime})\delta_{\epsilon,\lambda}(\lambda^{\prime})\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime}\\
&=\int^{\infty}_{\lambda^{\beta}}f_{2}(\lambda^{\prime})\delta_{\epsilon,\lambda}(\lambda^{\prime})\gamma^{+}_{b}(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime},\end{split}

(C.6)

substitute and replace by the limit (when taking
\epsilon\to 0
). This is doable thanks to Remark
8.2

3
, Lemma
9.1
and the continuity of
\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda^{\prime}_{\beta})\varphi
justifying the first equality, and the second is a
consequence of Remark
8.2

3
and Lemma
9.1
too. Recall from Subsection
9.2
that
T^{+}_{\beta}
is a finite sum of terms
expressed as

\displaystyle f_{1}(H)Q^{+}(b,l)^{*}B_{+}Q^{+}(b,l)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{b})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\beta}),\quad B_{+}\text{ bounded},

(C.7)

to be used below too.

For (
C.5b
) we will invoke
[
AIIS
, Theorem 1.8 and Corollary
1.9]
. Indeed thanks to these results and the presence of the factors

A_{1+}
in
\Phi^{+}_{\beta}
we can compute the left-hand side to be
equal

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}+\mathrm{i}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)T^{+}_{\beta}\big)J_{\beta}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

(C.8)

To see this, considering only the ‘difficult term’

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\breve{w}^{+}_{b}f_{2}(k_{\beta})\delta_{\epsilon,\beta}(\lambda)\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

we insert
I=\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})+\chi^{2}_{-}(2B/\epsilon_{0})
to the right of the factor
R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)
. Using the factors of
A_{1+}
and a little commutation
the contribution from the second term
\chi^{2}_{-}(2B/\epsilon_{0})

allows the insertion of the weight
\langle x\rangle^{-s}
for some
s>1/2
to the
right of
R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)
and we can use (
6.8a
) and
(
C.6
) to compute the ‘
\epsilon\to 0
’–limit for this
term (more precisely we use that
\langle x\rangle^{s}\chi^{2}_{-}(2B/\epsilon_{0})f_{1}(H)Q^{+}(b,l)^{*}B_{+}
is bounded). As for the contribution from the first term
\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})
we write

\displaystyle R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})=R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})\langle x\rangle^{-s}\langle x\rangle^{s};\quad s>1/2-\mu/2.

Then by
[
AIIS
, Theorem 1.8 and Corollary 1.9]

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{-1}f_{1}(H)R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})\langle x\rangle^{-s}\text{ is uniformly bounded},

and there exists

\displaystyle\Lambda\ni\lambda\to\langle x\rangle^{-1}f_{1}(H)

\displaystyle R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})\langle x\rangle^{-s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\langle x\rangle^{-1}f_{1}(H)R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\chi^{2}_{+}(2B/\epsilon_{0})\langle x\rangle^{-s},

constituting a continuous
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
-valued
function. Consequently, using again (
C.6
), it suffices
to check the existence and continuity of the
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}(C_{b}),{\mathcal{H}})
-valued
function

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}\ni\lambda\to f_{2}(\lambda)\langle x\rangle^{s}T^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}.

Expanding
T^{+}_{\beta}
as above,
we can use Lemma
9.1
to combine for
each term the factor
Q^{+}(b,l)
with the factor
J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}
(as done above). Each remaining factor of a ‘
Q
-operator’
(more precisely
Q^{+}(b,l)^{*}
appearing to the left) contributes by a factor

{\mathcal{O}}(r^{\rho_{1}/2-\delta/2})
, cf. Remark
8.2

1
. Whence we are left with checking that

s+\rho_{1}/2-\delta/2\leq 0
is possible. Due to (
4.5
)
it suffices to produce an
s>1/2-\mu/2
such that
s+1/2-2/(2+\mu)\leq 0
,
and therefore in turn to check that
1/2-\mu/2+1/2-2/(2+\mu)<0
.
But the latter condition is fulfilled for all
\mu>0
,
so the check is done. Consequently indeed the left-hand side of (
C.5b
) is given by the
integral (
C.8
).

Next, using the above arguments and
[
AIIS
, Corollary
1.10]
, the expression
(
C.8
) may be computed with
\chi_{\rho}:=\chi_{-}(r/\rho)
as

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}-R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)\Phi^{+}_{\beta}\big)J_{\beta}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)\big(\Phi_{\beta}^{+}-R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\chi_{\rho}(H-\lambda)\Phi^{+}_{\beta}\big)J_{\beta}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\int_{\Lambda}\,g(\lambda)f_{1}(H)R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)[H,\chi_{\rho}]\Phi^{+}_{\beta}J_{\beta}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{b}^{+}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=0,

proving (
C.5b
).

Now to prove (
9.2
), we would like to substitute the adjoint expression of (
C.4a
) with
g(\lambda)=\delta_{\epsilon,\lambda^{\prime}}(\lambda)
into
(
C.3
) and then interchange the order of the two
integrations. This requires of course some modification since the
meaning of the right-hand side of (
C.4a
) is a vector in
L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbf{X})
for any
s>1/2
(but unlikely any smaller), and there is no
reason to expect that the quantity
T^{-}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}^{-}_{a}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\delta_{\epsilon,\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime})\varphi_{a}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X})
for any such
s
. However, writing
T^{-}_{\alpha}
as a finite sum terms on the
form

\displaystyle f_{1}(H)Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}B_{-}Q^{-}(a,k)f_{1}({\breve{H}}_{a})f_{1}({\breve{h}}_{\alpha}),\quad B_{-}\text{ bounded},

(C.9)

we can introduce
the modification, say denoted by

T^{-}_{\alpha,\rho}
, given by inserting for each such term the above factor

\chi_{\rho}
to the right of
Q^{-}(a,k)^{*}
. Using Fubini’s theorem and
the computation

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\,\delta_{\epsilon,\lambda^{\prime}}(\lambda)\delta_{\epsilon,\alpha}(\lambda^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda^{\prime}=\delta_{2\epsilon,\alpha}(\lambda),

we then obtain

\displaystyle(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-2}\langle\varphi_{b},\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}\varphi_{a}\rangle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\lim_{\rho\to\infty}

\displaystyle\quad\int_{\Lambda}\,f_{2}(\lambda)\langle\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi_{b},\gamma^{+}_{b}(\lambda_{\beta})J^{*}_{\beta}(T^{+}_{\beta})^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,\rho}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}^{-}_{a}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\delta_{2\epsilon,\alpha}(\lambda)\varphi_{a}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

valid for any
\varphi_{a}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{a})
and

\varphi_{b}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbf{X}_{b})
for

s>1/2
.

With an analogue of (
C.6
) for
\alpha
rather
than
\beta
we compute

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Lambda}\,f_{2}(\lambda)\langle\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi_{b},\gamma^{+}_{b}(\lambda_{\beta})J^{*}_{\beta}(T^{+}_{\beta})^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,\rho}J_{\alpha}\breve{w}^{-}_{a}f_{2}(k_{\alpha})\delta_{2\epsilon,\alpha}(\lambda)\varphi_{a}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda}\,f_{2}(\lambda)\langle\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi_{b},\gamma^{+}_{b}(\lambda_{\beta})J^{*}_{\beta}(T^{+}_{\beta})^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,\rho}J_{\alpha}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})^{*}\gamma_{a,0}(\lambda_{\alpha})\varphi_{a}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

uniformly in
\rho>1
. Due to these
features we can interchange limits above and conclude that

\displaystyle(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-2}\langle\varphi_{b},\widetilde{S}_{\beta\alpha}\varphi_{a}\rangle=\lim_{\rho\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda}\,f_{2}(\lambda)\langle\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi_{b},\gamma^{+}_{b}(\lambda_{\beta})J^{*}_{\beta}(T^{+}_{\beta})^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha,\rho}J_{\alpha}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})^{*}\gamma_{a,0}(\lambda_{\alpha})\varphi_{a}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda}\,f_{2}(\lambda)\langle\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi_{b},\gamma^{+}_{b}(\lambda_{\beta})J^{*}_{\beta}(T^{+}_{\beta})^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}f_{2}(\lambda)\gamma_{a}^{-}(\lambda_{\alpha})^{*}\gamma_{a,0}(\lambda_{\alpha})\varphi_{a}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

showing (
9.2
). Note that indeed the right
interpretation is given by expansion into a (finite) sum, substituting the expressions
(
C.7
) and (
C.9
), cf. Subsection
9.2
. ∎

Remark C.1
.

Note the following analogues of
(
C.4a
) and (
9.2
), cf. (
4.7a
) and (
4.7b
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{-}_{\beta}g(k_{\beta})\varphi

\displaystyle=-2\pi\int\,(gf_{2})(\lambda)\big(f_{1}(H)\delta(H-\lambda)T^{-}_{\beta}J_{\beta}\gamma_{b}^{-}(\lambda_{\beta})^{*}\big)\gamma_{b,0}(\lambda_{\beta})\varphi\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

(C.10a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}16\lambda_{\beta}\lambda_{\alpha}f^{2}_{2}(\lambda)&m_{b}(\pm\hat{\xi}_{b})^{2}m_{b}(\pm\hat{\xi}_{a})^{2}\,\delta_{\beta\alpha}\\
&\quad=-(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}f^{2}_{2}(\lambda){\gamma_{b}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\beta})}J^{*}_{\beta}\big(T^{\pm}_{\beta}\big)^{*}\delta(H-\lambda)T^{\pm}_{\alpha}J_{\alpha}{\gamma_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda_{\alpha})}^{*}.\end{split}

(C.10b)

The quantity to the left in
(
C.10b
) is meant to be an operator in

{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}(C_{a}),L^{2}(C_{b})\big)
; the use of the
Kronecker symbol
\delta_{\beta\alpha}
specifies that it vanishes unless

\beta=\alpha
. The operator may be considered as the fiber of
F_{\beta}\big(\widetilde{W}_{\beta}^{\pm}\big)^{*}\widetilde{W}^{\pm}_{\alpha}F_{\alpha}^{-1}
at energy
\lambda
, invoking
the orthogonality of channels. The formula (
C.10b
)
results
by mimicking
the above procedure for showing (
9.2
).
